Homiliae in Ecclesiasten
by Hugh of Saint Victor

## OF WHICH THESE ARE THE ARGUMENTS:

- *In hom. XIX on Ecclesiastes, on the varied exposition of Holy Scripture, and on Solomon's intention. Preface.*
- *On the title of the work, on Solomon's names, and books, and on the beginnings of this book, on the explanation of the vanities. Homily I.*
- *On the proof of the vanity of all things under heaven: through the corruption of the elements, through the generation of things, and through the forgetting of those things which have been. Hom. II.*
- *How Ecclesiastes proves by his own works that all the works of men are vain, with an epilogue upon the foregoing. Hom. III.*
- *On the threefold vanity of things, and on the worst occupation of men. Hom. IV.*
- *On the letter of the words of Ecclesiastes, by the literal and the moral exposition. Homil. V.*
- *What it is to be distended in the worst occupation. Hom. VI.*
- *That the perverse are corrected with difficulty,* etc. *Hom. VII.*
- *That man flees away from the truth: as Adam fled in paradise, and hid himself. Hom. VIII.*
- *On the various vain endeavors of Ecclesiastes. Hom. IX.*
- *On the remaining matters as far as that place: « The fool walketh in darkness. » Hom. X.*
- *How wisdom reacheth from end to end mightily,* etc. *Hom. XI.*
- *On that passage according to another translation, « The eyes of fools are in the ends of the earth, » where our copy has: « The fool walketh in darkness, » and on the rest of the second chapter. Hom. XII.*
- *How all things have their own time. Hom. XIII.*
- *A setting forth of the remaining things which have their own time, and a repetition of the things said. Hom. XIV.*
- *On time and on the things subject to time, according to another interpretation. Hom. XV.*
- *On the spiritual understanding of those things which have been said concerning time. Hom. XVI.*
- *On the confusion of minds arising from the passage of the times. Hom. XVII.*
- *On the perverse manners of men, and what Ecclesiastes judged from them. Hom. XVIII.*
- *On the oppression and abandonment of the innocent, and on the vain and foolish judgment of the impious concerning this life. Hom. XIX.*

## PREFACE. *On the varied exposition of Holy Scripture, and on Solomon's intention.*

[0113C]

What I lately discoursed before you concerning the book of Solomon which is called Ecclesiastes, [0114C] I have now marked down with the pen, briefly running over it (because certain things there seemed worthy of remembrance). All Scripture expounded according to its own proper interpretation both shines out more clearly, [0115A] and opens to its readers an easier approach to the understanding of itself. Many, not understanding the power of the Scriptures, becloud their comeliness and beauty with foreign expositions; and when they ought to have unlocked hidden things, they darken even the manifest ones. But to me they seem to lie under a like fault, both those who obstinately deny that the mystical understanding and the profundity of the allegories is to be sought in Holy Scripture where it is, and those who superstitiously contend that it is to be applied where it is not. Wherefore in this work I judge that I must not labor much over tropologies, or over mystical senses of allegories to be searched out through the whole sequence of his narrative at least: especially since the author himself here aims not so much at movements to be instructed, or at mysteries to be recounted, as at stirring the human heart [0115B] to contempt of worldly things by the manifest truth of his reasonings and by evident exhortation. Nor yet do I deny this, that many uncertain mystical things belong to this narrative, which require their own proper explanation . especially in what follows, since always, in the advance of the narrative, he touches more and more upon spiritual things according to the increase of contemplation, and is lifted up from things visible. But it is one thing to attend to that by which the whole intention of the writer and the whole sequence of the narrative is led; and another to hold that certain things said mystically by accident, and to be understood spiritually, are not to be passed over negligently. Now therefore we undertake to explain the surface of the narrative, which is strong with so great a loveliness of speech and of sentiments, so that the things which you now read written (this poor little lucubration, whatever it be, [0115C] affording a way to the understanding) you may henceforth rejoice not only to have written for you, but understood by you.



## HOMILY THE FIRST. *On the title of the work, on Solomon's names, and books, and on the beginnings of this work, on the explanation of the vanities.*

*[n: (ECCLE. I.)] The words of Ecclesiastes, the son of David, king of Jerusalem.* This is the title of the book: in which both the quality of the work that follows is briefly expressed, and equally the person of the author is commended. For in that it is said, *words*, a manifold disputation is signified, and one drawn out into diverse sentiments. For because in this book the manners, the pursuits, and the works of many are described, [0115D] therefore it is necessary that the speaker take up the voices of many, and express in his own discourse the opinions of many, so that he may be able to present in his own person the persons of many (though he himself who speaks be but one). For near the end of the book he testifies that he has spoken to many, and that many were in himself, saying: Let us all alike hear the end of speaking. *Fear God, and keep his commandments: this is every man.* This also is why he willed to be named Ecclesiastes in this work; because, namely, his discourse here is directed not to some one man in particular, but to the whole Church, that is, to the assembly, or the multitude of the people, and his oration in this book serves at once for the expressing and the forming of the manners of many. By three [0116A] words indeed we read that Solomon was called: Idida, which is interpreted *beloved*; and Coeleth, which in Greek is called *Ecclesiastes*, in Latin *concionator*, an assembly-speaker; and Solomon, which sounds *peaceable*. Further, he may be called Ecclesiastes, or assembly-speaker, who instructs the Church or the assembly, that is, the multitude of the people, as he himself is plainly shown to have done in this book. And that Solomon was beloved by the Lord, and enjoyed great peace in his kingdom until the last times of his life, when he transgressed the covenant and the law of the Lord, the history plainly discloses the kingdom. And so, according to the three names, he composed three volumes. The first, whose title is Parables, or the Proverbs of Solomon. The second, which we now have in hand, which is called Ecclesiastes. The third, which is called [0116B] the Song of songs. In the first, as out of a father's affection, he addresses his beloved son, and by frequent admonition exhorts him to turn aside from vices and to pursue the virtues. In the second, he admonishes a man grown and of ripe age not to think anything among the things of the world to be perpetual. But at the last, when he is now perfected and, having trodden the world underfoot, is unencumbered, in the Song of songs he joins him to the embraces of the Bridegroom. Not far indeed from this kind of treatment even the philosophers of the Gentiles were accustomed to form their hearers, setting before those to be instructed first ethics, then physics, and last of all theology.

These things having been set down first for the opening up of the understanding of what is to be said, let us now come to the very sequence of the book. Solomon's matter in this work is all things subject [0116C] to vanity, that is, things falling and transitory. His intention is to persuade contempt of the world. His manner of treatment is such. He shows, according to a threefold vanity, that all things are subject to vanity, that is, are falling and transitory — namely both the things which were made for the sake of men, and the things which were made by men, and the things which were made in men. In those things which were made for the sake of men, the vanity is of mutability. In those which were made by men, the vanity is of curiosity. In those which were made in men, the vanity is of mortality, and all is vanity. And of these indeed we shall discourse more amply hereafter, if we first treat of the things that must be said concerning the very kind of contemplation. In which the mind of the beholder, lifted up, was able either to see or to recount such new and such wondrous things concerning [0116D] the condition of man's estate. For let us see what he says:

*Vanity of vanities, said Ecclesiastes,* etc. But where do you suppose the mind of this man was when he said these things? He was a man, but he was above man. For unless he had gone beyond man, he would not have seen that every man is a liar. Therefore concerning the consideration of this man a consideration must first be had by us, and the kinds of spiritual beholding must be distinguished. There are three visions of the rational soul: cogitation, meditation, contemplation. Cogitation is when the mind is touched in passing by the notion of things, when the thing itself is suddenly presented to the soul by its own image, either entering through sense or rising up from memory. Meditation is the assiduous and shrewd turning over of cogitation, striving either to unfold [0117A] something involved, or, searching, to penetrate something hidden. Contemplation is the clear-sighted and free gaze of the soul, poured out on every side upon the things to be beheld. Between meditation and contemplation this difference appears to lie. That meditation is always about things hidden from our understanding. But contemplation is about things manifest, either according to their own nature or according to our capacity. And that meditation is always occupied about the probing of some one thing; contemplation is poured out to the grasping of many things, or even of all. Meditation therefore is a certain curious force of the mind, and a shrewd one, striving to investigate obscure things and to unravel perplexed ones. Contemplation is that liveliness of understanding which, having all things in the open, grasps them by a manifest vision. And thus [0117B] in a certain manner what meditation seeks, contemplation possesses. But of contemplation there are two kinds: one which is both prior and belongs to beginners, in the consideration of creatures; the other which is later, and belongs to the perfect, in the contemplation of the Creator. In the Proverbs, Solomon went forward as though by meditating. In Ecclesiastes he ascended to the first step of contemplation. In the Song of songs he carried himself over to the highest. In meditation there is as it were a certain wrestling of ignorance with knowledge, and the light of truth in a certain manner flashes out in the midst of the darkness of error, just as fire in green wood at first indeed takes hold with difficulty; but when it has been roused by a more vehement blast, and has begun to blaze more sharply into the material set under it, then we see certain great globes of smoky gloom [0117C] rise up, and wrap round that flame, still of a slight sparkling, shining through but rarely, until at length, the burning gradually increasing, when all the vapor is drawn off and the gloom scattered, a serene splendor appears. Then the victorious flame, running through the whole heap of the crackling pyre, freely lords it, and flying about the material set under it, and grazing it with a soft touch, by licking burns it up and penetrates it; nor does it rest before, by penetrating, it has stolen into the inmost parts and drawn wholly into itself, in a certain manner, whatever it found outside itself. But after that which is to be burned up has, by the burning, been consumed and has passed over wholly, as it were out of its own nature, into the likeness and the property of fire, then all the crash falls away, and the noise [0117D] is lulled, and those darts of flame are lifted and taken out of the midst, and that savage and devouring fire, all things being subjected to it and incorporated with it by a certain friendly likeness, composes itself in deep peace and silence; because now it finds nothing either diverse beside itself, or adverse against itself. First therefore fire was seen with flame and smoke, then fire with flame or smoke, last of all pure fire without flame and smoke. Just so, without doubt, the carnal heart, like green wood not yet dried out from the moisture of carnal concupiscence, if at any time it has conceived some spark of divine fear or love, first indeed, while depraved desires struggle against it, a smoke of passions and disturbances arises; then, when the mind has been strengthened, and it has begun with the flame of love to burn more strongly and to shine more brightly, [0118A] presently all the gloom of the disturbances vanishes: and now with a pure mind the soul pours itself out to the contemplation of truth. But last of all, after the heart has been penetrated by assiduous contemplation of the truth, and has entered to the very fountain of the highest truth, to the marrow, with the whole affection of the soul, then, being as it were wholly set on fire into that very sweetness and turned into the fire of love, it rests most at peace from all noise and disturbance. First therefore, because among the perils of temptations counsel is sought, it is as though in meditation there were smoke with flame. Secondly, because with a pure mind the heart is poured out to the contemplation of truth, it is as though at the beginning of contemplation there were flame without smoke. Thirdly, because now, truth having been found and charity perfected, nothing further is sought beyond that [0118B] which alone is; in the fire of love alone, in the highest tranquillity and felicity, he is sweetly beaten back. Then, when the whole heart is turned into the fire of love, God is truly felt to be all in all, since he is received with so inward a love that, besides him, nothing even of itself is left to the heart. That we may therefore distinguish these three by their proper words: the first is meditation; the second, speculation; the third, contemplation. In meditation, the disturbance of carnal passions, rising up importunately, beclouds the mind kindled with pious devotion. In speculation, the newness of an unwonted vision lifts it up into wonder. In contemplation, the taste of a wondrous sweetness changes the whole into joy and delight. Therefore in meditation there is solicitude; in speculation, wonder; in contemplation, sweetness. [0118C] Yet even spiritual speculation itself is wont to refresh the soul with great delight, when after the wrestling of temptations and the gloom of error it suddenly settles it in unhoped-for peace, and floods it with an unwonted brightness. Into this, therefore, this man, caught up in mind above all things falling and transitory, saw that there is nothing of all things that abides, and, terrified as it were by the stupor of this new and unwonted vision, cried out: Vanity of vanities, and all is vanity. For he was gazing upon the whole universe, and upon it wholly subject to vanity, and called its vanity the vanity of all vanities, that is, the vanity containing in itself every vanity, as it were the genus of all genera (in that it contains in itself all the genera of things). For all is vanity, and out of all things [0118D] is the universe, and the universe is vanity. But now, since we have already said, as much as seemed fitting for the present, concerning that very kind of contemplation by which this human mind was able to look through into these things concerning man, or concerning human affairs, we shall return to our proposed subject, and shall next distinguish the kinds of all the vanities which, illumined by that same contemplation, he detected to be present in things that fall; because in that we strive first to explain lucidly the very matter of the whole work, we also open its text more manifestly to those who read it. For his whole narrative is woven out of the vanity of temporal things.



## HOMILY THE FIRST. *On the title of the work, on Solomon's names, and books, and on the beginnings of this work, on the explanation of the vanities.* (cont.)

There are therefore three kinds of vanities, which this book especially pursues, in which it embraces every vanity: and it testifies that all things which are done under the sun [0119A] lie subject to these. The first is the vanity of mutability, which is present in all falling things by their condition. The second is the vanity of curiosity, or of cupidity, which is present in the minds of men through the disordered love of passing and vain things. The third is the vanity of mortality, which is present in human bodies through penalty. The first vanity therefore is natural, and fit or congruous. The second vanity is culpable, because it is perverse. The third vanity is penal and wretched. And we call the natural vanity, that is, mutability, fit for this reason: because out of the ordered alternation of passing things the greater beauty of the universe stands firm, and its instability is dispensed to the greater advantage; because in it both weariness is taken away from human minds by the variety of things, and the comeliness [0119B] of the universe, as has been said, is increased. For corporeal nature, whose beauty is perfected according to diverse species and forms, is adorned with an ampler comeliness out of its very mutability, while through the intervals of times, and the alteration of things passing away in time and things succeeding in time, it takes what it could not take all at once; because its species come on by succession, which could not be present in it all together. Further, that vanity which rules over depraved minds stands forth so much the more deformed than the rest, inasmuch as mutability was alien to spiritual substance not only through grace but even through nature. Therefore this alone is reproved among all the rest, and before all the rest this alone is shown to be iniquitous and perverse. But the penal vanity, which is in human bodies through mortality, is for this reason [0119C] called wretched, because man now has it from the penalty of sin, that he lies subject to the condition of things temporally passing away — he who before, in his own creation, had received to stand above the condition of all visible things. For the condition of passing things is that all things which have risen should set, and things increased should grow old. But the nature of man, taken indeed out of corruptible matter, yet through grace raised above corruption and confirmed against corruption, had received this as a gift: that it should indeed have a rising with the rest, but should not have a setting; and that among things to be born, but not to die, it should receive increase by the growths of age, and should not suffer defect. This dignity man lost through the sin of transgression, when, stripped of the robe of immortality, and fallen back to the condition of his own origin, [0119D] he heard from the sentence of the Creator: *Earth thou art, and unto earth thou shalt go [n: (Gen. III)]*. To this vanity therefore (which was born to lie subject to the rest) to man alone is it reckoned for a penalty; because man alone had received to stand above it through grace. Since therefore we have distinguished three vanities: the first indeed, that is, the vanity of mutability, is the cause of sin; the second, that is, the vanity of cupidity, is sin; but the third, that is, the vanity of mortality, is the penalty of sin. Therefore the vanity of cupidity is reproved in the vanity of mutability. In the vanity of mortality it is punished. For as the Psalmist says: *Man when he was in honour did not understand; but was compared to senseless beasts, and was made like unto them [n: (Psal. XLVIII)]*. For unless he had first, through disordered [0120A] concupiscence of the flesh, inclined himself to the things that belong to beasts, he would in no wise have been made like to beasts through the mortality of the flesh. But now, because through the desire of his mind he began to lean upon mutable things, he too could not be stable in that which he had been. We can still distinguish these vanities by another division, so that the same thing, the more manifoldly it is set forth, may so much the more evidently be known. One vanity is in created things. Another in human works, another in bodies, another in minds. The first is of mutability; the second of curiosity; the third of mortality; the fourth of iniquity. The vanity of mutability is considered in things in two ways: either because they are empty, or because they are transitory. In the one they are vain, because they show what they do not have; in the other they are vain, because they do not remain [0120B] in what they have. There they are vain, because they have form alone, and do not have essence. Here they are vain, because although they have some essence, yet they have no substance. There they are vain, because without truth they set an appearance before us. Here they are vain, because they hold out a standing and pass away. So are all things falling and transitory, false and deceiving: they who love vain things, and trust in vanity, and for that reason are made not only vain, but vanity itself out of vanity, fail away.

The vanity of cupidity consists in three things: namely in the concupiscence of the eyes, in the concupiscence of the flesh, and in the pride of life. For in all these there is vanity. For all things which pertain to the flesh, whether they seem good or evil, are neither true evils nor true goods; nor can they always be, whether they be good or evil, [0120C] that which they are. Therefore it is as vain to place hope in those things which seem good as to dread those which are thought evil. For what does the concupiscence of the eyes do? See what it does, and you will find how great a vanity lies hidden, cloaked under this veil. It catches at the deceitful paints of things and at slippery sights. Forgetful of itself, it is poured out abroad, and giving itself wholly to curiosity it goes round all things, ranges over the universe, in case anything new, anything unwonted, anything wondrous should occur — what this or that may mean, what appearance each single thing may have, or what signification they hold out. To all the motions of things it is always changeable, always headlong, rash, forward, unstable, wanton, impatient and slippery; often exulting in vain hope, often trembling with empty fear, having no [0120D] root within, but always suspicious, hanging upon the outward motions of things. Before peril it wavers, before danger it trembles; everything which can befall on either side, whether good or evil, fearing and desiring both, it turns aside from vainly, by suspicion alone. It burns after the manner of one impatient, awaiting to see what is to come; at things present it is dissolved, at things that stand firm it is dulled, and at every vicissitude of things, whether they be sad or glad, it is varied by an inconstant fluctuation of the mind. But consider how vain this is. Assuredly every creature is such to a man as he himself is to it, so that by those things which are without, neither can a good mind be harmed, nor an evil one helped. Therefore all these things can be neither for evil to a good man, nor for good to an evil one, [0121A] except so far as the soul itself either profits by spurning them, or fails by loving them perversely, or by fearing them vainly. How great a vanity then it is, to look upon these things with so great solicitude and curiosity, as though they were of some moment either to give or to take away salvation, and either not to attend to, or to dissemble, those inward goods in which true salvation consists? What shall I say of the concupiscence of the flesh? How vain it is, since the delight of the flesh itself, however great it be, can neither help when past, nor remain when present? What does it profit to defend with so great effort against corruption a flesh that is to die, by the excess of delights and the affluence of pleasures, when there is no one who can guard it from corruption? For as to the pride of life, whether it will glory in riches or in dignities, what can be vainer? [0121B] For who would not vainly glory that he is more heavily laden than the rest? This indeed is all that riches, all that dignities confer upon their possessors: that whereas they seem to receive in them more than others, they are thereby found more heavily laden than others; whom certainly, for the acquiring or the keeping of those same things, they always either wear down with labor or afflict with care. But in all these things there is great vanity, and superfluous care. The vanity of mortality, which consists in two things: namely in the penalty which wears down, and in the penalty which dissolves. The penalty which wears down first makes us grow old through the failing of life. The penalty which dissolves afterwards compels us to go into rottenness, and to return, through the corruption of the flesh, into the dust from which we were taken according to the flesh. Hence therefore let man weigh in what vanity every man lives, who unwilling [0121C] suffers the corruption of the flesh, because willing he works the corruption of the mind. Wholly then is he subject to vanity who both flows away in flesh through mortality, and in mind through iniquity. But the one of these is his own from himself, the other his own against himself, and the whole is in himself. These three kinds of vanities the author distinguishes at once in the beginning of his book, which afterwards, pursuing them one by one through the whole sequence of his work, he extends by copious [sic: *disputat one*], everywhere reproving in the three that fourth kind of vanity which we said is present in depraved minds, and showing this alone to be liable to fault and owing a penalty. The vanity which is present in created things through mutability he sets forth first. Vanity of vanities, and all is vanity. Then in the second place [0121D] he adds the vanity which is in human works. What hath a man more of all his labour that he laboureth under the sun? In the third place he subjoins that vanity which is present in human bodies through mortality, saying: *One generation passeth away, and another generation cometh.* And according to these three distinctions he also divides the whole sequence of the following work into three parts, assigning to each its own portion. In the first part, whose beginning is: *The sun riseth, and goeth down,* he disputes concerning the vanity of mutability. In the second part, which begins thus: *I Ecclesiastes was king of Israel in Jerusalem.* He pursues most widely the vanity of human cupidity. In the third part (which is placed as it were for a close in the last position in the disputation, whose beginning is: *Remember thy Creator in the days of thy youth* ) he calls to mind the vanity [0122A] of mortality, so that the human soul may as it were hear at the end that which it is the more needful to perceive with intent consideration, and to recall to memory the more often. These things we have prepared, according to the grasp of our understanding, for the opening of Ecclesiastes, which is the second of Solomon's books, for those entering upon the reading of it. But we confess that to explain worthily its whole breadth is above our powers: in matters of this kind, covered over with deep profundity, seeking rather to hear a teacher than to snatch a teacher's place. For it is better, as Plato says, modestly to learn what is another's than shamelessly to thrust forward one's own. But if we have been able to say anything fitting upon this, let it be beyond hope and expectation. If anything usefully, not beyond our intention. First, as it were [0122B] a certain proem, he sets before his book: in which he distinguishes that threefold matter of the following work which we have called to mind, concerning which he afterwards enters more amply into disputation. The proem therefore is: *Vanity of vanities, vanity of vanities, and all is vanity,* as far as: *The sun riseth, and goeth down.* From there the first part of the book is begun, and the rest as we have said. Now let us consider the very words of the letter, whether we may be able to grasp in them anything of the things which have been said above.



## HOMILY THE FIRST. *On the title of the work, on Solomon's names, and books, and on the beginnings of this work, on the explanation of the vanities.* (cont.)

*Vanity of vanities (said Ecclesiastes), vanity of vanities, and all is vanity.* With weight is that to be pronounced which he speaks of himself as though of another: said Ecclesiastes. For because for authority's sake he set down his own person, he fittingly spoke of himself not as of himself, but as of another, so that, since he had to speak of himself as some great one, as the matter [0122C] demanded, he might more becomingly and more maturely say this of himself in another than in himself. So Balaam, speaking of himself, says: *He hath said, the hearer of the words of God, who hath beheld the visions of the Almighty, who falleth and hath his eyes opened [n: (Num. XXIV)]*. And John in his Gospel. He speaks of himself as though of another, saying: *This is that disciple whom Jesus loved, who giveth testimony of these things, and we know that his testimony is true [n: (Joan. XXI)]*. And Paul speaks of himself as though of another to the Corinthians, saying: *I know a man in Christ, whether in the body, or out of the body, I know not, God knoweth, such a one caught up even to the third heaven, and such a one caught up into paradise, and that he heard secret words, which it is not granted to man to utter [n: (II Cor. XII)]*. But this kind [0122D] of speech, as often as it is taken up in Scripture, is done either for the sake of humility, or of admiration — namely, that when we would say great things, we should rather ascribe them as it were to others, lest it seem either proud or excessive, if we should attribute them to ourselves. For we ought both to remove great things from ourselves for humility's sake, and to show wondrous things as it were from afar, for admiration's sake. For those things are less wondrous which come nearer to knowledge. Therefore, that the minds of all may be raised up to the utterance that is to come, and may awake as at some miracle of newness, it is rightly said: Said Ecclesiastes. As if it were said: What so great and such a one has said could not be vain, even though he said it concerning vanity. The common teacher and the instructor of all, Ecclesiastes himself, has said it. What did he say? Hear what he said, and attend. For they are no small things [0123A] that are said: Vanity of vanities, vanity of vanities, and all is vanity. If all is vanity, then he also is vanity who said this. And how will it stand firm that what vanity said concerning vanity is not vain? But if it is true that what he said is vain, it is not to be heard, but spat out. What then shall we say? Either not all is vanity, as he said, and he said what is false; or all is vanity, as he said, and he himself is vanity, and said a vain thing. But assuredly, as he himself said: All is vanity, and he himself, who said it, is vanity; and not only vanity, but even universal vanity: and yet what he said is not vanity, but truth; because in that which he said he himself was not vanity, since he said against vanity what he said concerning vanity; and it is agreed that he could not be contrary to vanity in that in which he was vanity [0123B]. Something therefore was in him which was not vanity, and that could speak not vainly against vanity. But what was this, or where was it? Assuredly, if we shall say that this body was it, or something in the body, which spoke without vanity concerning vanity: then could the body, which is corrupted, subject to vanity and born for vanity, at some time be without vanity — a body which wholly lives in vanity? We remember the saying of the Psalmist: *Universal vanity, every man living [n: (Psal. XXXVIII)]*. For that which dies is the very thing which lives: which is born that it may live, and lives that it may die. But what is it that dies, save that which is deprived of life? That therefore dies in man which is extinguished from life. And what is this, save the body and the bodily sense? For it is not itself after the living creature, [0123C] from which it is a living creature, and when it ceases to be capable of sensation, the sense ceases to be. Therefore that which dies in man is the very thing which lives in man; which is for that reason subject to vanity, because it is liable to mortality. Therefore the bodily sense could not reprove vanity, in which nothing can be found free and pure from vanity. What then must be said this was? Was it perhaps the soul? Have we not shown above that it too is subject to vanity? For in it we placed the vanity of iniquity: which stands forth so much the more deformed than all other things, in that from it alone the others have either that they are harmful, or that they are at all. For without this vanity, neither would there be the vanity of mortality, nor would the vanity of mutability be harmful [0123D]. How then can the soul, which is subject to so great a vanity, truly reprove vanity? Or is something surviving found in it free from vanity, and was it said without cause: Universal vanity, every man living? For man is universal vanity, because in body and in mind he is subject to vanity. What man? *Every man living.* Not every man life, but every man living. For man is both life and man is living. Where the life, and where the living. Life in that which quickens; living in that which is quickened. And the flesh lives from the soul, and the soul from the flesh: and both live, but the flesh has wholly from the soul that it lives? the soul has not wholly in the flesh that it lives. For it lives also in God, and would that it did live? What lives in the flesh is vain. What lives in God [0124A] is not vain, but is true, because it is from truth. In that part therefore in which it lives from truth, it truly reproves vanity, and it is found how vanity reproves vanity, not through vanity but through truth. Rightly therefore Ecclesiastes, who in mind was lifted above all vanity and had come through to truth itself, saw sublimely in that same truth what he truly said concerning the reproving of vanity: Vanity of vanities, vanity of vanities, and all is vanity. The driving home of the words with the affection of the speaker signifies both the greatness of the matter and the newness of the wonder. And it is a sentiment uttered with heightening. For there are certain degrees, and as it were certain progressions, for one going both to the better and to the worse. For thus is it said the Song of songs, and the age of ages [0124B], just as here it is now said: Vanity of vanities — so that by a certain like distance and a similar difference the Song of songs may be understood to excel above the song only so far as a song excels a word, and next a word silence. And an age of ages to excel an age only so far as an age alone excels a moment, or again a moment nothing. And with a like progression made here by the contrary: that vanity of vanities is worse than simple vanity by so much as simple vanity is worse than a solid and abiding essence. Thus by driving home the sentiment may be read: Vanity of vanities, vanity of vanities, and all is vanity — so that in all things vanity of every kind may be expressed, and out of all things a certain sum may be understood to be made up, which contains every vanity, so that as it were each single [0124C] thing is by itself vanity, and the universe gathered out of them all is vanity of vanities. So in the creation of things, when God was completing his works, each single work is by itself called *good [n: (Gen. I)]*, and last of all, all things being perfected, the universe is named *very good*. For if that which in part is good is found better in the whole, that also which in part is evil must needs be worse in the whole. Therefore vanity is of vanities, and the vanity of all vanities is universal vanity, and the universality of vanity is all vanity. Or else that vanity of human minds is here reproved by comparison with the other vanities: which, as risen out of the other vanities, and having surpassed all vanities by comparison of vanity, is deservedly called vanity [0124D] of vanities. It can also not incongruously be that this threefold repetition of vanity is referred to the three kinds of vanities recalled above, so that as it were one of the vanities is the work of man, another mortal nature, the third this whole world with its universe of mutable things. For this man considered the works of mortals, and saw of each single one that with labor and grief they come to their effect. When made they subsist for a brief time; but passing away they leave no fruit behind them. He saw with how great affliction and misery human life is worn down daily without ceasing, even in those things which it works for its own consolation, and how it almost always suffers more loss in seeking a remedy than it receives of consolation in obtaining a solace. And in all these things, at how vainly every man living is troubled, wondering and amazed [0125A] he says: Vanity of vanities. Then, attending to the very condition of mortals, and finding in it a greater and more miserable vanity, he saw that if it is reckoned a great vanity when that which man makes passes away, then that vanity is altogether to be grieved over and pitied when that which man is cannot stand. Upon this therefore, redoubling again, he cried out and said: Vanity of vanities. Last of all, beholding the [sic: *inconstantinam*] and the fluctuation of all mutable things hastening to their setting, concluding as it were in a sum he added and said: All is vanity.



## HOMILY THE FIRST. *On the title of the work, on Solomon's names, and books, and on the beginnings of this work, on the explanation of the vanities.* (cont.)

*What hath a man more of all his labour that he laboureth under the sun?* What hath he more — it is understood — than that which has been said of all things: Vanity of vanities, vanity of vanities, and all is vanity? As [0125B] if he were saying: If all things are subject to vanity, how can the works of men be alien from vanity? If according to something that is vain which God created, how is that not much more vain which man makes? If that is temporal which the Eternal made, what is that which the temporal makes? Therefore what hath a man more of all his labour that he laboureth under the sun? He laboureth under the sun, namely either as acting or as suffering. Distinguish these two: he who acts and he who suffers labors under the sun; each labors, both he who acts labors, and he who suffers labors. But this is the difference, that the one labors as though unwilling, the other willingly. For he who acts labors, and himself makes that from which he labors. But he who suffers labors, and does not himself make, but sustains, that from which he labors. These two kinds of men live in this world, namely those who labor [0125C] and act, and those who labor and suffer. Who are they that labor and act? Hear the Psalmist: *Nevertheless universal vanity, every man living. Nevertheless man passeth as an image; yea and he is disquieted in vain. He storeth up, and knoweth not for whom he shall gather these things [n: (Psal. XXXVIII)]*. Who are they that labor and suffer? Hear Paul the apostle: *The creature,* he says, *was made subject to vanity, not willingly; but by reason of him that made it subject, in hope [n: (Rom. VIII)]*. Therefore both labor: both those, namely, to whom this life is sweet, and those to whom this life is bitter. Those labor to enjoy its delights; these labor to be freed from its misery. Those labor in fear lest they go hence quickly; these labor in dread lest they remain here long. The labor of the former is reproved for vain solicitude. The labor [0125D] of the latter is crowned for patience. To the former it is said: *Sufficient for the day is the evil thereof [n: (Matth. VI)]*. And that: *Be not solicitous, saying: What shall we eat, or what shall we drink, or wherewith shall we be clothed? [n: (Ibid.)]* To the latter it is said: *Be you also patient, strengthen your hearts [n: (Jac. V)]*. And that: *If we suffer with him, we shall also reign with him. If we die with him, we believe that we shall live also together with him [n: (II Tim. II)]*. To the former it is said: *Ye sons of men, how long will ye be dull of heart, why do ye love vanity, and seek after lying? [n: (Psal. IV.)]* To the latter it is said: *Blessed are all they that fear the Lord, that walk in his ways. For thou shalt eat the labours of thy hands, blessed art thou, and it shall be well with thee [n: (Psal. CXXVII)]*. Therefore the labor of the former has a penalty in the work, and a penalty in the recompense. [0126A] But the labor of the latter has indeed a penalty in the work, but a reward in the recompense. In the labor of the former there is true misery. In the fruit of their labor, a false and vain consolation. In the labor of the latter, a temporal and transitory affliction. In the fruit of their labor, eternal blessedness. Therefore the labor of the former is empty in its fruit, true in its misery. The labor of the latter is transitory in its penalty, eternal in its fruit. But there, for the evil man, what of good seems to be present is vain; the evil of these men which appears is as it were vain, because it is transitory; but the good which does not appear is perpetual. Let it not therefore seem wondrous that we call the evil in this life laboring and acting, but the good only laboring and suffering, since if we look more closely upon the truth, we shall find the evil always laboring by acting, and the good always by suffering. [0126B] For as incontinence harasses the evil even then when they seem to be at rest, so patience keeps the good tranquil even when they are set in labors. Wherefore in a certain wondrous manner all the good and the just, when for God's sake they waste themselves with voluntary affliction, then also labor by suffering; because, setting themselves against themselves, what outwardly they inflict by raging in strictness, inwardly, remaining quiet and without disturbance, they bear in patience. But all the perverse and lovers of the carnal life, even then when they sustain evils brought upon them from without by another's violence, because inwardly they disturb themselves with the goads of fury and of impatience, they rather themselves make that from which they labor. Therefore the good labor in this life, but the evil labor even for the sake of this life; because the temporal labors to which all the depraved [0126C] and perverse subject themselves for the obtaining of temporal sweetness, the good bear patiently for the sake of eternal consolation. But because this mortal life, in which and for which men labor, tends daily through the spaces of living to the end of life, rightly is all the labor of man reckoned in the number of the vanities, when it is said: What hath a man more of all his labour that he laboureth under the sun? [nt: It is understood: apart from vanity.]



## HOMILY THE FIRST. *On the title of the work, on Solomon's names, and books, and on the beginnings of this work, on the explanation of the vanities.* (cont.)

It is to be noted, however, that he does not say: What more is the labor of man? But: What hath a man more, he says, of all his labour that he laboureth under the sun? Whence it stands firm that here the matter treated is not so much the labor of man as the fruit and profit of human labor; [0126D] because it is not asked what or of what sort the labor of man is, but what fruit man obtains of all his labor by which he labors under the sun. And all this fruit is declared to be nothing other than vanity, when it is said: What hath he more? As if it were said: Man hath nothing more than vanity of his labor. Therefore vanity is the whole fruit of man's labor, and every man labors in vain, being about to receive nothing but vanity of all his labor. Was the labor then of the saints also vain, who faithfully served God, and for love of him suffered so many torments and kinds of tortures? Who would presume to say this? For how was the labor of those men vain who, vexed indeed for a little while, came through transitory griefs to abiding joys, and through a temporal death obtained everlasting life? [0127A] Or is that labor to be called vain for this reason, because what they suffered has passed away: and yet according to something not vain, because what they received abides? For that even the labor of the just is vain — so far, that is, as regards the transitory penalty and this as it were imaginary appearance of misery — the Psalmist seems to hint, when he says: *Thou who framest labour in commandment [n: (Psal. XCIII)]*. For God as it were frames labor for his elect, when outwardly, as though angry, he inflicts upon them by judgment a temporal penalty, for whom inwardly, by the providence of his mercy, he keeps the rewards of eternal blessedness. Since therefore the Psalmist names the labor of the just, and this imaginary grief, because it has the appearance of misery but does not have true misery, a *framed* thing, he assuredly shows [0127B] that this same labor can also, according to something, be not unfittingly called vain. Behold therefore the labor of the work passes away. What was vain passes away; what was transitory has passed away: the labor has passed away, the grief has passed away. Has the fruit of the work passed away, or the wage of the labor? Therefore it is permitted: behold, according to a certain manner of speaking, the labor itself and the temporal grief is not unfittingly called vain. Yet will the wage of that same labor ever rightly be called vanity? God forbid! How then shall that stand which was said: What hath a man more of all his labour? For it was said of the reward of labor and not of the labor itself, of the profit, not of the work: What hath a man more of all his labour? Behold, man hath more, and much more hath he, [0127C] and thou sayest: What more hath he? Therefore he added and said: That he laboureth under the sun. Behold, we have the difficulty of this question resolved. For here he openly shows what labors he reproves of vanity. Those which are done, he says, under the sun. What is it, to be done under the sun? Perhaps what he says, under the sun, is to be taken simply; because men, dwelling in this lower part of the world, receive from above the light of the sun, and are lighted so that they may see for the doing of the works necessary to human uses. For on this account the times of human life have been so distributed by divine providence in alternating turns, that the very vicissitude of the alternating times might repair sick bodies wearied by too great a stretching out of their actions. Therefore night and day unceasingly succeed one another [0127D] with mutual evenness, so that by day the sun, passing over from its rising to its setting in its course above, may pour out its light on every side upon the things beneath it, and, illuminating human eyes by its presence, may rouse both those eyes and those of the other living things of the earth to the exercise of acting, and at the same time afford the means of working. But by night, withdrawing itself from human sight, and, as with certain swaddling-bands of infancy, spreading over them the veil of darkness and covering the eyes of mortals, it may foster their weary limbs to rest and slumber, that it may restore them again, repaired and more eager, to labor and to the day's exercise. This wondrous order of the divine dispensation the Psalmist beheld, when, commending God's works, he calls this to mind among the rest as something excellent and worthy of no small praise, saying: *He hath made [0128A] the moon for seasons; the sun knoweth his going down. Thou hast appointed darkness, and it is night: in it shall all the beasts of the woods go about. The young lions roaring after their prey, and seeking their meat from God. The sun ariseth, and they are gathered together, and they shall lie down in their dens. Man shall go forth to [sic: od] his work, and to his labour until the evening [n: (Psal. CIII)]*. Hence also is that which God promises to Noah as he goes out of the ark after the flood, as for some great sign of his loving-kindness and gift of his bounty, saying: *All the days of seedtime and harvest, cold and heat, summer and winter, night and day shall not cease [n: (Gen. VIII)]*. Beautifully therefore he said that men, distended down below in this ark of the world through the various runnings-about of the occupations of mortal life, labor under the sun, so that this too belongs to the vanity [0128B] of human work: that a light passing over from above, and to be ended in darkness, gives light to man for his working. For he considered how abject and wretched is the condition of mortals, who upon the surface of the earth, like certain worms creeping in vain curiosity, are roused by a light suddenly poured in from above, as though to be mocked, to snatch at what they vainly desire, and, the same being withdrawn after a little, are wrapped about again in a sudden blindness, and can in no wise go forward further into effect. Therefore the very light by which they are lighted is shown to them from afar and at a distance from above, and unapproachable to man, so that it may be proved to stand in no way in man's power; so far that man receives from it only so much as the graces of God, who bestows and orders all things, shall have permitted, not so much as [0128C] man's perverse will, serving cupidity, desires. A great vanity therefore it is to labor under the sun, and small confidence to labor under the sun: whose light, however pleasant to look upon and delightful to the sight it be, yet cannot always be present to human eyes nor profit them. But if it please us to push our consideration yet higher and to look into the matter more subtly, we can take time to be signified by the name of the sun, so that what he said, under the sun, may be the same as if he had said, under time. For because chiefly by the sun both the course of the times is distinguished and the nature of temporal things is varied, time itself is fittingly expressed by the sun, when it is said under the sun it is the same as if he had said, under time. But has he perhaps in this too willed to intimate [sic: *in nuere*] something to us, in that he did not say in time or with time, [0128D] but under time: and must a discernment be had by us, that we may understand that some things are those which were as it were made with time; but others those which were either made in time, or are done under time? Let us discern therefore, if we can, whether perchance we may be able to find the reason why he said rather that the works of men are vain as made under time than either with time or in time. All things which were made were either made with time, or in time. For those things were made with time whose rising time did not precede: such as the angelic nature, and that formless matter of visible things, which in the beginning God created. Those things were made in time whose rising time preceded, such as those works of the six days in which God perfected and completed [0129A] the fabric of this sensible world; but those things also which afterwards were made formally, or essentially, were all made in time. Again, of those things which are done in time, some are done in time and under time, others are done in time only, not under time. Those are done in time and not under time which indeed begin in time, but are not ended in time. Those are done in time and under time which both begin in time and are ended in time. Likewise, of those things which are done under time, some are done for the sake of time, others are done for the sake of eternity. Those therefore which are done for the sake of time are vain both in act and in fruit. But those which are done for the sake of eternity are indeed vain in act; but in fruit they are not vain, because, even if they pass away in the work, yet they abide in the recompense. For a good work [0129B] is not this alone which appears outwardly transitory; because it consists both of a visible action and of an invisible devotion. For, as man consists of a living thing and of life, and the living thing indeed dies, but the life does not die; so without doubt the good has both a visible action in itself, and an invisible devotion. The visible action is as it were the body; the inward devotion is as it were the spirit. The action lives through devotion, as the body through the soul. But devotion quickens the action, as the soul the body. For every action which is without devotion is dead. Therefore the visible action itself as it were lives, and the invisible devotion itself is life. But that which lives, this dies; but that which is life, this does not die. Because a transitory action, just as it has its beginning from time, so indeed it is ended in time. That therefore which dies, [0129C] which is ended, which passes away, which is given over to mutability, is itself liable to vanity. But that which is life, which abides, which does not admit of an end or of corruption, does not itself admit vanity. Let it be said therefore: What hath a man more of all his labour that he laboureth under the sun? [nt: it is understood: apart from vanity.] Nothing more, because all things which are done under the sun are vain. And what would he have of vain things but vanity? What then? Will good works which are done under the sun be able to confer nothing more upon those who work them than vanity? And how did we say above that this question was resolved by that which he says, under the sun? Behold, the same question rises up for us again, no less difficult than the one above, because we cannot deny that good works too are done under the sun, that is, under time, according to what [0129D] has been expounded above; and yet we do not dare to say that nothing but vanity follows from them. And behold, the book plainly says: What hath a man more of all his labour that he laboureth under the sun? But if we are willing to consider the very words more diligently, it is plainly shown what those works are which are reproved as vain. For he did not say: What hath a man more of all his labour which is done, or which was done, under the sun? But rather he says, which man himself laboureth under the sun. He does not therefore reprove the labor so much as the laborer as being under the sun. So that because man himself, set under the sun, labors, therefore what he labors at is vain. But who is he who labors under the sun? He who, pressed down by love of temporal and transitory [0130A] things, labors for the obtaining of the same, if he does not have them, or for the keeping of them, if he has them. This man, set under the sun, labors, and this man labors in vain. But he who exercises good works for the hope and desire of eternal things, this man does not labor under the sun, even though the labor in which he labors is under the sun; because, although the work which he carries through is temporal, yet he himself transcends all temporal and falling things in mind and in devotion. This man therefore does not labor in vain, even though that in which he labors is vain; because that for which he labors is not vain. And when that which is vain, in which he labors, has passed away, he has ready that which is not vanity, for which he labors. Such a man therefore does not belong to that sentiment in which it was said: What hath a man more of all his labour that he laboureth under the sun? Because he does not [0130B] labor under the sun, but above the sun, who does not set the wage of his labor in things rolling and passing away with time.



## HOMILY THE FIRST. *On the title of the work, on Solomon's names, and books, and on the beginnings of this work, on the explanation of the vanities.* (cont.)

*One generation passeth away, and another generation cometh.* Behold vanity. One generation passeth away, and another generation cometh. This human nature conceived, corrupted in the first root of its stock, and, sown together with this same corruption, it sprouted out into the whole offspring of posterity. For the first parent of the human race, as soon as he heard: Earth thou art and unto earth thou shalt go, from that time made mortal, because he fell from the state of incorruption, began as it were to pass over from life to death through mortality. This way therefore all men after him pass through, who descend from him by the birth of the flesh. This is that vanity of which the Psalmist spoke, saying: *Nevertheless [0130C] universal vanity, every man living. Nevertheless man passeth as an image [n: (Psal. XXXVIII)]*. This vanity therefore, as greater than the rest and more horrible to human minds because of the dread necessity of death, he added in the last place, so that man might hear this at the end, in which he may more evidently recognize the misery of his own condition. For the first vanity is not even in the name; nor is it referred to man. The second vanity is indeed not in man; but yet it is referred to man. The third vanity both is in man, and is referred to man. Accordingly, as though advancing by certain steps always to higher things, he set first that vanity which is neither the penalty of man nor his fault. Then he added that vanity which is not the penalty of man, but the fault. Last of all, that which is not the fault, but the penalty: [0130D] although yet neither can such a fault ever be without penalty, nor is such a penalty — at least in those to whom the sacrament of salvation does not come to aid — altogether free from fault. But I have called that the fault of man, not the penalty, and this the penalty, not the fault; because in the one man's desert is discerned, and in the other judgment is found. This penalty therefore, as heavier and more monstrous than the rest, he set last: which, though it be not more wicked than the fault, is yet more grievous to the flesh, and more effectual for the rousing of carnal minds. Let him say therefore: One generation passeth away, and another generation cometh. As if he were saying: If you think it is to be neglected that everything you do is vain, is even this to be neglected, that you yourselves are vanity? One generation passeth away, and another generation cometh. If the dying [0131A] depart and those who are to die follow after, what joy is there? The grief of the living seems indeed to receive consolation in those who are born; but from that whence it is thought to be ended so that it should not be, from thence it receives that it should always be. For that the dying depart is grief. What consolation follows in that those who are to die come on? A consolation which is bounded by mourning. And what can be vainer in either? One generation passeth away, and another generation cometh. If some lamentable chance were to involve only a few, or at least not all, it would seem tolerable; but as it is, a savage plague drags all with it to destruction. One generation passeth away, and another generation cometh. If it passeth away, whither does it go? If it cometh, whence does it come? Alas, dread lot! How canst thou be secure, O man, wrapped in such great darkness? Behold, thou knowest that by living thou tendest to death. But canst thou know [0131B] of what sort, or where, thou art to be after death? Consider therefore in how great a vanity thou livest, thou who daily seest that which thou art pass away; nor yet canst thou by any reasoning know of what sort is that which thou art to be. One generation passeth away, and another generation cometh.

*But the earth standeth for ever.* Hearken, O man, and blush. The earth standeth for ever. Why does the earth stand, and thou canst not stand, for whose sake the earth was made? Perhaps thou wilt say, because it stands for thy consolation. It stands that it may send forth those who come, bear those who pass over, and receive those who depart. For those who are born come from the earth by the origin of the flesh, and the living pass over upon the earth by the mutability of the flesh, and the dying return into the earth by the corruption of the flesh. Therefore it stands for thy consolation; nay rather it stands for thy confusion [0131C]. How? See of what sort it stands, of what sort it bears, and whom it bears, and out of what sort it bears. Of what sort therefore does it bear? Hear of what sort: *Cursed is the earth in thy work; thorns and thistles shall it bring forth to thee [n: (Gen. III)]*. And whom does it bear? Hear again: *Cursed art thou upon the earth [n: (Gen. IV)]*. And out of what sort does it bear: *In the sweat of thy face shalt thou eat thy bread; and thou shalt eat the herbs of the earth? [n: (Gen. III.)]* Therefore the cursed bears the cursed. And yet it bears them. But from that whence it bears, it has something for thy consolation and something for thy penalty. What is for thy consolation is with thy labor; what is for thy penalty is through thy labor. It has bread, and it has thorns also. Bread with thy labor, for thy consolation; thorns through thy labor, for thy penalty. In the sweat of thy face [0131D] shalt thou eat thy bread: this is for thy consolation, with thy labor. When thou hast worked, the earth will not give thee its fruit, but thorns and thistles shall it bring forth to thee. This is for thy penalty, through thy labor. Therefore it stands not only for thy consolation, but much more it stands for thy confusion. It stands not only for the easing of thy misery, but it stands for the testimony and the penalty of thy fault. The earth standeth, and thou standest upon the earth. Stable is she who bears, and thou fallest in ruin who art borne. Why is this? Art thou too not earth? What then was said to thee: Earth thou art, and unto earth thou shalt go? Therefore thou art earth, and thou art upon the earth, and thou art of earth. Why then dost thou not stand for ever? Hast thou been made weaker than that earth from which thou wast taken? What then did the hand of the craftsman work, if it did not advance [0132A] its work to something better? Was there then no knowledge with him, or was he found weak, so that in his hands that good matter should be made worse? God forbid! Nay rather, he made thee much better than that had been out of which he made thee; because he had made the earth stable, but thee he made out of earth immortal. Therefore he made thee better than that had been out of which he made thee. Not therefore from the craftsman did thy corruption proceed, who made thee with so great power and with such goodness that nothing could be less than perfect, nothing would he have worse than the best. And so the best craftsman made a good thing formed, whose eternal incorruption and immortal goodness neither begets a fault nor receives a fault.

But perhaps the matter is to be blamed, which was such that nothing else could be made of it. For the matter was earth, and there came forth an earthen vessel. What? This is a wonder. [0132B] Thou therefore, in saying this, oughtest to have looked to this: that, if thou art an earthen vessel, then thou wast hardened out of soft mud into a potsherd, and thy clod, if it had not the nature of firmness, yet had the nature of confirmation. If it had not that this should be by nature, yet it had by nature that this could be by grace. Therefore thy matter did thee no harm, which both keeps whole what nature conferred, and does not itself diminish what grace gave. For the earth standeth for ever. What is *standeth*? It perseveres in that which it was made; it keeps its own nature; it does not desert its own condition; what it received, this it retains uncorrupted. Earth it was made, and earth it remains; never is it other than what it is. And when it is other, it is other not by the failing of corruption, but by the advance of exaltation. Just as in thee [0132C] it has been made other, and is to be other, than it was born; but by exaltation, not by corruption. And when in certain earthly things it seems to carry itself over from one thing into another, yet it is never other than it is; because it is earth, and always it is earth, and that which passes over is earth and that into which it passes over is earth; and when this passes over into that, or into that other, yet that which is earth does not pass over, which in this and in that is the same. Therefore the earth has to be moved within the bosom of its own nature, and has to work out of that which it is, that which it is: it has not to pass over beyond the bounds of its own nature out of that which it is into that which it is not: and for that reason, the earth standeth for ever in that which it is not. Why therefore hast not thou too stood in that in which thou wast made upon the earth, just as [0132D] the earth stands in that in which it was made? Why hast thou degenerated, so as not even to imitate thy matter, which so far keeps what it is, that even when thou hast been made together with the earth, thou canst not be other than the earth is? As far as that thou flowest away by thy own fault; beyond it thou art not permitted to go forward by its nature. Behold how much it guards what it is, so that even when it has received thee, flowing and unstable, it does not suffer thee to pass over further into another thing which itself is not. Whence then is it for thee to flow away? Search diligently, and think. Neither thy craftsman nor thy matter did this for thee; because she, both by standing for ever, keeps in thee also what she herself is, and he, by making thee for eternity, gave also more than she is. Whence therefore is this for thee, save from thy own fault, out of which it is now a penalty for thee that thou returnest into her, to whom this had been given of grace, that thou shouldest stand always [0133A] above her? Therefore thou passest away, and the earth standeth in testimony against thee, so that, reproving thee as mortal and receiving thee as dying, it may convict thee before it punishes thee. Therefore the earth standeth for ever. One generation passeth away, and another generation cometh; but the earth standeth for ever.

These things we have said, according to the littleness or the scantiness of our understanding, upon the opening of the book of Solomon which is called Ecclesiastes, in no way presuming that we have said what most of all had to be said, since it is so great a thing for us only not to have said what had not to be said. But for the rest it must be known that this book requires a certain new kind of exposition; because, since it wholly aims at moving the affections of the human heart, one must more often form one's discourse in it as though by conversing than by expounding. Whence [0133B] it is needful in these matters sometimes even to dwell longer upon words in those things which seem plain and open, so that the very driving home of the utterance may touch more strongly and penetrate more effectually the heart of the hearer. He who shall wish to handle this Scripture otherwise, even if he serves conveniently the understanding of his hearers, yet, not retaining its force and property, profits perhaps less unto edification.



## HOMILY II. *On the proof of the vanity of all things under heaven: through the corruption of the elements, through the generation of things, the succession, and the forgetting of those things which have been.*

The words of Ecclesiastes, which testify that all things under the sun are vain, have — I know not by what compact — just now, when they were read, been made sweet in our ears. And behold, we have begun willingly to hear our own evils, and what we do not love [0133C] we nevertheless love to hear. For we do not love our own evils, and we love to hear our own evils; because by hearing the evils which we do not love, we remember the goods which we do love. And this very remembrance of goods (even amid evils) is sweet to the soul, and assuredly so much the sweeter the more bitter are those evils themselves, which, whether by hearing or by feeling, we recognize to be far from those goods for which, at least by remembrance, we sigh. This was the whole reason why we wonder that this recital alone has just now been savory to us, in which the hardship of our exile is described to us, and it is declared of what sort and how great our misery and calamity is. For our mind, in the hearing of its own evils, as though awaking out of a certain sleep of former time, suddenly [0133D] recognized where it was, and, amazed and wondering at so great an abyss of ruin, began at the same time also to consider from how great a height it had fallen into this very depth of misery, and, because it could not yet do so in effect, it sighed after that height with a certain wondrous desire of inward ardor. This therefore was what in those words which recited our misery drew our affection. This made it that it should ever be pleasing to us thus to hear our own evils, and that whatever that be which is perceived along with that which is so sweet cannot be bitter. This daily makes their very laments sweet to the wretched, and amid sighs and groans feeds the afflicted delightfully with tears; because the remembrance of the things that are loved is sweeter amid adversities, and the soul itself burns the more with desire of things absent, is touched the more sweetly by the memory of things past, [0134A] is the more strongly inflamed by the deferring of things to come. This our Ecclesiastes knew excellently well, who in his whole narrative so diligently pursues the vanity of passing things, that out of the consideration of it he may admonish the human heart to meditate upon heavenly things and to awake in desire of things eternal. For he knew that the more evidently the vanity of those things which pass away in time is recognized, the more sublimely within is the eye of the mind opened to the state of eternity. Beginning therefore, in this first part of his work, he shows that all things are vain which are under the sun, that is, that whatsoever things are visible are subject to corruption, or liable to mutability. For he shows that neither in heaven, nor under heaven, nor upon the earth, nor in the earth is there anything perpetual which does not suffer vicissitude nor [0134B] pass over into alteration. And therefore that nothing truly is out of all those things which are always without ceasing drawn into that which they are not, and cannot keep stable or uncorrupted what they are.

*The sun riseth, and goeth down, and returneth to his place: and there rising again, he goeth about by the south, and turneth again to the north.* This is to be distinguished by itself. Then follows:

*Surveying all things round about, the spirit goeth forward, and returneth in his circuits.* And this is to be set down by itself. Afterwards follows:

*All the rivers run into the sea, yet the sea doth not overflow: unto the place from whence the rivers come, they return, to flow again.* And this is to be joined together.

Three things therefore he seems to set forth as an argument [0134C] for the mutability of all things: the sun, the spirit, the rivers, that is, fire, air, moisture — so that by the sun fire, by the spirit air, by the rivers moisture may be understood to be signified, as the whole from a part. For above he set the earth as it were an immovable foundation in the middle place. Now he disposes the other three elements, that is, fire, air, water, fluctuating about it with unstable motion, so that in the procreating of mutable things he may show that to these three elements the motion of acting and, in a certain manner, the office of the craftsman has been assigned. But that the earth lies subject, immovable, as a certain matter of all things to be procreated; powerless indeed to act, but only fit that out of it, while the rest work, there may be formed those things which are to be created [0134D]. Through these three elements therefore nature exercises its power in that fourth, which elements, running about that fourth and in it by a wondrous law, and tempering one another, bring forth out of it so many innumerable forms and species of things as are shown by the yearly, the monthly, the daily vicissitudes of the seasons, alternating continually without ceasing in their approach and their withdrawal. Since therefore all things being born which rise out of the earth and receive from the earth their substance and nourishment obtain the nature of vital motion from these three elements, rightly did he place in them both the origin of mutability, and did he express the nature and property of all mutable things, so that in these we may equally see both whence it is that whatever rises under time cannot stand, and by what way that which is not comes forth to be present, or that which [0135A] is returns to not being. The sun riseth, and goeth down, and returneth to his place, and there rising again he goeth about by the south, and turneth again to the north. In few words he has embraced a great matter: concerning which we must say some things according to the time, yet not detain the hearer's mind longer upon such matters, lest we seem, against our own purpose, to be rolled away from the sacred exposition into describing the courses of the stars. All these things indeed have been said for this alone, that the human mind may be the more conveniently roused by the recital of those things which it knows, and that through these it may awake to think upon those things which it does not know: and therefore it ought not to be a weariness to us, nor seem unworthy, to attend diligently to those things which the divine discourse has taken care to recount diligently to us. He shows therefore by what way [0135B] this great luminary, which illuminates all things by its presence, is carried round in this globe of the sensible world with unceasing agitation, and by its approach and withdrawal works the various changes of things. The sun riseth, and goeth down, and returneth to his place. First he shows that it has a circular motion, and always returns to that from which it goes forward, so that, because in a circle no end can be found, its course may be shown to be perpetual. The sun riseth, and goeth down, and returneth to his place. Then he describes the windings of its progression, saying: He goeth about by the south, and turneth again to the north — so that this very thing may belong to the great changing of things, that it does not always in the same manner nor in a straight line direct its course as it goes. The sun riseth, and goeth down. It riseth daily, when emerging [0135C] it is presented to human sight. It goeth down, when descending to the lower parts according to the convexity of its [sic: *circum actionis*] it withdraws itself from our eyes. It returneth to his place; because, its rolling driving it on, it is called back beneath again to its rising. But after its rising, tending to its setting, it is first turned toward the south, because by an oblique course, especially when it tarries in the summer signs, it climbs from the rising to the meridian line. But then, descending to the setting, it is bent again by an oblique progression toward the north. Still another exposition, fitting enough, can be applied to these words: namely, that we take all these things to be said not of the long-continued but of the yearly course of the sun. For the sun riseth to us when, [0135D] entering through the vernal equinox, it rises toward our pole. But it goeth down, when going out through the autumnal equinox it descends to the lower parts. It goeth about by the south, when it tarries in the winter signs. It turneth again to the north, when it is carried round in the summer signs, which are nearer to the northern pole. The sun riseth therefore, and at its rising all things equally rise which revive from its heat. It goeth down, and at once, the vital fire being withdrawn, all things fall which flourished from its presence. It goeth about by the south, that the winter's cold may temper the summer's burnings. It turneth again to the north, that those things which the winter's frost and icy torpor had bound fast may, warmed again by the summer's heats, be quickened. Thou seest therefore how great changes of things one body draws along with it.



## HOMILY II. *On the proof of the vanity of all things under heaven: through the corruption of the elements, through the generation of things, the succession, and the forgetting of those things which have been.* (cont.)

[0136A]Surveying all things round about, the spirit goeth forward, and returneth in his circuits. If by the spirit we have taken the air, then in fitting order, after he had described the instability of the heavenly element, he shows that the lower things also suffer the same law, saying: Surveying all things round about, the spirit goeth forward. As if he were saying: If the force of nature does not suffer those things which are in heaven to stand, it is necessary that the same condition of mutability govern those things also which are under heaven. Indeed, just as to the sun? so also to the air he attributes an orbicular motion, so that its agitation may be shown to be perpetual. But that he subjoins in the plural: He returneth in his circuits — this can signify that the motion of the air is not always and everywhere the same, but that, since in one place it is stirred more vehemently, in another more moderately, its very fluctuation, [0136B] poured back into itself by a reversed turn, is moved unequally; and does not uniformly come to rest, so that we may not be forced to extend a motion once begun as far as the uttermost, but that the several motions and impulses, made as it were upon themselves; and gradually, the motion languishing over the space, come to rest. But that also which he says, Surveying, is aptly attributed to this element, in that by its mobility it cleanses and scatters all the filth sprinkled or gathered anywhere. Or because, penetrating all things by its subtlety, it pours itself out everywhere. Or else this word is coupled to the preceding sentiment thus: The sun riseth, and goeth down, and returneth to his place; and there rising again he goeth about by the south, and turneth again to the north, surveying all things round about. And here, a distinction being made, then the following sentiment is brought in thus: [0136C] The spirit goeth forward, etc. For the sun by its course surveys all things round about; because it touches all the parts of the world, rising or setting through the diverse signs. For in Cancer, rising and setting, it touches the north. In Capricorn, the south. In Libra and in Aries, it passes through the middle by the rising and the setting. There follows: The spirit goeth forward, and returneth in his circuits.
