According to the preceding exposition, we can now not incongruously [sic: *incovenienter*] take the spirit to be that fiery power which, proceeding from the sun itself, pours itself out through all things, and, invisibly penetrating the universe, quickens and moves it. Whence the ancients also said that nature is. A craftsman fire, going forward into sensible things to be procreated. For of vital motion, and of sensible quickening, in all things that are born [0136D] the fiery power is the origin: which supplies increase to all things, and, feeding and cherishing them with an invisible nourishment, brings them at length to an invisible substance. And this hidden power of nature, moving and feeding all things, the poet also named a spirit: and his words are well known: *First, the heaven and the lands, and the liquid plains,*
*And the shining globe of the moon, and the Titanian stars,*
*A spirit within doth nourish: and, poured through the limbs,*
*Mind stirs the whole mass, and mingles itself with the great body.*
[n: (Aeneid. VI, 688.)] Although in these words he seems rather to approve that error which asserts that this sensible world is as it were a soul consisting of soul and body, and that its very soul is the spirit moving all things. But [0137A] in whatever way the opinion of those who err be expounded, we with a sound understanding can take the spirit going forward into all things, and returning in his circuits, to be the hidden power of nature, which invisibly nourishes and quickens the universe, and leads each single thing to a certain end and lawful term. This spirit therefore, that is, the hidden power of nature, surveys all things; because, pouring itself out invisibly through all the parts of things, it distributes to each its proper quality according to the capacity of its nature. It goeth forward, and returneth, because it first advances by increase all things which rise in time, and then leads them back, as they grow old, to their origin. For it goeth forward, because it goes out in things being born, and in things growing it proceeds as far as a certain measure. It returneth, because in things growing old, and slipping backward through the failing [0137B] of their nature, it is called back as far as the beginning of its own origin. Thus therefore we rightly take the courses and progressions of individual things to be as it were certain circles and orbs running back into themselves; because all things that have risen in time return at length by their setting thither whence they went out by the beginning of their birth. And each single thing indeed is proved to have, according to its own measure and the time in which it subsists, a course differing from another, that is, greater or less. But all things are known to have, as one origin, so also one end; because all things return by their setting to the earth, which at the beginning of their rising went out of the earth. Just as therefore the sun riseth and goeth down, and returneth to his place, so the spirit goeth forward, and returneth in his circuits. Because [0137C] just as in the body of the sun the fire, going forward locally, is led back to the beginning of its rising, so also naturally in all things that are born, the motion which it raised up through the increase of things, it leads back to its own origin through their failing and setting.

There follows a third clause in which is treated the element of water, which in the order of things is placed third after fire, in procreation is associated as second. For as much as pertains to the disposition and the order of things, fire possesses the first and highest place; air the second, next to fire. Afterwards water claims the third place; in the last and lowest place the earth settles. Among these four, fire and water, by nature indeed most opposed, yet friendly by an even confederation [0137D], are the origin of all things that are born. And therefore if we attend to place or to nature, we discern these two to be separated from one another. If we look to their effect, we find them both nearest to one another and friendly. Therefore concerning the element of water, which affords to things being born the other vital fostering along with fire, as though third after fire and air, or second along with fire, silence was not to be kept among the natures of mutable things. And for that reason he describes this element's circuit also, and its running out into the procreating of sensible things, saying: All the rivers run into the sea, and the sea doth not overflow. And at once he adds the reason why the sea does not overflow when all the rivers have been received into it: namely, because again they return to the place from whence the rivers come, to flow again. For the channels of the waters, through the whole [0138A] body of the earth, draw moisture inwardly and from without after the manner of veins, so that the watering may be evenly poured out into every part. But that this infusion may be able to be continual and perpetual, therefore the course of the waters has been disposed in an orb, and in that perennial circling, so that what is poured in from above may never fail; as much as flows downward in the one part, so much in the other part is called back to its rising through hidden passages. So also the food of the body is carried by a certain circuit, and first descending from the palate into the belly, and then passing over into the privy, and in that very passage of it, as by a certain instilling of nature, nourishment is supplied to what is failing; and when this has been evaporated and emptied out, it is necessary again that recourse be had to the help of eating for the repairing of the weakening limbs. As it were therefore a circle [0138B] of ever-renewed need, running back into its own origin, is drawn; because, while without ceasing what has been taken passes away, it is always necessary that it be taken again, so that it may be able to furnish substance for the supplying of the defect. Such nourishment therefore as the substance of food supplies by assiduous repetition to the body it feeds, altogether such nourishment does the reflowing of the elements pour into the body of the earth for the procreating of its offspring and the quickening of its seeds. This fire, this water, this air cease not to do by continual approach and withdrawal without intermission, so that they may always replenish the things that befall and empty out the things that recede, so that, because the vigor of the past infusion cannot subsist, of necessity the watering for the repairing of fullness may be repeated anew. What confidence, therefore, of perpetual subsistence will the nourishments of things be able to afford, [0138C] when in all things thus always without ceasing both the things that repair pass away, and the things repaired fail? For although a repeated infusion seems to afford some sort of consolation to this defect, yet it is a great misery always to receive for need, and never to be able to lack the need of receiving. Whence it is manifest that all transitory and falling things are vain, which continually grow empty of the solidity of their own state, either by passing away or by failing. A great spectacle therefore has here been prepared for holy minds, which know how to draw from visible things the knowledge of invisible ones. Which know how to say to their Creator: *Thou hast made all things in wisdom, O Lord [n: (Psal. CIII)]*. *Thou hast delighted me, O Lord, in thy making, and in the works of thy hands [0138D] I shall rejoice. How magnified are thy works, O Lord, exceeding deep are thy thoughts become [n: (Psal. XCI)]*.



## HOMILY II. *On the proof of the vanity of all things under heaven: through the corruption of the elements, through the generation of things, the succession, and the forgetting of those things which have been.* (cont.)

For behold how all things transitory and subject to vanity are borne about in a circuit. And we know that a circle has no end. The things therefore which run in a circuit do indeed run, but they never arrive at an end. What rest, then, can be hoped for, where there can be no standing still? For where there is perpetual running, there is no standing. But where the way is a circuit, there is no place where the course could surely have an end. The things therefore which run in a circuit always run and never come to a standing. They always pass away, and never subsist. They are always being ended, and cannot find an end. When they have gone by, they are yet to come; when they have come upon us, [0139A] they do not subsist. This is the way of all mutable things, and the way of all who love mutable things and who follow mutable things. For of the wicked also it is said: *The wicked walk in a circuit [n: (Psal. XI)]*. And again: *The head of their circuit [n: (Psal. CXXXIX)]*, *and make them as a wheel, O Lord [n: (Psal. LXXXII)]*. The head too of all the wicked bears witness of himself, saying: *I have gone round about the earth, and walked through it [n: (Job I)]*. And of him the Apostle says: *He goeth about as a roaring lion, seeking whom he may devour [n: (I Petr. V)]*. You see therefore how the perverse always love the circuit, and do not withdraw from the circuit. Therefore the things which are perversely loved by the perverse, these themselves also all run in a circuit, for a testimony to them that they too follow a circuit. On the contrary the lover of eternal things says: *My foot hath stood in the straight way* [0139B] [n: (Psal. XXVII)]. And again: *Direct me in the path of thy commandments, for the same have I desired [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*. And again: *The path of the just is straight [n: (Isai. XXVI)]*. And elsewhere once more: *They shall go,* he says, *straight, the things sent forth; and the Lord our God shall lead them to a sure place [n: (Sap. V)]*. O straight way, O circuit, whither dost thou lead. And thou, whither dost thou lead? Thou leadest, and leadest through. But thou leadest, and leadest astray; for those whom thou leadest, thou destroyest, thou dost not lead through. O circuit, how dost thou enwrap, how dost thou embrace thy followers? To those who run through thee thou grantest neither a going out from thee, nor an arrival within thee. Therefore all things transitory and falling are rolled through circuits while they pass away, so that we may not seek in them the good of eternity — things which we always behold both so to arrive that they pass away, and so to pass away that they do not subsist. Let us see [0139C] therefore yet further these circuits of vanity. For the wisdom of God, as has already been said, has wonderfully provided this: that the motion of all things should be driven round in a circle, so that bodily nature — which by flowing away might at some time have felt failure — always by running back into its own origin, while without interruption it receives what it has poured out, might pour out without failure what it receives, and that there might be a failing without failure; nor might that ever be wanting which is able to fail, so that it may always be failing. Whence in one and the same thing, both that which it is, is wretched, and that which has been made is wonderful, because in the same work both the matter is found frail, and the craftsman's design is to be admired. And corruptible nature indeed persuades to contempt of the world, but the mind sinks down in wonder at the contemplation of the design. [0139D] To the beholding therefore of this design, after the course of mutable things has been unfolded, our Ecclesiastes turns himself, that in it he may exercise the mind's gaze through contemplation; because knowledge is not able sufficiently to comprehend it, he is eager to venerate it with worthy wonder. For, since he has unfolded the mutability of all things in three kinds of things — fire, air and water — according to the motion that moves, he shows thereafter how inexplicable that same mutability is according to the motion which is moved, through each several thing that rises and sets in the universe.

*All things are hard: man cannot explain them by speech: the eye is not sated with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing.* When any thing whatever is discerned on its surface, not yet is either its hidden cause or [0140A] its nature penetrated. For that which is, is seen; but of what sort that is which is seen, or what quality it keeps in the hidden place (whither bodily sense cannot come); why also it is so as it is seen, or whence it is so, or to what end it is — what man (I will not say in all things, but not even in any one thing whatever) is able to comprehend to the full? In how great darkness therefore is man enwrapped; by how great a blindness of ignorance is he hemmed in, whose sense can scarcely reach even so much as the surface of a few things? And even if he should discern all things that are according to their outward appearance, he would still not penetrate the hidden power of things and their invisible nature. Therefore the totality of things is in both ways incomprehensible to man: namely, both according to outward appearance, and according to inward [0140B] quality. But some individual things come outwardly in part by their appearance to the sense, others are hidden altogether — either because they are remote in place, or because, though present in place, they exceed by their own subtlety the slowness of human sense. Scarcely therefore does human sense suffice to attain some few things among things; but of the whole it comprehends nothing perfectly as it is. Therefore all things are hard: hard according to the totality, namely, in that they cannot be attained, and hard according to each several thing, because they can be fully comprehended by no sense nor understanding. And if they cannot be perfectly searched out by reason, much less can they be explained by speech. For this very thing is more unspeakable than all things in things: that in so great a confusion of mutability nowhere is an evident and manifest [0140C] design lacking to one who considers well; that even that which in things is not good is so well disposed, that in a certain manner the corruption of individual things is the beauty of the whole. Therefore human sense, sinking down in the consideration of these things, is sweetly refreshed out of its own failure; because in that which displeases according to corruption, in the most ordered beauty of the whole, even corruption itself is beautiful and pleases. Thus in a wondrous and unspeakable manner the Founder, in his creature, both punishes malice through the corruption of things and at once delights nature through beauty, so that man may recognize in his punishment what he has deserved through fault, and in his delight what he has lost. For how great an affluence of sweetness do we believe to be in the Creator of things, if [0140D] we find so wondrous a pleasantness in the beauty of creatures? Therefore, when he had spoken of the failure of human understanding in so manifold and perplexed and various a depth of things, he at once adds the delightfulness of nature in the beauty of those same things.

The eye is not sated with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing. Since all the bodily senses find in created things their own delights, and each of them as it were certain proper dainties prepared for it — sight, namely, appearance; hearing, the sweetness of melody; smell, odor; taste, savor; and touch, smoothness. All the rest indeed serve bodily necessity, or even pleasure. But these two senses, that is sight and hearing, draw their nourishment rather toward spiritual delightfulness. Whence their refreshment also has no draining off of dregs, [0141A] because together with the perceiving of sweetness they draw no mass of corruption. For which reason their refreshment does not arrive at a satiety of sweetness; because no difficulty running through delays their appetite from perceiving sweetness, nor does any ensuing trouble restrain it. This nourishment therefore, pure and unmixed, without all bodily bulk, passes wholly into the refreshment of the soul; because although it flows forth from bodily appearance and passes through the sense of the body, it cannot nevertheless be retained by the body, since it has nothing bodily, nor does it bring in anything corpulent. For this reason therefore sight and hearing, as being more excellent than the rest and having nothing of depressing weight, have obtained a loftier seat in the body, so that they may freely and without obstacle flow out to their delights, or [0141B] admit into themselves without difficulty those things that flow in. There are, to be sure, two things which enter to the mind from the appearance of things through sight. And two likewise which enter in through hearing from the perceiving of voices. For through sight there comes from things to the mind's knowledge substance and form. From voices through hearing, signification and melody. And substance from things works the same in the mind through sight as signification from voices does through hearing. And likewise form from things does the same in the mind through sight as melody from voices does through hearing. For there are two things by which the whole nature of the rational soul is ordered, namely knowledge and affection, that is, wisdom and love. And if the soul perfectly obtains these two and lawfully orders them, it is blessed. But if either it falls short in obtaining them so far as nature requires, [0141C] or perverts them, once obtained, against nature, then in that part, without doubt, in which it suffers either the failure or the confusion of them, it must needs be made wretched. The whole substance therefore of the rational soul is governed by these two, that is by knowledge and affection, so that through wisdom it may find truth, and through love may embrace virtue. Therefore, that outward things also might serve the rational soul for the sake of blessedness, these two instruments of the senses were placed in the human body, so that through them the notions of visible things might enter in to the soul, and might produce in it wisdom or virtue, if it were not there at all, or increase it, if it were there in less measure. Therefore the essence of things entering in to the soul through sight, and the signification of voices through hearing, beget knowledge. But the form of things entering in through sight, [0141D] and the melody of voices through hearing, kindle the mind to delightfulness. For as often as outwardly either sight is affected by the appearance of things, or hearing is soothed by the sweetness of voices, the mind awakes within with wondrous affections, answering to that by which it feels itself touched outwardly. And it comes to pass sometimes that, through the sweetness which it draws by bodily sense, it returns to the remembrance of invisible goods and, admonished in a certain manner from the likeness, begins to long with unspeakable desire for that whose shadow and image, as it were, it feels itself to have perceived in bodily affection.



## HOMILY II. *On the proof of the vanity of all things under heaven: through the corruption of the elements, through the generation of things, the succession, and the forgetting of those things which have been.* (cont.)

Let Ecclesiastes therefore say: The eye is not sated with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing. Because through these senses the beauty of visible things, while it affects the mind with delight in [0142A] the contemplation of itself, kindles in it an unspeakable longing for invisible goods. Which longing, namely, thirsting after an ineffable sweetness, can rather be provoked than filled out by the bodily sight or hearing by which it is aroused, however great a delightfulness they may bring; and by human speech, however profuse or eloquent it may have been — though it is scarcely told even thinly — it is never perfectly explained. For from the appearance of visible things the rational mind conceives only, as it were, certain seeds of joys; but as desire soon grows out into immensity, it travails and does not contain itself in so great a delight. Let no one therefore think the sight of visible things harmful to chaste minds; because, if to see the works of God were altogether harmful, the use of seeing would by no means [0142B] have been created by him. For the work of God is as it were his word, through which he speaks to us, and the eyes themselves are as it were instruments by which the words of God are perceived through contemplation. Just as therefore the ear is an instrument for perceiving the word of man, [d: so the eye is an instrument for perceiving the word of man,] so the eye is an instrument for perceiving the word of the Creator. Therefore fittingly in the human body both the eyes are set in front and the ears placed at the side, as though by this it were signified to us that our intention ought to be directed secondarily to our neighbor, principally to God. Rightly therefore, that it might be shown how from the consideration of visible things the desire of eternal things is born in the mind, the eyes and ears alone are mentioned. Because by these alone, well nigh, does the way of wisdom [0142C] and of virtue lie open to the mind, since, while beyond the other senses they perceive the beauty of visible things unmixed, they alone more purely kindle within the love of invisible goods. All things are hard: man cannot explain them by speech.

The eye is not sated with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing. It is a marvel. The human heart does not suffice for the whole world, and the whole world does not suffice for the human heart. Why does the human heart not suffice for the whole world? Because all things are hard, and man cannot explain them by speech. Why does the whole world not suffice for the human heart? Because the eye is not sated with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing. Therefore knowledge sinks down, affection transcends. Knowledge stretches itself as far as it can, and cannot take in the whole; but love drinks up the whole, [0142D] and still cannot be satisfied. Why is this? Because charity rises high above knowledge, nor does the human heart say: It is enough — until it has come to him, and has found him by whom it was made that it might be, and for whom it was made that in him it might be blessed. Every delightfulness, every pleasantness, every beauty of created things can affect the human heart; satisfy it they cannot, save that sweetness alone for which it was made. For the appearances of visible things are only, as it were, certain veins through which the invisible beauty, manifesting itself, flows out even to us. And therefore, when these naturally provoke our sense and affection toward themselves, they do not indeed fill up desire, but goad it to seek out the appearance of the Founder and to long for his beauty. [0143A] Therefore all these things which are seen outwardly were made for provoking human affection, not for satisfying it, so that, aroused by them, it may rise up and, increased, may transcend them. Wherefore the eye is not sated with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing. For that which we behold made beautiful does indeed delight, but it does not suffice, unless we find him who made it.

Or what he says can be understood thus: The eye is not sated with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing. For because he had set down before that all things are hard, and that man cannot explain them by speech — lest perhaps it should be thought that they could be comprehended even by knowledge, he added: The eye is not sated with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing. As if he were to say: It is no marvel if man cannot explain by speech the difficulty [0143B] [sic: *cultatem*] of things, who cannot even by sense take in or search out their depth. For whether he seek through himself the things to be found, or hear from another the things already found, everything that he can take in is beneath the satiety of fullness. For if he begins to contemplate through himself, the eye is not sated with seeing. If he seeks to be taught by another, the ear is not filled with hearing. For the narrownesses of the bodily instruments, if they pour themselves outward, do not suffice for the depth or the immensity of things to be unfolded. But if they pour themselves inward, they cannot fill the capacity of the human heart. For because the heart of man, blinded by the darkness of sin, has not an eye within by which it may fully see the light of truth, nor an ear by which it may perceive the word of God sounding inwardly, it is compelled to open bodily eyes and ears for the watering of the dryness [0143C] of its ignorance, that outwardly it may draw at least some drop of truth in the appearance of things, whereby its thirst, though it cannot be altogether quenched, may at least a little be tempered. For this is what the Lord reproaches to his people through the prophet, saying: *My people have done two sins. They have forsaken me, the fountain of living water, and have digged for themselves cisterns broken apart, that cannot hold waters [n: (Jer. II)]*. For we know that a cistern is dug for this reason, that water gathered from without may flow down into it, and be drawn from it again to pass over into the uses of men. But because this has no living vein, however great it may seem and overflowing with a gathering of waters, it can at some time be drained out [0143D] and dried up; because, when that which is poured in from elsewhere has been taken away and consumed, nothing of its own is left over to it whence it might be repaired. But the fountain which has a living vein, even if it is small, yet cannot altogether fail, nor does it ever feel a failure of its own outpouring, since to it an increase from its own comes always without failure. The bodily eyes and ears therefore, although they are the chief instruments by which the way is opened for the disciplines to the mind, yet, because the notions of visible things do not come from within from the fountain of truth, they lead them down to the heart of man through the narrow conduits of their own capacity and as it were like waters gathered here and there into a cistern. And because in the very things which they perceive they are able neither to comprehend the totality nor to penetrate the innermost parts, rightly is it now said: The eye is not satisfied [0144A] with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing. Because the human mind, whether by living or by hearing, even if it perceives something, however little, of the truth, is nevertheless driven far from the fullness thereof by the condition of its own infirmity. It is no marvel therefore if the dryness of human speech does not suffice to explain the difficulty of things, since human sense also sinks down in the consideration of it. And therefore all things are hard, and man cannot explain them by speech: the eye is not sated with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing.

*What is it that hath been? The very thing that is to be. What is it that hath been done? The very thing that is to be done. Nothing new under the sun; nor is any man able to say: Behold, this is new. For it hath already gone before in the ages which were before us.* Perhaps someone may reckon that that error is [0144B] here confirmed by which the philosophers of the Gentiles, concerning the revolution of the ages and the return of all things into the selfsame, attempted with marvelous madness to establish the eternity of times. For they said that the series of mutable things had run from eternity in the same order in which it is now beheld, and would run without end, by the same law, the world in its totality having neither beginning nor end, through certain ages succeeding one another. And that the great age consists of fifteen thousand years (which they call the year); and that when it is completed, all the heavenly bodies being called back to their own place whence each several one had gone forth from the beginning of the age, and again beginning their motion into the opening of another age by a like plan and manner, and likewise moving the nature subject to them by a like plan — that again absolutely the same [0144C] things all rise up anew, in the order in which they had gone before in the preceding ages, in the same order nonetheless, with the same essence, the same form, in altogether the same places and times, and accomplish their own course and a fate like to the former and the very same; that they suffer also the same law and undergo the same fortune, so that it befalls that the same men are born, that the same men likewise beget the same sons, live by the same fortune, die by the same lot: the same outcome of things, absolutely the same quality, and the same state and course of all things which the past times of the earlier age had held. And that such a revolution and repetition of all things into the selfsame always has been and always shall be without end, so that, just as by [0144D] the return of one star through each several year all these things are then renewed to their former state, so by the return of all the stars and of the whole of nature to their rising, through each several age and the great years, all things are restored to their first condition. But how great this error is, manifest reason will easily prove. For that times cannot be eternal is most evidently proved by this: that all time had a beginning; and it stands without contradiction that everything which had a beginning at some time was not. Further, all past time was at some time present. But all time which was at some time present, before it was present, was not yet. Otherwise, if it was always present, it was no longer time, but eternity. If therefore all past time at some time was [0145A] not, there was [a time] when there was no time. And so times could not be eternal; but before times there was an eternity without time, which times neither increased when they began, nor will they consume when they shall be ended. It is clear therefore that the assertion of those is false who have proclaimed the eternity of the ages, and have testified that the beginning of mutability was without beginning. But lest perhaps in this place an error of this kind should seem to be confirmed, when it is said: Nothing new under the sun; nor is any man able to say: Behold, this is new. For it hath already gone before in the ages which were before us — in what understanding this is to be taken he shows at once in the words that follow, saying:



## HOMILY II. *On the proof of the vanity of all things under heaven: through the corruption of the elements, through the generation of things, the succession, and the forgetting of those things which have been.* (cont.)

*There is no remembrance of former things; nor indeed shall there be any recollection of those which are to be hereafter, with them that [0145B] shall be in the last time.* For if the former things and the last were absolutely the same as the present, he would by no means call men now present other than the former and other than those to come in the last time; nor would he say, with them that are to be after us in the last time, but he would say, with us who are to be in the last time. Unless perhaps someone should say that the first and the last are to be referred to the beginning and the end of the same age; otherwise those are neither first nor last who have been without beginning and are to be without end. But that the first and the last should be referred to the beginning and the end of the same age is forbidden by what he says: It hath already gone before in the ages which were before us. Because, while he speaks of some ages of those going before and others of those coming after, he shows that he has by no means meant the first and the last of the same age. [0145C] Therefore, since that sentiment shrinks back not only from Catholic truth but from reason as well, let us refer this saying to the likeness of things, which have been so established in their kinds from the beginning that each several one extends its offspring according to the likeness and the first form of its own origin, and there is nothing which exceeds the limit of the first disposition in its own kind. Thus in the very elements of the world, thus in those things which have been or are procreated out of them, nature keeps in all things the first disposition, so that nothing can be found under the sun other than the first, that is, diverse or unlike. As, for example, from the beginning heaven above, and earth below. From the beginning the lights in heaven — sun, moon and stars; birds in the air, fishes in the water, beasts on the earth. From the beginning flight for birds, swimming [0145D] for fishes, walking for men, and for certain beasts crawling, and dragging for serpents. And all these things, as they were, so they are, so they remain. And if at any time perchance, by some miracle happening beside the accustomed course of nature, they become other than they always are, yet they do not become so in the whole, nor do they always become so, so that that first establishment, although it may sometimes seem to be interrupted, may never nevertheless seem to be dissolved. Therefore the wisdom of God rules this confusion also. And what is confused to us is not so to him. And therefore nothing is new under the sun; because that which passes away in time has been ordained from eternity by him who is above the sun. And therefore each several thing, because they cannot stand in themselves by immutability, yet keep their standing in their own kind [0146A] through likeness, so that although individual things cannot always be what they are, yet the whole are never found to be other than the individuals. Therefore nothing new under the sun. How much more above the sun? For therefore also nothing is new under the sun, because that which is above the sun is eternal. Under the sun and moon there is nothing new; nor yet is there anything eternal. For nothing under the sun is eternal, because everything that is under the sun passes away in itself; yet it is not new, because it subsists in the likeness of its own kind. Therefore under the sun nothing is new, and nothing eternal. For because it hath gone before in the ages which were before us, therefore it is not new; and because there is no remembrance of things past, therefore it is not eternal. And therefore, because everything that comes passes away and does not subsist, nor does anything remain [0146B] under the sun, the eye is not sated with seeing, nor is the ear filled with hearing; because, while it enters it slips away, and everything flees while it is thought to be held; and the sentiment is fulfilled: Vanity of vanities, and all is vanity. What then, since under the sun the eye is not sated with seeing, nor the ear filled with hearing? Let it go above the sun, where not only is there nothing new, but nothing transitory either; where there is both the remembrance of former things and the presence of things to come — nay rather, where all things past and to come are present; because nothing there is either past or future, where everything that is, is present, and everything that is, is there. There therefore both the eye can be sated with seeing and the ear filled with hearing: where a consummate and full perfection is perceived, and, the fullness having been perceived, eternity is not emptied out. So therefore this whole sentiment, by which it is said that under the sun there is neither anything new [0146C] nor anything eternal, seems to look to this: that man may recognize what he ought either to flee or to follow. For in this, that nothing under the sun is new, the eternal disposition is shown. But in this, that nothing under the sun can be abiding, it is demonstrated that in those things which are under the sun eternity is indeed signified, yet is not. In those very things therefore which are under the sun we recognize at once both: in that nothing is eternal, that what they are must be fled; and in that nothing is new, that what they are not must be followed. Wherefore let him run back to the highest sentiment, that, because in those things which are under the sun the eyes cannot be sated nor the ears filled by knowledge — since they do not comprehend the whole that is — nor can they be by desire, because, even if they should comprehend, the whole that is does not suffice, [0146D] nor does that which is present remain as long as they are able. They know that everything which is under the sun is vanity, and that man can have nothing more out of all his labor wherewith he labors under the sun.

Let him say then: All things are hard, so as to be comprehended by human sense — things which can neither be numbered in their multitude, nor comprehended in their quantity, nor searched out in their depth. And therefore it is no marvel if speech fails where understanding sinks down; and on this account man cannot explain them by speech, so as to render an account of each several thing, because he does not know all things: For the eye is not sated with seeing, when it considers by itself; nor is the ear filled with hearing, when it is taught by another — so that either the intention going out to the search of things might comprehend the whole, as much as is without, [0147A] or knowledge entering in might fill up the whole, as much as is within. Therefore the eye and the ear suffice neither for the whole world, so as to take in the whole, nor for the whole mind, so as to fill the whole. And yet through those things which are without they are admonished to go forward thither where life is, where they may find the whole and take in the whole, because all things *in him were life, and the life was the light of men [n: (Joan. I)]*. Thither therefore let them go forward, where at once they may find the whole and take in the whole and possess the whole, where in the eternal disposition those things have already been made which are to be in time. For what is it that hath been? For the very same, and not another, is to be in time than that which was before times, in the eternal disposition of God. Nor is any other mutable thing unfolded thereafter in the order of things than that which was ever fixed and abiding [0147B] in that ratified and unvarying divine disposition. And that it may manifestly appear that the subsequent effect of things accords with providence, therefore also in the very series of things the future does not disaccord with the past. For what is it that hath been done? The very thing that is to be done. Therefore what has always been in providence is at some time to be in fact; and what has already been done in fact is still to be done through likeness. But it seems to me that this ought not to be passed over, that he does not say. What is it that is to be, that he might then subjoin. The very thing that hath been — since past things are more certain than things to come, and because, even if they are not, yet they were at some time and were seen. But things to come never were.

And therefore that which is less certain would seem [0147C] rather to be put in the question; but in the answer, that which is agreed to be more known. But it is to be noted that the effect of things, according to cause, is later than providence; in our knowledge, earlier. Because until we behold a thing in its own act, we are utterly ignorant of what has been disposed concerning it in the eternal providence. And therefore rightly, when he asked, saying: What is it that hath been? (because providence in itself is hidden) he at once sends his hearers to the act of things for the understanding of it, saying: The very thing that is to be; because we cannot know what goes before in foreknowledge, until we have seen what follows after in the effect of things. But in the things themselves also we shall gather the quality of past things which we have not seen from the comparison of things to come [0147D] when they shall have come upon us. Let him say then: What is it that hath been? The very thing that is to be. Because, as has been said, that which goes before in providence — this same and not another follows after in the effect of things. And thereafter: What is it that hath been done? The very thing that is to be done. Because the same nature of things in each several kind has exhibited in act, in things past, the same likeness which it will bring forth they shall have come upon in things to come. And therefore there is nothing new in the kinds of things under the sun whose likeness has not gone before. Nor is any man able to say: This is new — as far as concerns the likeness of its own kind. For it hath already gone before in the ages which were before us, in its own kind, of which it is. And yet all things flow, nor does anything remain under the sun. And the things which are fixed in the disposition of him who orders [0148A] them, passing away in themselves, withdraw at once both from our presence and from our memory. For first they are withdrawn from our presence, so that they do not subsist by appearance; then they are also blotted out from memory by forgetfulness, so that they do not subsist even by recollection. And lest perchance the passing of things past should not suffice to draw the hard hearts of men to contempt of visible things, if, when those had been taken away, they who are present could have had confidence about their own standing. Therefore, after he said in recalling their passing: There is no remembrance of former things, he at once demonstrates the destruction of these also, saying: But neither of those who are to be after us. For if they are to be after us, they are surely others: it is plain that we who now subsist, when those shall have come who are to be after us, and we too taken away by the same [0148B] lot, shall then by no means come even into the recollection of the living. What then does it profit us that we have succeeded into this life those who were taken away by death, since we too, going down when our own time shall have come, shall leave other successors behind us. If therefore we wish to know of what sort we shall be with those who are to be after us, let us consider of what sort they now are with us who, long since taken away from this life, have gone before us. And that every hope of present things may be taken away from wicked minds, those also who are to be after us are said not to be about to remain, nor to be about to come into recollection with their own posterity — lest even this should come as a consolation to depraved minds, if he who cannot stand in himself might at least subsist in his heir or successor. Therefore [0148C] he shows one and the same destruction of all things past, present, and to come. There is no remembrance of former things; nor shall there be any recollection of those who are to be after us, with them that shall be in the last time — that he may subject all things to vanity, and turn the human mind from the love of those things which are seen under the sun to the desire of eternal things.



## HOMILY II. *On the proof of the vanity of all things under heaven: through the corruption of the elements, through the generation of things, the succession, and the forgetting of those things which have been.* (cont.)

But if anyone should not be willing to be content with the simple exposition, he has something else which he may understand in these words that have been expounded above, fittingly enough and perhaps more manifestly. For because he had said that neither is the eye sated with seeing nor the ear filled with hearing, he immediately adds the cause why neither can sight fill the eye nor hearing the ear: namely, because [0148D] the appearances of transitory things, coming indeed through the sense, arouse longing, but, swiftly fleeing away, cheat desire by their passing. For concerning this passing he subjoins the sentiment, saying: What is it that hath been? It already is not: for it has passed away, and now is not. The very same shall that also be which is to be: for it too, when it shall have come, will pass through, and when it shall have passed through, it will be no more. What is it that hath been done? That too has already passed through, and is not itself. Therefore the same shall that also be which is to be done; because it too, when it shall have been done, will pass through, and will be no more. But it can be asked why he mentioned past things only and things to come, when he rather intends to persuade to contempt of present things? But this very thing pertains to a great contempt of those things which [0149A] are seen: that he willed to mention in their number only those things which alone the sense of the flesh comprehends as present, as though he saw that those things alone are not, which alone seem to be — so that he cannot even say what it is that is. That which is, while it is, is well nigh nothing: for the moments of present things flee away with so hasty a course and so swift a slipping by that they cannot even wait for the words of those who speak about them. For even if it can sometimes be rightly said by the questioner, according to something, "it is," yet it is always more truly said by the answerer, "it was, and is not." Thus the question itself seeks an answer which as yet is not. But the answer, when it comes, does not find the question, which now is not. In a true consideration therefore he saw that this alone, as it were, is not, which alone seems to be. For since it at once both [0149B] begins to be out of that which is not yet, and ceases to be into that which now is not, that which is, is well nigh nothing. And therefore by no means ask: what is it that is? Because that very thing of the questioner, to the answerer however near at hand, has already been, and is not. Rightly therefore is it said: What is it that hath been? The very thing that is to be. What is it that hath been done? The very thing that is to be done. As if he were to say: From the consideration of things past weigh what you ought to expect concerning the things that come after; for both are the same. Both that which hath been, and that which is to be. That which hath been done, and that which is to be done. It is the same, not by essence, but by a like nature. For that which hath been (as far as concerns things) and that which hath been done (as far as concerns actions), both have passed through. And there is not now even that which hath been [0149C] done. Likewise that which is to be among things, and that which is to be done among actions, the whole will pass through, and there will be nothing perpetual under the sun. Whence is this? Because the condition of things past, and of those which are to be, is the same, and the nature alike. For nothing is new under the sun, nor is any man able to say: Behold, this is new. For it hath already gone before in the ages which were before us. And because the condition is alike, therefore as things past, so also things to come. And of what sort, thinkest thou, are those things past themselves? Hear of what sort: There is, he says, no remembrance of former things. Therefore not only has their presence been taken away by destruction, but their memory too has been blotted out by forgetfulness. A great ruin. Of such sort are also those things which are to be. For neither of those who are to be [0149D] after us shall there be any recollection with them that shall be in the last time. And the sentiment is true: Vanity of vanities, vanity of vanities, and all is vanity.



## HOMILY III. *How Ecclesiastes proves through his own works that all the works of men are vain, with an epilogue of the things aforesaid.*

*I Ecclesiastes was king of Israel in Jerusalem: and I proposed in my mind to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things that are done under the sun.* To search out the words of the wise and their riddles, and to draw forth to the taste that sweetness of wisdom which is hidden within, and to bring its beauty forth into the light — who is able? A scribe learned in the kingdom of heaven, *who bringeth forth out of his treasure new things and old [n: (Matth. XIII)]*, if he should come to us — behold, he who God gave wisdom [0150A] and understanding to lay it open and to make it manifest, that men may know that in it is life. But what are we? Darkness before light, that we should think to comprehend what God has hidden from the eyes of men, to reveal the secret of his wisdom to his friends. What do we do? Therefore we shall not meditate on his commandments, nor shall we search out her paths, and our heart will be able to rest until it find her. Only let us pursue her with these very words; and if perhaps the finding be deferred, let the search for her meanwhile feed us above all things; if we cannot [teach] her as found, yet let us teach her as sought. Behold, our Ecclesiastes, disputing thus far concerning the vanity of mutable things, has embraced in a brief discourse so many things that in those words of his this appears wonderful to us: in what manner [0150B] the whole, and the whole concerning the whole, has been so said that for brevity indeed nothing less, but for evidence nothing more, seems needful to be said. But wisdom did this, which is whole in the whole and whole in each several thing. Nor is she, whole in individual things, contracted; nor is she, whole in the whole, expanded; but she is as great in individual things as in the whole, and in the whole such as she is whole in individual things, because neither can unity be less than itself, nor immensity greater. That same wisdom therefore has expressed in this brief body of discourse the whole image of the universe, that we, being small, may see the great in the small, since in itself we cannot [see] the whole. And we have seen there the whole world, and have recognized that truly there is nothing of all things that abides, but that all things which are under the sun flow and pass through, and that the sentiment stands true, that all is vanity. Now therefore, [0150C] after he has demonstrated to us of what sort those things are which were made along with us, he passes on to teach us what we ought to think or to expect concerning those things which are done by us. Because if that also which God made is subject to vanity, it cannot be doubted that what man, who is vanity, makes is altogether vain, and much more vain. This is what he now enters upon to demonstrate: namely, that all the works of men which are done under the sun are vain, and that man has nothing more out of all his labor wherewith he labors under the sun. But that he may win credence for his assertion, he sets himself forth as an example of all the things he is about to say, asserting that he has learned by trial all that he speaks and has proved it true. And therefore he sets forth his own person as of such a kind [0150D] that it may not seem incredible that he could have had trial of all these things. Therefore he demonstrates his power and dignity: king of Israel in Jerusalem. He shows his wisdom also: And I went before in wisdom all who were before me in Jerusalem. He enumerates his riches: houses, vineyards, gardens, orchards, fishponds, menservants, maidservants, herds, flocks, gold, silver. Lastly, lest anything should be thought to have been lacking, he adds luxury also and pleasure: singing men, singing women, and the delights of the sons of men; and all things, he says, which my eyes desired I denied them not, nor did I keep back my heart from enjoying every pleasure of its own in those things which I had prepared. And he testifies that in all these he ran through every trial of things, and found nothing beyond vanity and [0151A] affliction of spirit and superfluous care in all things that are done under the sun. But what those things are which are done under the sun has already been said above. But that the discourse may pass over more suitably to those things which are to be said, we shall now briefly and summarily repeat what has been said.

For we have shown that by the sun time is signified, for this reason: because chiefly by the sun is all the mutability and vicissitude of times distinguished, so that the same is understood by that which is said, under the sun, as if it had been said, under time. Again, that it might appear more evidently what those things were which, done under time, the author reproves of vanity, we distinguished that some things were made with time, others made in time. Likewise of those which were made in time, [0151B] some were made in time, not under time; others in time and under time. And those indeed which were made under time are more subject to time and liable to mutability, because they began in time and will likewise be ended in time; and therefore, lest they should extend themselves in any degree to eternity, they are in a certain manner shut in by time, since their time both went before their beginning and follows after their end. Yet, lest vanity should altogether rule in these, and truth should not find under the sun where to lay its head [n: (Matth. VIII)], we have excepted certain of those things which are done under time; and if we do not altogether take vanity away from them, yet we forbid them to serve vanity. For of those things which are done under time, some are done for time, others are done for eternity. And those which are done under time for time [0151C] are vain in act and in fruit. But those which are done under time for eternity are in a certain manner vain in act, but in fruit are not; because, even if they pass away in the work, yet they remain in the recompense. Wherefore such things, even if they have not fully escaped vanity, yet serve not vanity but truth; nor is that which they are altogether vain in them, because that for which they are is not vanity. But those which are done under time and for time are altogether subject to vanity; because both that which is in them is vain, and that which is from them is likewise vanity. And these are the things of which we treat at present. In which, under the sun, nothing beyond vanity and affliction of spirit — either when they are being done, or when they have been done — is found by those who love transitory things.



## HOMILY IV. *On the vanity of things, and the worst occupation of men.*

[0151D]

And yet in these things vanity itself rises up to increase as though by certain steps, and corruption, growing out as it were from a little thing, increased by degrees, always proceeds to the worse. For in them is found the first origin of vanity, curiosity; then cupidity; lastly pleasure. Among which the first indeed pertains to the concupiscence of the eyes, the second to the ambition of the world, the third to the concupiscence of the flesh. Through which vanities, namely, the human mind, flowing down from the highest truth, is cast headlong so far that, pressed at last by carnal desires and wholly bound fast [0152A] by the enticements of pleasures, it not only cannot of itself remember its own origin, but cannot even when admonished will either that what evilly is should not be, or that what evilly is not should be. For what is, is evilly; and what ought to be, evilly is not. And that it wills either that what is should not be, or that what is not should be: this is worse than both. This therefore our Ecclesiastes here begins to demonstrate by the example of himself: namely, in what manner the human mind falls down through these steps of errors from the highest truth into vanity, and, its own vice driving it on, always goes to the worse. For to cleave to truth, and to love truth itself for its own sake, ought to have been its true good. When therefore it forsakes truth and turns away from her the mind's attention, then first, repulsed from her, it suffers the punishment of its own error: that [0152B] the very truth which it would not love in itself and for its own sake it now begins to seek for the sake of vanity; and because it has ceased to contemplate that one thing in which it might see all things, now, aiming not so much at truth as at novelty, it longs to know many things. For because it has lost that inward contemplation in which is the true refreshment of the mind, it seeks hungrily, as it were the food of understanding, in the knowledge of those things which are without. But because in all those things which appear outwardly a perfect knowledge of the truth can by no means be found, the wretch, languishing in the misery of his own failing, begins, as with a certain loathing, to run about always from some things to the desiring of others; and, casting away the former as empty and barren, he hopes that he can obtain the effect of his desire in those that remain — so foolish and blind that, though [0152C] he sees himself always frustrated in them, and never reaching the end of his desire, yet these things by no means ever cease to be courted as vain and unfruitful. But a just punishment nevertheless follows upon this so shameless desire and foolish curiosity, by which it stretches itself out superfluously in the knowledge of visible things, and either neglects the knowledge of its Creator — in which alone true blessedness consists — when it is had, or dissembles to seek it when it is not had; since in the very place where it perversely seeks delight, it finds nothing beyond grief and affliction of spirit. For in so great an occupation, finding a difficulty greater than its strength, it is oppressed as by a certain weight of its own anxiety; because, while desire drives it to seek wisdom in things of this kind, where it cannot be found, it is at once distracted by their multitude [0152D] and beaten back by their depth. This then is the labor and affliction of spirit by which the foolish and shameless mind is deservedly worn away for its own rashness, while, not only forgetting its Creator but also neglecting itself, it is occupied in searching out the lowest things, which do not pertain to salvation. And yet sometimes knowledge, sought with such vanity and found with so great labor in however small a part, puffs up the mind through pride, and casts it headlong from curiosity to elation, as from vanity to vanity. Which elation, namely, bringing in the ambition of the world, while it makes the mind greedy of praise, kindles it to the love of pomp and the glory of riches, so that, while it slips down from the glory of the inward good to the appetite of the outward, it is always [0153A] cast headlong into a worse vanity. But neither can corruption, once begun, stand still here. For as the goods of the flesh, while they are not yet had, flatter the greedy man by their appearance, so that they must needs be sought, so afterward, while they are possessed, they entice to pleasure one nervelessly broken and softened by use. And so the wretched mind, after the love of vain praise, after the ardor of a more vain cupidity, is lastly inflamed to the appetite of pleasure, so that now it begins even to sow vanity in the flesh, that afterward, made flesh out of flesh, it may reap the fruit of vanity in corruption. For this is the fruit of vanity: that the wretched mind, overwhelmed at last by carnal desires and lulled asleep by the pleasure of carnal desires, sleeps so deep that now it seeks wisdom not only not in truth, but not even in appearance. [0153B] And it pours itself out wholly to the trying of the evilly sweet pleasure of the flesh the more greedily, by how much the more, wearied within and repulsed from the intention of its own search, it despairs of finding the truth. And because, stretching above itself through search, it considers that it has sunk down under labor and excessive difficulty, it lets itself loose with all greediness to another consolation, and one that can be had without labor; but there too, finding vanity, it is not suffered to rest, because in the pleasure of the flesh it can neither keep hold of the fleeing delight when it is present, nor call it back when it has passed away. And thus, wearied and scattered on every side, it is shaken by the storm of its own vanity, so that through that very thing wherein it went away evilly delighted, it may at last, wholesomely afflicted, be compelled to return. And often there is a certain grievous struggle in the mind, so that, while [0153C] it considers itself at once delighted and afflicted in the lowest things, with great desire, for love of liberty, it longs to forsake even the things it has loved, if it could be done. This struggle therefore and tumult of the human mind through the various pursuits of mortal life Ecclesiastes will express in the words that follow, taking upon himself in a sentiment the case of all; and he will show in what manner it falls down from the vanity of curiosity to the vanity of pomp and of ambition, and thereafter to the vanity of pleasure, as is demonstrated in the sequence and order of the book. But since we have completed those things which were to be said in the preface, let us now return to expounding the words of his narrative.



## HOMILY V. *On the literal and moral exposition of the words of Ecclesiastes already spoken.*

[0153D]

*I Ecclesiastes was king of Israel in Jerusalem.* First this seems to me by no means to be passed over: that some judge that Solomon wrote this book in penitence, and that he therefore so studiously reproves in it the vanity of worldly things, and most of all of carnal pleasure, because he himself had learned all things by experience, corrupted by the luxury of delights and seduced by the blandishments of women. And that therefore he was unwilling to say: I am king of Israel in Jerusalem; because, the purple of the kingdom laid aside, he had already come down from his throne, and, despising the power which he had ill held, he was bewailing his guilt in the habit of penitence — and therefore he says: he was king in Jerusalem. And would that it stood established that this is true, and that we had as certain a testimony concerning [0154A] his penitence as we have an undoubted one concerning his fault! But now, since we cannot affirm doubtful things for certain, let us pursue those which are not doubtful. I Ecclesiastes. He sets himself forth, as we have said, as an example of all the things he is about to say, so that hearers may the sooner give credence to his words, in that they understand that he learned what he taught not by hearing only, but by experiencing. As if he were to say. I who teach you have made trial of all the things I say. For who could better know what vanity there is in the summit of earthly power? For I was king of Israel in Jerusalem. A loftiness great enough: to reign over so great a people and in so noble and famous a city, and to see oneself upon the throne of the kingdom, lord over all in power and set above all in honor. And to what end these things? [0154B] I was, he says. As though he should say: Now I am not; because even if I am, I now recognize that that very thing which I am is nothing. And how he came to this, that he should recognize himself to be nothing, he then pursues, saying:

*I proposed in my mind to seek and to search out prudently concerning all things which are done under the sun.* As though he should say: I, outwardly swollen with power and inwardly puffed up with knowledge, attempted to seek things higher than myself. But where by vainly presuming I swelled above myself, there by failing I truly recognized the direction of myself. I proposed in my mind to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun. A great purpose, if it could have come to effect; but how should the human mind suffice for so great things? Great pride therefore it is that mortal man should presume concerning so many things, and [0154C] again great curiosity to desire so many. I proposed, he says. See how all things sound of elation. He does not say, I thought; but, I proposed — whereas the temptation would at least have been venial and human if a swollen thought had touched the soul suddenly and in passing, as it is sometimes wont to do, and yet had not been received or approved by consent. But now the mind, not only lifted up but also blind, fell down into so great a depth of error that it not only thought to do that which was disordered, but even presumed that it could fulfil that which had been impossible. I proposed in my mind. A huge attempt. And what things? To seek, and to search out. To seek things unknown, and to search out things deep. How? Wisely. O foolish heart! [0154D] Therefore thou dost not seek wisdom, but professest only to seek by wisdom: for this thou hast said: I proposed in my mind to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun. If therefore thou seekest wisely, thou wilt seek by wisdom. And thou sayest: Just so; I will seek by wisdom. For I should seek ill, if I did not seek by wisdom. For if I should seek without wisdom, even if I sought wisdom, I should seek foolishly. But to seek wisdom foolishly — that very thing would now be not to be wise, but to be without sense. Wherefore I will seek wisely, and I will seek by wisdom, and I will seek wisdom. Tell me then the wisdom by which thou wilt seek: where wilt thou seek? and by what wisdom wilt thou seek wisdom. By which dost thou seek? Already, he says, I have the wisdom [0155A] by which I may seek; and therefore by the wisdom which I have I will seek; and the wisdom by which I may seek, I will not seek. What wilt thou seek? Concerning all things which are done under the sun. What is "concerning all things"? Why didst thou not say, all things which are done under the sun? Because, he says, I proposed something greater, and much greater. For the things which are done under the sun are all visible, and therefore, though it be a great thing, yet it is not the highest, to seek and to search out those things which can be comprehended by the eye. Therefore I will not seek those things, but I will seek concerning those things. To seek and to search out concerning all things which are done under the sun. Not those things which are seen, but, concerning those which are seen, those things which are not seen. Not those things which are done under the sun, which are seen; but, concerning those which are done under the sun, the reasons and causes of those things which are not seen. These things therefore I proposed: [0155B] to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun. Surely thou hast great wisdom. Who gave thee so great wisdom? Art thou a man, and wilt thou seek wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun? Whence hast thou such wisdom: God, thou sayest, gave it. Let us now see. For thou shalt not pass by unexamined. For we wish first to know whether thou canst render an account of thyself, thou who hast come with so great a purpose that thou thinkest to search out the reason of all the things which are done under the sun. Say then: in that wisdom by which thou proposest to search out the reason of all things, hast thou been able as yet to recognize at least thine own possibility: I, he says, unless I had hoped that I could bring the thing itself to effect — what I should believe altogether impossible, even if I thought to do it, yet I should by no means [0155C] propose that I would do it. O foolish heart! This is what I said: because thou hast not known thyself, therefore thou hast presumed to desire what was above thee — in the one blind, in the other swollen, in both foolish. Thou therefore wilt search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun. Search now, bestir thyself, give effort. Seek, and thou shalt find. What wilt thou find? Thou wilt find wisdom. What wisdom? That thou mayest know thine own folly. For thou shalt seek and shalt not find what thou seekest; thou shalt labor and shalt not profit, and vexation alone shall give understanding to the hearing.



## HOMILY V. *On the literal and moral exposition of the words of Ecclesiastes already spoken.* (cont.)

*For God hath given this worst occupation to the sons of men, that [they may be distended] they might be occupied therein.* Rightly and justly: that because they would not stand in truth, [0155D] they should, divided through multiplicity, be distended and occupied in vanity. Therefore, he says, I saw all things which are done under the sun, and behold, all is vanity and affliction of spirit. Thanks be to God. Now for the first time thou hast begun to be wise. Now I see that thou hast the wisdom by which thou canst seek and search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun. For wisely hast thou found, and wisely hast thou understood, that all is vanity and affliction of spirit. Yet I should like to know whence comes to thee this so sudden repentance. We expected something great, and that grand purpose had already made all men not attentive and expectant. For who would think that anything scanty could come forth out of so great a promise? To seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun. And how afterward: God hath given this worst occupation [0156A] to the sons of men, that they may be distended therein. What occupation dost thou mean? This, thou sayest: that they propose in their mind to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun. Why then dost thou call this the worst occupation: To seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun? Take away, he says, "wisely." For it would not be the worst occupation, wisely to seek and to search out concerning all things which are done under the sun. But who can do this? It is above the sense of man to search out the causes of all things and to comprehend the reason of all that is done under the sun. Therefore it can be proposed, but it cannot be done, to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun. For many things lie hid, and very many are hidden away, and all are reserved for hereafter; nor ought that to be searched out [0156B] which is not lawful to be done. Therefore what cannot wisely be done cannot wisely be proposed, even if it is proposed to be done wisely. I therefore foolishly proposed what I could not wisely do: to seek and to search out concerning all things which are done under the sun. Foolish on both sides — both when I proposed what I could not, and when I did what I ought not — with a third folly added: that I knew neither of the two. Yet I sought and searched out what I proposed to seek and to search out; I sought, and I did not find what I sought. Yet I found something: namely, that I could not do what I had believed; and there I recognized that God hath given this worst occupation to the sons of men, that they may be distended therein. Therefore it profited to have been foolish; but yet folly was not wisdom. Yet folly was given, that it might train [0156C] the sons of men unto wisdom. They have been left to themselves, that they may know themselves, and knowing may condemn and flee what they have become of themselves. This worst occupation therefore God hath given to the sons of men, that they may be distended therein. For because the mind of man would not stand in that one good in which it could happily rest, and without distraction or occupation possess the fullness of the highest truth, it is cast forth outside itself and scattered into the multiplicity of visible things; and the truth which, blinded within, it cannot draw from the fountain, it attempts at least to flee, with parched inward parts, as it were through certain rivulets of visible things. But the more it pours itself outward by intention toward visible things, so much the more does it grow dark within from the knowledge of invisible things; [0156D] because, while it is scattered more widely through outward things, it is the more blinded, being turned away from inward things. Whence fittingly Cain, when he beheld his own crime uncovered, and, reprobate by a greater crime, despaired of pardon for his crime, is read to have said to the Lord. *Behold, thou dost cast me out this day from the face of the earth! and from thy face I shall be hid: and I shall be a vagabond and a fugitive upon the earth [n: (Gen. IV)]*. For the reprobate mind, cast out from the face of the Lord, is hidden, while, scattering itself through outward things, it is so much the more covered from the sight of the inward light, by how much the more it is held always occupied in thinking upon those things which are without. Whence fittingly it is there said, a vagabond and a fugitive. A vagabond, namely, through disordered concupiscence, and a fugitive through a sinful conscience: while he divides himself in concupiscence through thought, and in conscience through despair [0157A] turns himself away, the sinner himself hides from himself the light of truth, that he may not be able to return. Therefore God hath given this worst occupation to the sons of men, that they may be distended therein. Why did he give it, that they may be distended therein? That they may stretch and distend themselves, may labor and fail, and may return, and he may heal them. For if they were not distended in their occupation, nor labored, that occupation itself would become exceedingly sweet, and they would not be willing to forsake it, nor would they long any more to return where truth is. Therefore God gave them the worst occupation, that they may be distended therein. Let us give thanks to his mercy, because he has made us labor in our evil, in our turning away; because he has hedged our way with thorns, that we may return and be converted to him, the husband and spouse and friend of our soul, because it was well with us then [0157B] more than now. But this we did not understand, and we thought it was well with us when it was ill. And therefore he himself sprinkled bitterness and wormwood, and gave gall into the cup, upon our evilly sweet turning-away, which flatters unto death, and upon the worst occupation. And he gave this worst occupation to the sons of men, that they may be distended therein. For occupation is the distraction and the binding of minds, which turns aside and scatters and ensnares souls, that they may not be strong enough to think upon those things which are of salvation — and therefore, occupation. Why the worst? Because there is another evil occupation also, but not the worst; and it is that which is busied about the necessities of mortal life, and is anxious about very many things; and that occupation is evil; and, to say something further, it is malice itself, so that of it that word is rightly understood: *Sufficient for the day is its own malice. [0157C] [n: (Matth. VI)]*. This occupation therefore is necessary to the misery of mortal life, to be swallowed up afterward by the glory of immortal life; and therefore it is evil unto misery, but not evil unto fault; since, if they have a punishment alien from blessedness, it has not a fault contrary to justice and truth. But the occupation of curiosity, by which the mind stretches itself out to unlawful things, and presses on to search and to investigate what it either does not befit it to know, or is harmful to it — that is the worst occupation; because, while it spreads itself beyond measure, either it takes hold of what it has set its affection upon, and puffs up the mind through empty knowledge, or, if it is not able to lay hold of it, it wearies the mind through delay and despair.



## HOMILY VI. *What it is to be distended in the worst occupation.*

[0157D] *This worst occupation therefore God hath given to the sons of men, that they may be distended therein.* What is, that they may be distended? That outwardly they swell, inwardly they become empty. For what is distended appears indeed greater without; but whence it is seen to have taken increase from without, thence it is truly proved to have lost solidity within. For while it strives to reach beyond itself into that which is not, it begins to fail in itself and to grow empty of that which is. So indeed the human mind, while through curiosity it is stretched toward searching out those things which are outside itself, is hindered by the very occupation of its own searching, so that it cannot consider itself. This worst occupation therefore God hath given to the sons of men, that they may be distended therein. O worst [0158A] occupation! O ruinous distension! whither dost thou drag the mind? How much dost thou promise, and how much dost thou take away? Thou promisest to man the whole of what he himself is not, and thou takest away the whole of what he himself is. What if thou didst give the whole that thou promisest, and take away the whole that thou takest away? *For what doth it profit a man if he gain the whole world, but suffer the loss of his own soul? [n: (Matth. VI.)]* How much more now, since falsely thou promisest the whole, and truly thou takest away the whole? Thou art worst, distending and scattering, afflicting and torturing minds; suggesting to those whom thou rulest that those things are always to be desired whose searching out is either harmful or vain, and whose finding is much more either useless or ruinous. If therefore thou wert sweet, and couldst possess minds without labor, who would ever detect thy malice? But now [0158B] the very labor and affliction which thou bringest upon minds make thee hateful, and what thou promisest is the more easily recognized to be vain, while what thou bringest in is felt to be hard. Thus thou trainest many unto wisdom, who then at last fully detect how great a vanity follows after thee, when they have begun to feel by experience how great a difficulty lies hid in thee for those who labor. Therefore he added and said: *I saw all things that are done under the sun, and behold all is vanity and affliction of spirit [n: (Eccle. I)]*. I saw, he says, where, didst thou see? Who taught thee? By doing, he says, I saw; by experiencing I came to know. What didst thou do? *I set before,* he says, *in my mind to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun [n: (Ibid.)]*. And behold, I had begun to seek and to search out, and I thought that in so laborious a study I should find some issue. I stretched out [0158C] therefore my mind as far as I could; I poured out my thought upon surveying all things. I took sleep away from my eyes, nor did I give rest to my heart night and day, until I might find what my soul was seeking, that I might find the reason concerning all things which are done under the sun. But what end could there have been in such attempts? I did and I failed, I strove and I was driven back, until my soul wasted away and my senses were darkened; since, in so great a multitude of things and in the depth of hidden truth, while the understanding began both to be scattered and to be beaten back, I was no longer able to bear the weight of labor and the straitness of an inextricable struggle. Then, returned into myself and as it were driven back from that dense and impassable pathlessness of hidden nature against which I was striving to press, I began to think that it is foolish [0158D] and vain that mortal man should seek with so obstinate a zeal the knowledge of those things which not only were made outside his nature, but also can neither add anything to his salvation if they be known, nor take anything away if they be not known.
