There therefore I saw that God hath given this worst occupation to the sons of men; that they may be distended therein, because all things which are done under the sun are all vanity and affliction of spirit. For because they have no fruit, they are vain; and because they afflict, they are harmful. For they do not help those who possess them, and they afflict those who seek them. For they cannot be sought without labor, but they can be possessed without profit. For the mind, as soon as it has been occupied with the care and desire of seeking and of finding them, and has poured itself out into them through the zeal of curiosity [0159A] and the solicitude of cupidity — to be afflicted with a punishment worthy of its own rashness — is both scattered by their multiplicity and beaten back by their difficulty. I saw therefore all things which are done under the sun, and behold all is vanity and affliction of spirit. I saw that this very thing also had been vain, that I proposed in my mind to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun. I saw that it is vain that the sons of men are occupied with the worst occupation, that they may be distended therein; and all this I saw when I had made trial of it, and I saw how great labor and affliction of spirit there was in this, that I proposed in my mind to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun. And I saw that all is vanity and affliction of spirit in all things which are done under the sun. These things therefore I saw. But why do not all the sons of men likewise [0159B] see the worst occupation which God hath given them, that they may be distended therein, so as to understand and see the vanity and affliction of spirit in all things which are done under the sun [sic: *sole*]? Why do they labor and not feel it; are afflicted and scattered; and are distended in the worst occupation, and do not understand their own vanity and the affliction of spirit in all things which are done under the sun? Because, he says, the perverse are corrected with difficulty. Of which let us speak more at large.



## HOMILY VII. *That the perverse are corrected with difficulty,* etc.

*The perverse are corrected with difficulty.* Therefore they cannot understand, because they are perverse; and therefore they labor and do not feel it, and they take delight in their own affliction *(Job [0159C] XXX)*, and think it is well with them while it is ill. For they are perverse to such a degree that the judgment of truth is not in them, thinking *good* to be what is *evil*, and what is *good* to be *evil, putting darkness light, and light darkness;* and esteeming *bitter sweet, and sweet bitter [n: (Isa. V)]*. For they reckon that there are delights beneath the senses; and therefore they love their own griefs, and thrust themselves willingly upon the worst occupation, and are distended therein; because, while with blinded mind they desire what falsely seems to delight, they do not attend to what truly tortures and afflicts. Therefore then they do not see their own vanity and the affliction of spirit in all things which are done under the sun, because they are perverse and are corrected with difficulty. And *the number of fools is infinite.* For they are not only turned away, but also [0159D] perverse. Turned away from God; in themselves perverse. And being turned away they are not easily converted, because being perverse they are corrected with difficulty. For if they were corrected in themselves, they would be converted through themselves. Because if they first received the judgment of truth in themselves, they would afterward see by their own judgment that vanity must be fled and truth followed. But now, being turned away, they do not see the light of truth; nor, being perverse, do they see by the light of truth; and therefore they are corrected with difficulty, because they know neither how to seek rightness and truth when absent, nor how to approve them when present. Therefore the perverse are corrected with difficulty, and the number of fools is infinite. For they would not be so many if they could easily be corrected. And in this it is manifestly shown how difficult the correction of the perverse is, that the multitude of fools [0160A] is infinite Now therefore the perverse and [sic: *stultitam*] are as hard to correction by their perversity as they are bold against truth by their multitude; and therefore the perverse are corrected with difficulty, and the number of fools is infinite. Wilt thou know, he says, how hard it is for the perverse to be corrected? Take me. I will this day become for thee an example of all. For I too was once perverse, and I myself did not understand my own perversity; but I thought myself to be upright, and to be acting rightly, when *I proposed in my mind to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun;* nor was there then any consideration of truth in me which might correct and restrain me, lest I should attempt to exceed my own measure, nor presume to desire those things which have not been given to men to know and to search out. I went down therefore into [0160B] the *worst occupation* which *God hath given to the sons of men, that* they may be distended *therein,* and I scattered my mind upon all things, and began to run through all things with thought, that I might seek and search out concerning all things which are done under the sun: but I was not able. And I began to labor and to be vehemently afflicted with weariness; and I saw that my mind, whithersoever it turned itself, was filled with grief and bitterness and anxiety, and the labor of seeking was immense, and the fruit of finding scanty. It came to pass therefore that at length the punishment itself and the great affliction made me prudent. *And I saw all things which are done under the sun, and behold all is vanity and affliction of spirit.* I knew therefore the truth, and I rejected vanity, because I felt the affliction. And I seemed already wholly corrected of my perversity; but there was still another entanglement; [0160C] and I did not know that the perverse are corrected with difficulty. I was then already corrected, so that I might know truth out of vanity, and I was not yet corrected so that I should not be proud of the knowledge of truth. One chaff of error had already been shaken off, and there still remained another, more tenacious and clinging, the chaff of elation. For the perverse are corrected with such difficulty that they either do not admit correction and purgation at all, or do not receive it to perfection. Therefore I had already begun to be corrected, but I was not yet perfectly corrected; and therefore I knew the labor of my presumption, but I did not know the use of my finding. I knew that it is a vain affliction to seek things not profitable to salvation; but I did not yet know that it is a vain elation [0160D] to be proud of things found.

Therefore *I spoke in my heart, saying: Behold, I am become great.* For I knew not that I had swollen with puffing up, and therefore I seemed great to myself, and I said in my heart: Behold, I am become great. In my heart I said: for out of the heart I swelled, and therefore in the heart I said it; because in the heart I saw whence I swelled, so that the elation might be there where the cause of the elation was. I spoke in my heart: Behold, I am become great. How great!

*I went before,* he says, *in wisdom all who were before me in Jerusalem.* Little by little one proceeds into corruption, and the depraved mind, as the swelling grows, is always cast headlong into the worse. First it thought of itself more than it ought; then it came even to the contempt of others. [0161A] I went before in wisdom all who had been before me in Jerusalem. Therefore then thou didst swell and wast puffed up, because thou didst go before in wisdom all who were before thee in Jerusalem. And how great a thing was this, that thou shouldst therefore lift up thy neck, because thou didst go before in wisdom all who were before thee in Jerusalem? Do not wonder, he says. Often pride rejoices more in singularity than in comparison. For elation does not always care to attend to what sort those are whom it goes before in the comparison; because sometimes this alone abounds for its exaltation, that among all who were before it in Jerusalem it recognizes none equal to itself. For this reason therefore I too, seeing that I went before in wisdom all who were before me in Jerusalem — even if I discerned that there had been few wise men before me in Jerusalem, [0161B] yet I gloried vehemently, considering myself superior to them in wisdom; since, although those before me, in comparison with whom I was made pre-eminent, were neither very many nor great, yet I saw that many wise men could come after me in Jerusalem, to whom I should become the first by the example of wisdom. That therefore I preferred myself in wisdom in comparison with those who went before looked also to this, that I gloried that I was established by a pre-eminent example into an authority for those to come, so that to those who went before I might be found the highest, but to those who came after the first author of wisdom. And therefore I spoke in my heart, saying: Behold, I am become great, and I went before in wisdom all who were before me in Jerusalem.

*And my mind hath contemplated many things wisely, and [0161C] I have learned.* Therefore thou wast already somewhat corrected, because thou didst not say, my mind hath contemplated all things wisely, as thou hadst above proposed *to seek and to search out wisely concerning all things which are done under the sun;* but thou didst bring in something more moderate, saying: My mind hath contemplated many things wisely — that thou mightest show thyself taught by the worst occupation, and to have seen the vanity and affliction of spirit, so as not to presume any further concerning all things; but yet not fully corrected, so as not to be proud concerning very many. By seeking all things therefore thou didst find very many, not all; and by failing toward all thou didst understand that man ought not to presume concerning all things; but by finding very many thou didst not consider that man ought not to be proud even concerning very many. Therefore in some respect thou wast corrected, and [0161D] in some respect uncorrected, when thou didst say: My mind hath contemplated many things wisely and I have learned; because, although they were many in comparison with few, yet those very things were few in comparison with all. But this looked rather to humility: that thou, who hadst already made trial of thine own failing with regard to all things, shouldst call the very many things thou hadst found not many in comparison with the few of others, but rather few in comparison with those things, all of them, which thou couldst not attain. But because the rashness of curiosity had already been repressed through its own failure and affliction in the searching out of all things, but the swelling of elation had grown in the contemplation of very many things, therefore thou didst still swell and say: Behold, I am become great, and I went before in wisdom all who were before me in Jerusalem, and my mind hath contemplated many things wisely [0162A] and I have learned. But, I ask thee, if thou didst go before in wisdom all who were before thee in Jerusalem, from whom didst thou learn, that thou shouldst say: My mind hath contemplated many things wisely and I have learned? If thou wast wiser than all, from whom couldst thou learn what thou thyself didst not know? Or wast thou therefore also wiser than all, because thou didst learn from all, and the wisdom of all was gathered in thee alone, so that thence thou mightest go before in wisdom, out of all men, each one severally — in saying which thou hadst all men as teachers, from each severally? Or perhaps thou didst not learn from men, but the trials of things taught thee what thy mind contemplated wisely, so that thence the contemplation of wisdom might become more certain to reason, in that the very outcome of things manifestly proved it to one making trial? For it does not seem to accord with thy character that thou shouldst learn from men, [0162B] thou who hadst been raised in esteem so far above men that thou didst think to seek and to search out not only many things wisely, but even all things which are done under the sun: therefore thou didst contemplate and learn, because thou didst think and prove, so that thou mightest demonstrate the force and power of thy understanding to be so much the greater, the more evidently thou didst bring forth all the arguments of thy reason to the visible trials of things. And perhaps this man willingly became a fool in his own wisdom, that he might teach fools wisdom. For he would not so diligently set forth to us the ways of his own error, unless in that same error of his he were thinking of our instruction. Therefore he still redoubles and commends that evil diligence of his, and subjoins how he obtained that wisdom of which he swelled, [0162C] saying:

*And I gave my heart that I might know prudence and doctrine, and errors and folly.* Which words can be joined to those above in two ways under the same sense; for, being about first to show this elation, he had thus begun. I spoke in my heart; and then he subjoins what he spoke. Behold, I am become great, and I went before in wisdom all who were before me in Jerusalem; and my mind hath contemplated many things wisely and I have learned; and afterward he brought in that which we have just now set forth. And I gave my heart that I might know prudence and doctrine, errors and folly. This whole series can be comprehended under one continuation, so that he is understood to have said all this in his heart: namely, that he was made great; and that [0162D] he went before in wisdom all who were before him in Jerusalem; and that his mind contemplated many things wisely and learned many things; and that he gave his heart that he might know prudence and doctrine, and errors and folly; and that after all these things, when he had thus spoken in his heart and had exalted himself concerning the searching out and the consideration of so great things, at last, coming to his senses, he recognized that this glorying also was vain, and on this account subjoins:



## HOMILY VII. *That the perverse are corrected with difficulty,* etc. (cont.)

*And I knew that in these also there was vanity and affliction of spirit: in that in much wisdom there is much indignation; and he who adds knowledge, adds also labor.* But if anyone wishes to distinguish the sentiment, and to divide off what we have brought forward last from the things said above, [0163A] he can draw it from what precedes in this way: that all the things which have been said above he spoke, as it were, in his heart — namely, that he was made great, that he surpassed in wisdom all who were before him in Jerusalem, that his mind contemplated many things wisely and learned them; and when he had said all these things in his heart, he then testifies that things higher still are to be investigated. As if he were saying: All these things which have been said above I spoke in my heart, and when I had spoken all these things together in my heart, then at last, taking confidence from the preceding contemplation, I gave my heart, that I might seek things higher still and investigate things deeper; namely, that I might know prudence and doctrine, and errors and folly. But that he says he gave his heart is to be understood thus: that he made it free [0163B] and unencumbered, so that it should not be divided among this thing or that, but should stretch itself wholly toward the consideration set before it. In this word he also expresses the type of pride and the swelling of elation: that he says he gave his heart to the contemplation of wisdom, as though the possibility of finding the truth lay ready at hand for him, if only the will to seek were not lacking. But to what end he gave his heart he sets forth, saying: That I might know prudence and doctrine, and errors and folly. Above he testified that he had contemplated and learned many things wisely; but now, after all these things, he disposes himself to investigate, that he may know prudence and doctrine, and errors and folly. Whence it is plain that the things above are to be referred to the nature of things, but these which he subjoins to the morals of men — using indeed a preposterous order, [0163C] since first his own life ought to have been corrected by circumspection of morals, and then study applied to the other things which lay outside his own nature, so that first the inner eyes of the heart might be cleansed from vices, and afterward opened in the contemplation of truth. But elation, which wished to appear great without, sought first the outward things, so that first, through outward knowledge, it might open a way for boasting, and then, by searching its own inward things as well, might cast them forth without for display. Whence rightly, when he had said that he had sought prudence, he added doctrine equally; because a mind greedy of glory and display wished by no means to have the prudence of circumspection for living well, but for teaching; and therefore it reckoned that the knowledge of prudence, without the knowledge of doctrine, would be useless to it and unfruitful. [0163D] But as for his confessing that he wished to know errors and folly also: by no means did he wish to know evils for this, that once known he might avoid them; but that he might both exalt himself over the knowledge of evils, and condemn others for their work. Now error is when evil is committed through ignorance, but folly is when evil that is known is not avoided. Therefore he wished to know both goods and evils alike, and along with the knowledge of both he coveted doctrine at the same time, so that all that the mind might comprehend through knowledge, it might be able to manifest for display through doctrine. But the perverse soul, finding everywhere the punishment of its own wickedness, fled back, on account of the labor and affliction of spirit, from all that it had inordinately desired for the sake of elation. [0164A] For because curiosity was driving him to inquiry and pride to display alike, it was fitting that labor should press him as swollen, and that occupation should scatter him as curious, so that at least in punishment he might recognize his own vice, and that, if not the love of virtue, then the pain of affliction might hold him within measure. Therefore above, being curious, he found occupation; but here, being elated, labor and affliction; since, while he seeks to know evils and goods not for edification but for elation, that very knowledge becomes for him a witness of his own depravity, so that the knowledge which in his elation he was seeking for display reproves the wicked man through his conscience. For he is compelled to despise himself from the very thing by which he strives to become great in the eyes of others; because while he seeks to know more of the truth that he may insinuate it to others, [0164B] he sees the more in himself, of his depravity, what he must condemn. Hence, therefore, the soul is indignant with its very self, and takes up a certain quarrel and wrestling against itself, because the foulness of the vices which pride despises, depravity defends. In this conflict of such a kind, therefore, great labor and indignation of pride is born; because, while it disdains to lie subject to the vices and strives to drive them from itself, it is overcome by its own depraved desires, so that it may not be able to accomplish what it wills. Thence, therefore, it now begins to abominate even the very knowledge of truth, so that, since on account of its infirmity it cannot take away what it hates in itself, it may at least be able, through ignorance, not to see it. Therefore, when he had said that he had found labor and affliction in his knowledge, and had set forth whence that same affliction and labor came, saying: In that [0164C] in much wisdom there is much indignation, and he who adds knowledge, adds also labor — he at once subjoins how, worn down and seized with accidie by that same affliction of spirit and labor and indignation, he overcame knowledge itself, and turned his soul away from the inquiry of wisdom, where he was afflicted, to delights and pleasure, saying:



## HOMILY VIII. *That man flees away from the truth: as Adam in paradise fled, and hid himself.*

*[n: (ECCLES. II.)] I said in my heart: I will go and abound in delights and enjoy good things.* Behold how the despairing mind casts itself wholly into pleasure, and now calls the blandishments of the flesh alone good things, because in them it finds no labor and affliction, reckoning as evil, as it were, [0164D] the inquiry into truth, in which it had earlier labored, distending itself through curiosity more than was right. But this estimation, in its turn, because it displays manifest folly, is the more quickly caught out; because the pleasure of the flesh, which seemed sweet to one desiring it, is at once rejected by one who has tried it. Whence he immediately adds, saying:

*And I saw that this also was vanity. Laughter I counted an error, and to joy I said: Why art thou deceived in vain?* But how great a vanity there is in the delights of the flesh, I remember that I said above. Whence the remaining things which follow are to be considered by us, because these too, although they seem plain in themselves, yet have perhaps something which diligent investigation may add. He says, therefore: Laughter I counted an error, and to joy I said: In vain art thou deceived? [0165A] It is clear to all that through laughter the gladness conceived within is in a certain fashion drawn outward, and that what lay hidden within of joy is published abroad — the bars of modesty being, as it were, broken — by a certain levity and incontinence of a slippery mind. And therefore laughter is rightly called an error; because when the mind pours its joy outward through unfitting gladness, going as it were to the left, it forsakes the straight way by which it ought to have gone, rejoicing, into God. But it is to be known that spiritual joy by no means dissolves the soul into laughter; but only that gladness which is conceived from the blandishment of the flesh is easily opened out also through a motion of the flesh. For because inordinate joy, in first entering the soul, broke the bars of continence, afterward, when it has begun to grow within, it easily bursts out without [0165B] modesty by the same way by which it slipped in. For a slippery mind pours out the perverse seed which it first conceives without modesty, bringing it to birth afterward, as it were, without chastity; nor can it hold itself back from casting out the gladness growing within, the bars of whose integrity were broken when it was conceiving it. First, therefore, it is deceived, when it takes in the allurements of the flattering world to its joy within; afterward it errs, when it pours out the joy conceived within, through laughter, into the wantonness of the world. It is to be noted that joy is only reproved, but laughter is altogether rejected; because laughter is in every way evil, while joy is not always evil, save when it is of evil; and therefore he rejects the one and chastises the other, so that both what is utterly evil may be guarded against, and what can be good may be corrected. [0165C] Therefore, he says: Laughter I counted an error, and to joy I said: Why art thou deceived in vain? It is deceived, therefore, when it takes delight in harmful things, and thinks it is well with it when it is ill. It is deceived also when it is wrapped up in the occupation of present delights, and is hindered, so that it may not be able to consider the evils to come.

But why in vain? It is deceived in vain, because the delights of the world can indeed themselves turn the heart away from the consideration of evils to come; but they cannot free it from the pressure of those that come upon it. It is deceived in vain also, because the joys of a deceptive good, in which it exults, are mingled with such great bitternesses that they ought rightly to displease, even if they were bound to remain. In vain, therefore, is it deceived on both sides; namely, both when it does not see the evil which it suffers, and when it does not foresee the evil [0165D] which it shall suffer; since both the reason is manifest which can judge the one for him who considers it well, and the experience is present which shows the other to him who suffers it. Therefore he adds and says:



## HOMILY VIII. *That man flees away from the truth: as Adam in paradise fled, and hid himself.* (cont.)

*I thought* therefore *in my heart to withdraw my flesh from wine, that I might carry over my mind to wisdom, and might shun folly, until I should see what was useful for the sons of men: with what deed there is need under the sun, in the number of the days of his life.* O human heart, where art thou? How hast thou come hither, that thou knowest not this — what is useful for the sons of men, with what deed there is need under the sun, in the number of the days of his life? Where art thou, that thou art ignorant of this? *Where art thou, Adam, where art thou? I heard,* he says, *thy voice, and I hid myself [n: (Gen. III)]*. O thou hidden one, from whom hast thou hidden thyself? O [0166A] hidden one, where art thou? How far off art thou, and how near art thou? How far art thou from him, and how near art thou to him? Therefore, where art thou? Behold, thy God seeks thee and cries: Where art thou? Thou turnest thyself away that thou mayest lie hid, and closest thine eyes that thou mayest not see. But he, seeing one who does not see — and therefore does not see, because he desires not to be seen — cries and says, on account of thy affection and not on account of any defect of his own: Adam, where art thou? He seeks thee, therefore, that thou mayest find him; and he seeks thee that thou too mayest seek him and say: Lord, where art thou? But thou, what dost thou do? For he, if he has lost, yet seeks; if he has lost, he has not lost the whole, because he sees what he has lost; and therefore he seeks what he has lost, because he sees what he has lost. For he would not seek unless he saw. But thou, what? Adam, where art thou? Thou hast lost, and the whole; thou hast lost, because thou hast both lost, [0166B] that thou mightest not have, and hast ceased to see, that thou mightest not seek. For if thou seest what thou hast not, thou understandest what thou art to seek. But now thou art far off and art exiled from the truth, so that thou canst not see whence thou hast come and whither thou must return. Therefore thou seekest what thou oughtest to seek; because thou knowest not what thou hast lost. Thou seekest what is useful for the sons of men: with what deed there is need under the sun, in the number of the days of his life! Oh! how thou oughtest to have known this! But now thou art hidden from the truth, lying hid under the shadow of thine own ignorance; because thou hast withdrawn far off and hast been unwilling to be with her, without whom thou canst not be. For because thou canst not be without her, therefore thou seekest; and because thou art turned away and hidden, therefore thou knowest not what thou oughtest to seek — though yet in this thou hast by no means been able to be hidden from her, so as not to understand that thou must seek, [0166C] since thou seest that this too is lacking to thee which thou art seeking. Understand, therefore, that at least in this thou art utterly not hidden; because she follows him who flees, and presses herself upon him who is turned away, and teaches that she must be sought by him to whom she does not offer herself to be seen. Although this very thing too, outside the truth, thou dost not see — that thou seest it must be sought by thee, that thou seest it is lacking, granted that what it is thou dost not see. Therefore in the truth thou seest that thou must seek what thou dost not see to be the truth. Why is this? Because thou art hidden. For if thou wert not hidden, thou wouldest see that nothing besides the truth must be sought; and the truth itself thou wouldest not seek, but wouldest have. Now thou art hidden, and thou dost not see; and yet thou art not forsaken, because thou art admonished to seek what thou dost not see. Therefore it is said to thee: Adam, where art thou? — that thou mayest return to the truth and find the truth. [0166D] Thou wert unwilling to stand in the truth; now return to the truth, because thou canst not stand outside the truth. A wanderer and a fugitive and unstable shalt thou be all the days in which thou art not with her and in her; nor shall thy heart find where it may rest, if it be unwilling to stand in her; because neither will it stand outside her so as to seek, nor will it find, besides her, anything in which to rest. Upward, downward, far off and near, wherever it may go, it will not find rest until it finds her. Why art thou in tumult, unhappy one? Thou laughest, thou weepest, thou art cherished, thou art afflicted: what is that to wisdom? For because in wantonness and pleasure thou hast been foolish, in affliction thou shalt be wretched. What more will they be able to confer upon thee? *Vanity of vanities: vanity of vanities, and all things vanity [n: (Eccle. I)]*. [0167A] But thou hadst not yet come to know these things. Therefore, by thine own sense and thine own wisdom — which in her sight is folly — puffed up rather than solid, thou wert trying to struggle on, and didst suppose that thou wouldest do something when thou wert nothing. Thou hast tried all things, and everywhere thou hast failed. Soft things and hard, smooth and rough, prosperous and adverse: outside the truth they can corrupt, they cannot amend. Therefore thou wavest and art tossed; thou fallest and art thrown down and art driven on; thou desirest and dost not obtain. Groping and not seeing, thou goest forward in vain against what is opposite, seeking the truth where it is not. And thou, where art thou? She is within, and thou art without; and therefore she cries to thee and says: Adam, where art thou? Thou seekest, that she may reprove thy ignorance; she walks, that she may show thy instability. When the Lord was walking about *in paradise at the hour after midday, he called and said: Adam, [0167B] where art thou? [n: (Gen. III.)]* He did not walk, but walked about. What is "he walked about"? Hither and thither, as if wandering and roving, not going straight forward, but proceeding wherever — this is to have walked about. And why did he wish to walk about thus? That he might show himself outwardly such as he (had begun to be) within. For already the truth had been moved, and was wavering, so as to withdraw from the heart of the sinner. Nay rather, the truth stood and the sinful conscience wavered; and therefore the truth walked about without, because within the sinner wavered away from the truth. Yet it walked about and did not depart, nor did it go off straight on, drawing far away as though irrevocable and never to return again; but circling near and walking about close by, it went away and did not go away, now going and now returning, and surging with a certain great uncertainty, as though unwilling to desert its own seat and yet not [0167C] able to endure a polluted dwelling. What doest thou, Adam? Why art thou silent? Call her as she departs, follow her as she flees; since she is still near and waits to see whether perchance she be called back, and therefore she makes delay and gently threatens her departure, grieving that she has been injured and violently cast out; and she signifies that she wishes to return, if by chance she be invited to come. Therefore she signifies that she has not gone quickly away, but is waiting still: that she will go, nevertheless; and now, because there is no one to call her back, that she will go; and behold, that she walks in order to depart — since there is no one mindful of love, who remembers charity, who has at heart the affection of fellowship. O unhappy Adam, where is thy remembrance? Where hast thou fixed thy soul, that thou endurest these things? O hard one, hardened and obdurate, could not so great kindness, so great a flame, so vast an ardor of love soften thee, that thou shouldest melt and run [0167D] after her? But thou, what doest thou? *I heard,* he says, *thy voice, and I hid myself.* Why? *Because I was naked [n: (ibid.)]*. Therefore thou fleest the truth, because thou lovest depravity. But she, what does she do? She follows him who flees, she who forsook him as he departed. She who was turned away from him who did wicked things calls back him who persists in iniquity. Adam, where art thou? I, he says, know where thou art; thou knowest not. Therefore, where art thou? Attend to where thou art, that thou mayest correct what thou hast been made, and return to him by whom thou wert made. Adam, where art thou? I heard, Lord, thy voice, and I hid myself. O hidden and not hidden, not seeing and yet seen, from whom hast thou hidden thyself? I heard the voice, and the face thou didst not see. Why? Because hidden. For this thou hast gained by hiding thyself, that thou shouldest not see him by whom thou wast seen. Whom indeed [0168A] thou wast able to flee, but wast not able to escape; because not even hidden didst thou lie concealed from him. Therefore, being sought, thou wast found, because lying hid thou wast not hidden. But thou, by thy malice, fleest again, and again fleest, and art found and fleest; and always thou fleest, and dost not escape. Held, thou fleest; sought, thou fleest; searched out, thou deniest. Therefore, being sought, thou art found; having denied, thou art convicted; convicted, thou art condemned. Let us see, therefore, now, lest perchance we have made so great a digression without cause. Let us see our Adam — that ancient one, and new still by a repeated malice. Let us see him renewing still his old pursuits, and, a deserter from the truth, seeking the shade and the hiding-places and the coverings of leaves; convicted on every side, never willing to be reproved. Not able to lie hid, and always fleeing. Let us see, therefore, what he does. [0168B] For what does he do? *He saw* the food and gazed upon it, since it is delightful to the sight and sweet *to eat [n: (ibid.)]*; and he opened his jaws and said: I will go, and I will abound in delights, and I will enjoy good things. And behold, behind his back wisdom and truth cried out and said: Adam, where art thou? But he, hearing her voice and convicted by the truth crying within, could not deny the truth, and said: I heard, Lord, thy voice; and I saw truly that this was vanity, and therefore laughter I counted an error, and to joy I said: Why art thou deceived in vain? Behold a man hearing the truth and confessing the truth. Who, think you, is this man? He seems altogether great, and drawing near to the truth, who thus professes the truth. Truly great, if he were not hidden. For he hears, and does not see the truth; because he himself is without through concupiscence, [0168C] but the truth is within through wisdom. And the truth wishes to win him, and calls him back to herself, and again and so again cries from within and says: Adam, where art thou? But he, feeling himself betrayed and convicted, when one approach is blocked up, seeks other hiding-places for flight; and, held in check from pleasure, pours himself out toward avarice For indeed he first sought after the fruit for eating; now he seeks the leaves for shade, and hides himself from the light of the truth the more deeply, the more wickedly he covers the vice of ambition under the pursuit of thrift. Therefore he adds and says: I thought in my heart to withdraw my flesh from wine. Why? That I might, he says, carry over my mind to wisdom, and might shun folly. Well hast thou done this. *For no man can serve two masters [n: (Matth. VI)]*. Therefore thou hast done well, in cutting away [0168D] from thee the cloudy and darkening conscience, the delight of the flesh, so that thou mightest be able to find the luminous delight of truth, having nothing of impurity. Well hast thou done in this, that thou didst propose to withdraw thy flesh from wine, that thou mightest the better make thy soul drunk with wisdom, and gather it inward to its very self: where wisdom shines, where truth is known and prudence is found. For this very thing too was to shun folly: not to pour oneself out after empty and vain things to be desired, but to conform to the truth the whole intention of the mind and all the endeavors of the heart, restraining them under the governance of reason. This, therefore, thou hast done well. But how long? Until, he says, I should see what was useful for the sons of men: with what deed there is need [0169A] under the sun, in the number of the days of his life. See therefore that thou persevere, that thou seek in the truth, and that thou seek perseveringly; because the truth is not found save by those who seek her in the truth. Who are they who seek her in the truth? Those who approach her with the whole heart. Those who do not come half and withdraw half, but come whole and come wholly: these seek the truth in the truth. But those who gather their heart in one place and scatter it in another — these do not approach whole, nor do they approach wholly, and therefore they do not find the truth; because what they seemed to have gained of the truth in one place, they lose in another. Who gather wages and put *them into a bag with holes [n: (Agg. I)]*. If thrift gathers and avarice scatters, what does it profit? What does it confer if, binding the gullet, thou hast gathered through continence of pleasure, [0169B] and, opening the eyes, hast scattered through ambition? Thou markest that thou hast entered; but that thou hast gone out, thou markest not. If thou enterest and goest out, thou art without; just as, if thou goest out and enterest, thou art within. But if thou art without, wherever thou art thou art not with the truth; because the truth is within. And what does it matter where thou art, if thou art not there where the truth is? From wherever thou comest and by whatever way thou enterest, if thou art within, thou art well; because thou art with the truth. And wherever thou goest, by whatever way thou goest out, if thou art without, thou art ill; because thou art not with the truth. If, therefore, thou seekest in the truth, seek whole, approach whole, enter whole; because the truth is within. Why dost thou seek so long, and dost not find what thou seekest? Because thou seekest ill, because thou seekest there where what thou seekest is not. For thou seekest without, and what thou seekest is within. Therefore thou seekest ill, and [0169C] therefore thou canst not find what thou seekest, and for that reason thou wilt not be able to find rest there, where art thou? because the truth is not there where thou art. Where art thou? Under the shade, under the leaves; because thou hast lost the fruit, and therefore appearance deceives thee and the truth is not in thee. Wherever he shall have gone, thou wilt be defrauded: shadow is the whole of what thou seest, and the truth lies hidden. What is the shadow? The appearance of visible things is the shadow, and thou hast poured out thy soul into them, and hast hidden thyself under the leaves that thou mightest lie hid. And how dost thou seek the light while entering the darkness: in the fruit thou hast beheld vanity, and in the leaves dost thou think the truth to be? If all earthly things are vain for enjoying: how are they not much more vain for the seeing? For if they do not confer the true good on those who enjoy them, what can they confer on those who possess [0169D] and do not enjoy? Why then, after the good purpose of continence and the zeal for seeking wisdom, art thou so quickly turned again to vain occupation? Why? Save because thy mind has been cast forth without, and made a wanderer and a fugitive upon the earth. And therefore it cannot stand, because it is not fixed in the truth. Therefore, thou sayest, *I made my works great,* etc.



## HOMILY IX. *On the various vain endeavors of Ecclesiastes.*

*I made my works great. What works? I built myself houses, I planted vineyards, I made gardens and orchards, and I planted them with trees of every kind, and I built myself pools of water, that I might water the wood of sprouting trees.* This is what I said. Thou seekest without [0170A] what thou hast lost within. Thou makest thy works great, because in thine own self thou hast been made less. Thou buildest houses, because thou hast been cast forth from the dwelling of thine own conscience. Thou plantest vineyards and makest gardens, because the shoots of wisdom in thy heart have withered. Thou buildest pools, thou who hast not the fountain of life leaping up within. Thou waterest the wood of sprouting trees, because thou seekest shade, the light of truth being lost. What hast thou to do with these delights, unhappy one, forgetful of thine own goods? Where is now what a little before thou didst propose — to withdraw thy flesh from wine — thou who now makest thy soul drunk with every wantonness and vanity? Is wisdom sought thus? Thou didst seem to have gone out thither; how hast thou returned so quickly? How? Save because thou hast walked in a circuit, and thy path was tangled, and thrift led thee back [0170B] where gluttony had laid thee low. Behold, again thou hast fallen into pleasure, and hast been made the captive of vanity. How hast thou come hither, save because thou didst hear his voice and didst hide thyself? For this is the shadow of vanity under which thou liest hid, that thou mayest not see the truth. How, therefore, hast thou come hither? Oughtest thou not to have sought wisdom? But thy mind, exiled far from the truth and ignorant of the way of truth — from the very point at which it restrained itself from the one vice of pleasure by continence of the gullet, from that same point it poured itself out the more licentiously into every wantonness and superstition through the concupiscence of the eyes; and it is now the more wickedly corrupted in the whole, the more it seemed corrected in some part, however small. For before, thou wast corrupted and wast humbled, since thou didst see and understand what thou wast suffering; now, under the appearance of virtues, thou hast been made [0170C] the prey of vices. Within, avarice under the cover of thrift; and while it persuades the mind not to desert temperance in the things it possesses, it makes it keep things that will bring no profit. Thence, riches being multiplied, pride follows, while what has been sought through ambition is possessed for elation. Thence all these portents of vanity have arisen: that the foolish mind reckons the true good is to be found outside, in the trials of things — not as though the true good were to be sought within by means of the things which are without, but as though it consisted in them. Therefore without modesty it boldly diffuses itself through all things; and that mind which had found affliction in the abstinence of the flesh, another approach having been found through the concupiscence of the eyes, feeds carnal affection the more manifoldly. For it does not suffice the wanton man [0170D] to take delight in the things which were made for human service and for enjoyment, unless also, by a pride transgressing the equality of condition, he lord it over men. For this is what he adds after the rest and among the rest of his superstitions, saying.

*I possessed menservants and maidservants, and had a great household.* He was made lord of men, who was the servant of vices. How much better would a Lord be for thee, and here a companion? For there, to be lord would be the uprightness of one who reigns. But here, to be lord is not the uprightness of one who resists, but the iniquity of one who oppresses and the profit of one who endures. But the creature of glory attends to the appearance outwardly; the disease within it does not attend to. It thinks that what is puffed up is great, and therefore rejoices to be seen great outwardly. [0171A] But it does not fear to be empty and void within. I possessed, he says, menservants and maidservants, and had a great household. What could a great household have done without great possession, and much money, and an abundance of means? Therefore he added and said:

*Herds also, and great flocks of sheep, beyond all who were before me in Jerusalem.* Behold abundance. Where is glory? *I heaped up for myself silver and gold, and the substance of kings and of provinces.* How great the glory. And what follows, save that a mind forward to boasting quickly grows soft through wantonness and is unnerved, and says:

*I made myself singers, men and women, and the delights of the sons of men, cups and small pitchers [urceos] for the service [0171B] of pouring wines: and I surpassed in riches all who were before me in Jerusalem. Wisdom also persevered with me.* Great confidence. Therefore thou hast sinned securely. Therefore thou hast confidently poured out thy heart into the delights of pleasure, and upon the allurements of the beauties and enjoyments of vanity. Why? Because wisdom had persevered with thee. For why shouldest thou seek her, set within, if she herself did not leave thee wandering without? Yet what is this wisdom which so loves runnings-about and disdains the secret place? See lest perchance the likeness deceive thee, and what seems to be wisdom be not wisdom. Beware lest thou be deceived in the shadow of the sons, where thy father lay hid, who could not see the truth. For a shadow makes darkness; and if it seems to have rest, it has not light. Beware therefore lest thou too, while [0171C] under the shadow, thou seekest the rest of leaves, begin to suffer gloom. Nor canst thou, set in the shadow, discern clearly; because the image which appears is shadow alone, not truth. Hold this shadow of leaves, therefore, in suspicion, lest thou be deceived. What are the leaves? The appearances of visible things are the leaves, which now indeed appear beautiful and green, but will fall suddenly when the whirlwind has gone forth. What are the leaves? Houses, vineyards, gardens, pools, the wood of sprouting trees, households, possessions, gold, silver, the substance of kings and of provinces; lyres, citharas, pipes, organs, cups and pitchers, and precious vessels riches and pomps, and glory: all these are leaves. Why leaves? Because vain, because fleeting, because transitory: therefore leaves. They are green indeed [0171D] for a little time, but quickly wither and fall. But yet, while they stand, they make shade and have their own refreshment; but it is a dark shadow, and hostile to light. To the flesh indeed it seems to afford refreshment for a time, but it makes the eyes grow dim. Therefore we ought to hold the shadow in suspicion, and not easily to believe the things which are seen in the shadow. For images deceive the eyes, because the shadow itself too is an image, not truth. Therefore I said that thou shouldest hold the shadow in suspicion, thou who confessest thyself set under the leaves. Under the leaves thou art, in the shadow thou art, and thou seest wisdom beside thee. See diligently lest perchance it be not wisdom, but something else lying hid under her appearance. For what wisdom is there in the shadow of leaves? For the shadow of the leaves is delight, and enjoyment in the appearance and beauty of transitory things. And [0172A] this has its own wisdom. For thus men call wisdom that by which this rest, and this tranquility of the flesh, is cunningly and craftily sought and prudently preserved. And this is the wisdom by which *the children of this world* and the children of darkness *are more prudent than the children of light in their generation [n: (Luc. XVI)]* — which perseveres with those who lie in the shadow of the leaves; and they cannot see the light of true wisdom, in whose sight this wisdom is folly. For because they look to the advantages of the flesh alone, they do not attend to the losses of the soul; and because they wish to be cautious where there is nothing to be feared, they become foolish and imprudent to thee, where the danger is grave and the loss intolerable. For what wisdom is it to cherish the flesh and to neglect the soul? What wisdom is it to flee with the whole intention that which is troublesome [0172B] to the flesh for a little time, and not to guard against that which is agreed to be always deadly to the soul? This is the wisdom in which this man glories, lying under the shadow of the leaves. O leaves, and shadow, of what sort is your fruit? See, Adam, of what sort are thy leaves, and of what sort is thy fruit? Thou hast hidden thyself, and thy leaves afforded a covert, that, covered, thou mightest not see the truth. Thou hast eaten, and thy fruit furnished food, that, corrupted by the eating, thou mightest lose immortality. Recognize, wretched one; recognize thy evil; recognize thy misery; recognize where thou art and what thou hast been made. Turn thyself from the shadow to the brightness of light, that thou mayest not only hear the words but also see the face of the truth.



## HOMILY X. *On the remaining vanities, as far as that place: « The fool walketh in darkness. »*

[0172C]

*And when I had turned myself to all the works which my hands had made, and to the labors in which I had sweated in vain, I saw in all things vanity and affliction of mind, and that nothing remains under the sun.* We have heard above with how great zeal this man toiled at gathering riches and increasing his goods; and then, on his own testimony, we have learned how he poured out his soul upon wantonness and upon the trying of pleasure, in those affluences of things which he had prepared; but now let us hear what he subjoins after all these things. When, he says, I had turned myself to all the works which my hands had made, and to the labors in which I had sweated in vain, I saw in [0172D] all things vanity, and affliction of mind, and that nothing remains under the sun. And so at the outset this presses a question upon us: how does this man now for the first time say that he has turned himself to seeing his own works, when hitherto he seems to have done nothing else than to have applied his intention and his zeal to these things? When he made his works great, built houses, planted vineyards, and made gardens and orchards, and built pools, had menservants and maidservants, herds and flocks, silver and gold, the substance of kings and of provinces, and the delights of the sons of men. When, in short, not only in deed but also in mind he had so plunged himself into the love of his own things and into pleasure that he reckoned this his own portion, and in a manner believed himself to have been made for nothing else than that [0173A] he might delight himself in all these things which he had prepared: did he not see what he was doing? But he saw the appearance; he did not foresee the corruption. He saw what those things seemed to be which stood, but he did not attend to what those had become which had perished. For the love of present things, while it turned the whole intention of the mind upon itself, and, having entered through the gaze of the flesh into the deceptive appearance of the things that were seen, occupied the eyes of the heart as well, at one and the same time both took away the memory of things past and hid the foresight of things to come. Whence it came about that, while he looked to those things alone which were seen, he could not recognize the truth in them either; and at the same time, in his own works, being turned toward them, he saw the appearance which was coveted, and being turned away, he did not recognize the vanity which was to be guarded against. But it sometimes happens that [0173B] temporal goods are the more quickly detected to be vain when they have begun to abound the more — goods which often, when they are not had, are reckoned to have been able to profit, had they been at hand. But when, once had, they cannot, even in their own affluence, drive out the poverty of the mind, then for the first time they make manifest to him who tries them how scant a fruit they confer for those who labor. Whence fittingly this man, after so great an affluence of all things and the use of pleasures, says that he turned himself, that he might understand his own vanity. Because when he began to learn by experience that the things he possessed could not make him happy, then he recognized that the things sought with empty labor had made him far more wretched. When, he says, I had turned myself to all the works which my hands had made — namely, looking back to what sort of fruit could have followed such great labors — then, he says, I found [0173C] that the fruit of my labors was not repaid me in an equal balance, and therefore I judged that I had labored in vain, looking back on the labors in which I had sweated in vain. For in all things there is vanity and affliction of mind. First affliction, afterward vanity; but also after vanity, affliction; and before vanity, affliction; and in vanity, affliction; and the whole is vanity, and the whole is affliction. For all things are vain which, even while they are, do not show forth to their lovers what they promise; and when they are thought to be held fast, they pass away. But it could perhaps have seemed tolerable, if there were vanity alone and there were not affliction. But as it is, there is both affliction before vanity, and affliction in vanity, and affliction after vanity, since the things that were possessed with love can neither be acquired without labor, nor kept without anxiety, [0173D] nor lost without grief. And therefore both the whole is vanity and the whole is affliction, because both in affliction there is vanity and in vanity affliction. And on this account, being turned, he rightly says: I saw in all things vanity and affliction of mind: and that nothing remains under the sun. For, instructed in his own affairs, he began to judge the affairs of others also; and what he had come to know in part, he truly pronounced of the whole, saying: Nothing remains under the sun. And from this it can be gathered how much, in other matters, whether toward good or toward evil, it avails a man to know what sort of man he himself has been to himself. For in some measure all things begin to be to you that which you yourself have been to yourself. If you do not see yourself, you see nothing well. If you are not deceived in the judgment of yourself, you are easily directed in the judgment of another. As here too, [0174A] being turned and directed, when he had subjoined concerning his own works: He saw in all things vanity and affliction of mind — at once, as one instructed in his own affairs, he begins to judge the affairs of others, and from the judgment of his own works he forms a sentiment about all things, saying: And that nothing remains under the sun. But because for perfection it does not suffice to reprove evils unless a man know also how to choose goods, he subjoined and said:



## HOMILY X. *On the remaining vanities, as far as that place: « The fool walketh in darkness. »* (cont.)

*I passed over to contemplate wisdom.* "Hebrew" is interpreted *one passing over*. This man, therefore, was made a Hebrew in order to contemplate wisdom. And indeed he himself was a Hebrew before too according to the flesh, but he was not a Hebrew according to the truth, until he began to pass over to contemplate wisdom. Just as one is called a Jew according to the flesh, and a Jew [0174B] according to the truth, so one is a Hebrew according to the flesh and another a Hebrew according to the truth. But he who is openly a Jew according to the flesh, and he who is openly a Hebrew according to the flesh, is not truly a Jew, and is not a true Hebrew. But he who in secret is a Jew according to the spirit, and according to the spirit is a Hebrew, is truly a Jew and truly a Hebrew. For many pass over according to the flesh and do not pass over according to the truth, because they do not pass over in order to arrive at the truth. They would more easily cross the seas and traverse far-off regions than reach the truth — and she is near. *Near is,* says Scripture, *the word in thy mouth [n: (Rom. X)]*; and the truth of the word is in the heart, and the word itself is truth, because the truth is the word. [0174C] And though the truth herself is so near, yet nothing is farther from those who love folly. But what is more foolish than always to look upon the lowest things, and to have countenances bent down to the earth? For this was given to the beasts, to whom it is granted to desire nothing beyond. But wisdom dwells in the things above; and to her they are unwilling to be lifted up, who are compared to the beasts and gaze upon the earth. This is that very folly out of which this man passed over to contemplate wisdom, when he despised the false goods which he had loved on earth, and lifted himself up to contemplate and to love the true goods which are above; because in all these things which are below under the sun he beheld vanity. Therefore, when he had seen in all things vanity and affliction of mind, and that nothing remains under [0174D] the sun, he passed over to contemplate wisdom above the sun. O how difficult a passage, and a way of how many days, for those who have weak steps! And I know not whether the whole life of a man can suffice to accomplish this journey. For who, living in this mortal life, will be able to lay hold of wisdom fully? For wisdom is drawn from hidden things and is found in hidden things; and wisdom always loves the secret place, nor does she offer herself to be contemplated save to those who enter in to her. But this is very grievous to weak souls: that they should leave what they have loved and forsake what is beloved, and pass over to the desiring of things which they do not know unless they be instructed by their own griefs, and that things which before were sweet should begin to be bitter; and thus in a certain manner many are drawn back, and are compelled to enter and to pass over to contemplate [0175A] wisdom. For when they have begun to taste and to understand the sweetness and pleasantness of wisdom, then they have no more need to be compelled, but follow willingly; and they gladly despise all things and leave all things, that they may pass over to contemplate wisdom; and then they arrive quickly, because they run swiftly and seek ardently, and wisdom begins to be near to them, because they themselves draw near to her and do not go far off from her into alien desires. So when Moses was in the desert a shepherd of animals, and had an Ethiopian woman for a wife, he did not yet know any fire but a consuming one; therefore he wondered that the bush was burning and was not burned up [n: (Exod. III)]. For as long as the soul feeds its own desires upon the lowest pleasures, and, bound by carnal concupiscence, has not yet come to the embraces [0175B] of wisdom, it is in a fire consuming and devouring, even to perdition. For the flame of lust and the love of the flesh consume those whom they kindle. But the love of wisdom has light, and has no consuming; and if it kindles the cold, yet it does not burn up those who are burning. Therefore Moses wondered how the bush was burning and was not burned up. For he too was burning, because he had an Ethiopian wife; nor was this a miracle to him, but to burn and not be burned up: what he had not experienced, this was a miracle to him. And the miracle itself drew him and urged him to pass over and see the greatest vision — how the bush was burning and was not burned up; and he began to run into a sweeter desire, and to forget the old longings where he was burning and was burned up, [0175C] and to loathe the love of his Ethiopian wife, because she was black and was not beautiful, nor like that Shunamite woman who warms old men and does not burn the young. For that fire, by which the bush was burning and was not burned up, did not signify the love of this Shunamite, that is, of wisdom; and therefore Moses, after he had passed over to see the vision, and had begun to feel how sweetly her flame burned, could no longer be held by the love of his Ethiopian wife, but, his heart kindled with a better fire, came even to converse with the Most High, and was sent for the salvation of the people. For in fire God spoke to him, and out of the fire he was heard, and through fire he was understood. And there was within a flame which had set his heart alight, by which, like the bush, he was burning and was not burned up. The mind itself had conceived God in fire, [0175D] and through fire it knew God. For unless it had burned, it would not have seen nor known; because fire itself is love, and love itself is knowledge. He knew, therefore, and loved; he saw and burned; he tasted and loved; and fire conquered fire, love overcame delight. The Ethiopian woman was despised, deformed and black, whom her own flame had darkened, and her very love made her not lovable. She was despised in comparison with the fairer one; nor did he wish to serve any longer for her, who recognized that with her he would always be a slave. For the carnal life and the concupiscence of the flesh is not from God the Father, but from the devil the father; and it consigns its own lovers to the servitude of its father and subjects them to his domination. And Moses serves and feeds [0176A] the cattle, and buys the love of a foul wife with base attendance; nor can he be free until God comes in fire and appears in love. For then he easily despises what he had loved wickedly, when he has begun to taste what he may desire more sweetly. And now Moses does not deign to serve for the Ethiopian woman; and although he does not cast her off afterward when she follows, yet he does not receive her into love. For, made a lover of wisdom, he ought so to have admitted the care of the flesh as not to subject himself by affection to it when it wantoned shamelessly, but yet to condescend by compassion to it when it was weak and compliant. For it is one thing to love for joy, another to endure for use. It is one thing to receive into the fellowship of love, and another to endure in the part of compassion. The life of the flesh is to be borne in necessity; wisdom [0176B] is to be joined to us in love. That one is to be sought after for her own sake; this one is to be fled for its own sake to be endured for our sake. Therefore Moses after the vision does not serve in Midian the father of the Ethiopian woman, nor does she any more bear sons, as one driven back from the bed, where the place of love and of affection is; and another fairer and more lovable having been received, from whose fellowship the face of Moses would be made not dark but luminous, whose love was shown in that fire, when the bush was burning and was not burned up. But Moses ran that he might pass over and contemplate the vision, because he had not yet conceived the fire of wisdom nor felt its sweetness. Therefore he wondered, and by that very wonder was drawn, so that drawing near he might grow warm and burn. And the fire itself, which enlightened him while absent, kindled him as he drew near. Therefore, drawing near, [0176C] he loosed his shoe, gladly renouncing now the carnal desires, the sweetness of that love having been tasted and its pleasantness recognized. For what could the concupiscence of the flesh have availed, compared with the love of wisdom? Therefore Moses hastened to run and to see the vision, and to pass over to contemplate wisdom. And it has been made manifest how he passed over, and how he arrived at contemplating wisdom. How much he saw in her, and how much he came to know of him, and how he was made a friend of wisdom, penetrating even into her depths and privy to her secrets. And how many are there from the beginning who wished thus to pass over to contemplate wisdom? First Abraham, that great father, went out from the fire of the Chaldeans, and passed over to contemplate [0176D] wisdom, and came into the land of vision which God showed him, and saw the brightness of wisdom and rejoiced. And Isaac had gone out to meditate, and passed over to contemplate wisdom; and God spoke with him. Jacob too, going on to contemplate wisdom, passed over as he came from Mesopotamia, and saw the Lord face to face, and his soul was made safe [n: (Gen. XXXII)]. After these, all the patriarchs and all the prophets passed over, and all wished to contemplate wisdom. And last of all wisdom herself came, and passed over, and her friends contemplated her; since she had come for this, that she might be seen, lest she should be sought in vain if she were never seen; and she said to those who had passed over, that they might contemplate [0177A] her. *Blessed are the eyes that see what you see. Many kings and many prophets have wished to see what you see, and have not seen; and to hear what you hear, and have not heard [n: (Matth. XIII)]*. This she said, and passed over, that after her those might pass over who desired to contemplate her, lest perchance she should now not be sought, if she were always seen. And many others followed after her, and very many passed through, and wisdom was made manifold. And many wished to pass over and could not arrive, because they did not hold the way. Others passed over and arrived. Others wished to pass over and could not pass through, because they thought to go by themselves. For the philosophers of the nations also, they too wished to pass over to contemplate wisdom, but they went astray and became vain, because they thought to lead [0177B] themselves. And yet they had begun to pass over, and had arrived as far as something, and came up to the way; but they did not enter the way, because they did not believe in Christ. Do you wish to know how far they had come? They were on earth in body, and they ascended in mind and passed over all earthly things, searching out the heavenly. Even to the moon and even to the sun, even to the stars of heaven, finally even to heaven itself and even to the firmament itself they ascended by their own wit, and arrived by their understanding. And what was there beyond, whither they could have gone? But wisdom is above all things, nor can she be found within all things, she through whom all things were made. He is none of these things, from whom is everything that is. But where could they have found that? It was not seen, and yet it was sought; nor could it lie hid so as not to speak, [0177C] nor be searched out so as to be found. Wisdom was within, and they were without; and they entered in mind after her, that they might pass over to contemplate wisdom. And they investigated and searched through wisely, and comprehended the secrets of nature, and found many hidden things; and none of these was wisdom, because all this was made through wisdom. Above all things, whither they ascended; within all things, whither they entered; and there was nothing beyond where they could have sought wisdom. And they fell and failed, searching out searchings; and it seemed to them that there was something divine above all things, and yet they did not know it nor understand it; but seeing, they grew the more dim, because with weak eyes they struck against a splendid light. And they began to reckon many false things concerning the true, and to liken God [0177D] by their own perverse heart, not to what he was, but to what seemed to them, who could not see what he was. But they reckoned that they saw well, and became vain in their own thoughts; and they were the worse blinded, and were found to wander the more deeply the more subtly they searched. But others thought that wisdom was to be sought more subtly by morals; and very many became despisers of their own goods, but not correctors of their own vices. And these too arrived as far as something, because they had the appearance of truth, but did not hold the truth. They cast away money, but retained malice. What was without they cleansed, and what was within they kept polluted. But this was not wisdom, because it was not truth, [0178A] but a false image alone. But they thought that they were walking along the road of the virtues; yet whither they were tending they did not know, because they did not know their own direction and end. And there were yet others who had certain virtues and were borne into them by a natural leading; and they passed over and arrived as far as something; and from a certain part of nature, by which she did not lie wholly subject to corruption, they saw the good and desired it. And it was given them to be capable of much in the pursuit of the virtues, and with an upright disposition inclined to goodness. Who does not know this — how many men of virtues they too had, and how great things, wonderful and worthy of imitation, they did: pursuing justice, cultivating piety, keeping chastity, confirmed in patience, steadfast in fidelity, circumspect in prudence — and what shall we say? Were not all these things [0178B] good? They were indeed, but of nature, not of grace; of creation, not of restoration: goods which nature had kept, lest she should be wholly corrupted; not goods which grace had given, that nature might be freed from corruption. Goods which grace had indeed given to nature when she was first created, and afterward had left to her when she was corrupted; but had not added over and above to her when she was to be purged from corruption. Goods by which the beauty of a nature well created might be proved; not by which the loftiness of a nature to be glorified might be acquired. All these, therefore, were goods for their own part, but they were not true goods, because they did not direct the mind by intention to the highest good. And by all these wisdom was not found, because she was not sought in truth; and she drew near neither to those, that she might enlighten their eyes to the truth, nor [0178C] to these, that she might direct their steps to virtue. And therefore neither could the blind find her, nor the lame arrive at her. But let this our Hebrew say what he has done, and how much he has profited by his passing over; since he too testifies that he passes over with the rest to contemplate wisdom, saying: I passed over to contemplate wisdom. This, therefore, is what you ought to consider. For he who passes over to wisdom leaves folly behind. And what is it that follows?



## HOMILY X. *On the remaining vanities, as far as that place: « The fool walketh in darkness. »* (cont.)

*Errors also, and folly.* If he passes over to wisdom who passes over to contemplate wisdom, how does he withdraw from folly, or leave error behind, who passes over to contemplate errors and folly? But wisdom is light; error, however, and folly are darkness. And he who is in darkness sees neither the darkness [0178D] nor the light. But he who is in the light sees both the darkness and the light, because all things that are reproved are reproved by the light. Since therefore he cannot see his own darkness, because he himself is still in darkness, but comes from the darkness to the light, that he may see through the light both the darkness and the light — not the darkness through darkness and the light through light, but darkness and light through the light alone. No one goes to darkness that he may see darkness, but comes to the light that he may see through the light not only light, but both darkness and light. He, therefore, who wishes to see his own darkness withdraws from it, lest he himself be in darkness and see nothing; and he passes over to the light, that through the light he may see darkness and light alike. Rightly therefore Ecclesiastes, when he passes [0179A] over to contemplate wisdom, comes to her — about to contemplate errors and folly also — as to a light, that in her he may see both the darkness and the light. That is, that in her he may see also that she is wisdom; and that through her he may see errors and folly, which is not wisdom. For he who sees wisdom sees that she is wisdom; and he who sees errors and folly sees that this is not wisdom; and yet he can see wisdom, and can see errors and folly, only through her — and she is wisdom. He, therefore, who passes over to contemplate errors and to contemplate folly passes over to wisdom, just as he who passes over to contemplate wisdom passes over to wisdom. Yet the one — that is, errors and folly — he contemplates in order to flee it; but to the other, that [0179B] is, to wisdom, he draws near in order to contemplate her; because neither of the two is wisdom, but to contemplate both is wisdom; and he who passes over to contemplate both passes over to nothing other than wisdom, nor does he leave behind anything other than errors and folly. Let him say, therefore: He passed over to contemplate wisdom, and errors and folly. What is error: not to know what is right. What is folly? To know and not to follow. What is wisdom? To know [sic: *cognocere*] and to love the good. But to know evil and to hate it — this too is wisdom, and the first wisdom; because to have hated evil is to have loved the good, and to have known evil is to have understood the right. Wherefore to know and to love the good is wisdom, and this same is to hate and to have known evil. And all this is known in wisdom, [0179C] when wisdom is seen, and what she is, that it may be loved; and what she is not, but is made manifest through her, that it may be hated. O how good this passing over is, and how happily he removes who removes to contemplate wisdom! What wonder is it if the God of the Hebrews is the Lord God, who makes such passers-over, and makes them pass over to such things? *Pass over to me,* says Wisdom, *all you who desire me, and be filled with my generations. For my spirit is sweet above honey, and my inheritance above honey and the honeycomb. They that eat me shall yet hunger, and they that drink me shall yet thirst; they that make me clear shall have eternal life [n: (Eccli. XXIV)]*. Since I give light of doctrine to all as it were before the dawn; and I will enlighten all [0179D] who hope in the Lord. For do you wish to know how wisdom enlightens those who have passed over to contemplate her? Question this very man, who passed over to contemplate wisdom; and consider how he has been enlightened from the time that he began to see her. For how blind he was before he had passed over to contemplate her, you have heard sufficiently above, when he thought to go where there was no way for man: to seek out and to investigate all things that were under the sun; and believed it to be something great to investigate the nature of those things which stand outside the nature of man — things which, even if they be known, so far as lies in them, cannot confer salvation, nor, if they be unknown, take it away. There, therefore, he was blind, in not seeing nor knowing in himself what he could, nor desiring for himself what he ought. But [0180A] now, enlightened by wisdom, attend to what he says.

*What is man, I say? What is he, I say?* I do not ask what he is as though admiring his dignity, but as knowing his infirmity. What is man? Man is nothing. Man is not anything such that he may be found sufficient for this. For what?

*That he may be able to follow the king, his maker.* For if he cannot attain the thing made, much more can he not follow the maker. If he cannot investigate what has been made, how can he comprehend him who made it? What is man, that he may be able to follow the king, his maker? His king and his maker. The very same who is king is maker. He is maker because he created; king, because he governs in that which he created, and directs it [0180B] to that for which he created it. For he created in a great good, and directs to the highest good. Who is he that follows his maker? He who lives as he was made. Who walks according to that for which he was instituted? He who keeps the good which he received when created, and guards unpolluted what nature conferred. This man follows his maker. And who is he that follows his king also? He who tends toward that to which he is directed; who hastens to arrive whither he is invited; who obeys the laws of one who presides well; who at the command of him who orders bends himself to all things with a spontaneous will. This man follows his king. But who is the man who can answer God? Who will be found sufficient to render a return to God? If he himself goes before, and so that you may follow: then one thing is his, another yours. Nay, if he himself begins the good and you complete it, what is less [0180C] is his, and what is greater is yours. If God goes before by beginning the good, and you follow after by perfecting it, you are now found not only equal to God, but greater. But perhaps he goes before by showing, so that you may follow by imitating. And where then is what is said: *His mercy shall go before me and shall follow after? [n: (Psal. LVIII.)]* Where is: *Of him, and through him, and in him are all things? [n: (Rom. XI.)]* Where is: *What hast thou that thou hast not received? And if thou hast received, why dost thou glory, as if thou hadst not received? [n: (II Cor. IV.)]* For if to show is his and to follow is yours, then you have something which you have not received; nor are all things of him and through him and in him, but certain things are man's which are not of him, if a man can of himself do some things which are not [0180D] through him. But wisdom does not enlighten for this, that a man should think thus; or that he should say that a man can have anything as it were from himself, who could not have even this from himself, that he is a man. What, then, does he say? What is man, that he may be able to follow the king, his maker? For how far do you think man is from God, that he should reach where he is? For what is it to reach where he is, unless it be to be what he is? And what is God? Or what is man? See how far apart they are. God is just and blessed; man unjust and wretched. See how greatly the just and the unjust, the wretched and the blessed, stand apart. But misery cannot be together with blessedness, nor can injustice arrive at blessedness. Justice is the way, blessedness the fatherland. But the servant of sin cannot be just, unless he has been freed from sin. See [0181A] therefore that man cannot follow the king, his maker, unless he be first visited by the king, his maker. Man cannot go to him; but he can, if he will, come to man. Therefore the king and maker of man came to man. He came to that which man was, and he himself was made what he was not. He was made man for man, taking on our misery, retaining his own justice. And there appeared between God and men, as the mediator of God and men, the man God: as God drawing near to men through misery, and as man not withdrawing from God through justice. Thus God descended to one, and man ascended to one; and in the one, two were found, the one from what is ours, the other from what is his: for there were, as it were, three things.
