God was just and blessed, and man was wretched and unjust; and because these could not come together, [0181B] the man God set himself in the midst, joining the wretched and the just, that through justice he might conquer misery, and might restore justice to blessedness. Therefore, when Emmanuel was with us through the justice which he had brought to us, and through the misery which he had taken up from us, he began to return whence he had come. That in man he might conquer misery through justice, and might call man back, with justice, through misery to blessedness. He held justice, therefore, and endured misery, until he should conquer misery and crown justice. And he ran, and went before us. *Suffering for us, and leaving us an example, that we may follow his steps [n: (I Petr. II)]*. Freeing us from the servitude of sin, that we too may have justice in misery, [0181C] and through misery, with justice, may pass over to blessedness. Promising us what he received, if we suffer what he endured; that we may reign with him, if we suffer for him. Thus, therefore, the incarnate Word, our maker and king, because we could not follow him in his own majesty, went before us in our humility, and out of what is ours established a way, that we might come to what is his. But not even so could man follow him, unless he were helped by him; because not even a just man would run through death to life, unless he who gave justice to the sinner should also give constancy to him who suffers. Rightly therefore he who had contemplated wisdom, and had recognized how utterly nothing man is by himself, said: What is man, that he should follow the king, his maker? For truly this man had passed over to contemplate wisdom, and [0181D] truly had been enlightened by wisdom: much more now, in being wise, truly recognizing the infirmity of man, than before, vainly stretching himself beyond the possibility of man. For there he was lifted up so as to fail; here he was humbled so as to advance. There he swelled outside himself through elation, so as to be emptied within himself; here through humility he gathered himself to himself, that he might be given to the One alone. And wisdom did all this after he had contemplated her, showing him in her own light first his darkness, and afterward her own light also — and the whole in her light and through her light. And the darkness was not light, nor was there darkness in the light; but through the light the darkness was seen, and was divided from the light. And he says:

*And I saw that wisdom excelled folly as much as light differs from darkness.* How was this seen? [0182A] Because wisdom enlightens, folly blinds. And whence can this be proved, that wisdom enlightens and folly blinds? Attend:



## HOMILY X. *On the remaining vanities, as far as that place: « The fool walketh in darkness. »* (cont.)

*The eyes of a wise man are in his head; the fool walketh in darkness.* Just now it has been proved that wisdom enlightens, folly blinds. In what has it been proved? Because the wise man has enlightened eyes, the fool sees nothing. For if the wise man has enlightened eyes, then wisdom enlightens. And if [sic: *sit*] the fool sees nothing, then folly blinds. For wisdom is light, and enlightens. If you have eyes where she reaches; but if you have eyes where the light is not, you have eyes, but see nothing. And what does this profit? Let your eyes therefore be where the light is, lest you be made as one not having eyes, if you have not the light. But where is the light? Above is [0182B] the light, where wisdom is. For wisdom is above, and light is above, and all light comes from above. And as below there is darkness without light, so above there is light without darkness. But in the middle, after light there is darkness, and after darkness light. Therefore there are days of heaven which darkness does not divide, nor does obscurity break in upon them. And there are days of earth upon which darkness follows. Because where wisdom always is, there is always light; and where wisdom is not always, there is as much light as there is wisdom. And where wisdom never is, there is never light. Therefore there is light where wisdom reaches, and where wisdom does not reach, there is no light. And where does wisdom not reach, if she reaches from end to end — what is left outside, where she cannot [0182C] reach, she who penetrates the whole and embraces the whole? *I alone have compassed the circuit of heaven, and have penetrated the depth of the abyss, and have walked in the waves of the sea [n: (Eccli. XXIV)]*: *reaching from end to end mightily, and ordering all things sweetly [n: (Sap. VIII)]*. It is a great question, therefore, what that may be where wisdom does not reach. Where the eyes of fools are, who walk in darkness and do not see. Let us therefore question another Scripture, where the eyes of fools are, who walk in darkness and do not see. For it says: The eyes of a wise man are in his head; but the eyes of fools are in the ends of the earth. Therefore in the ends of the earth there is darkness, and for that reason the fools who walk in darkness do not see, because they have their eyes in the ends of the earth. For if the light is there where he has his eyes, fools do not walk in darkness, but [0182D] have enlightened eyes and see. But if the fool walketh in darkness, and the eyes of fools are in the ends of the earth, then beyond doubt there is darkness there, where the eyes cannot see. And where is this? In the ends of the earth. Therefore the darkness is far off, and the light is near. Why then do we find darkness more easily than light? Perhaps because we are not far from the ends of the earth, and dwell beside the people of darkness, nor have we yet gone from the ends of the earth to hear the wisdom of Solomon [n: (III Reg. X)], where the light is: therefore we strike against the darkness and are dimmed away from the light. Yet the light itself is near and presses itself upon us; but bleary eyes love the dark, and have turned themselves into darkness. Therefore the light is near, but we are far off; and the darkness is far off, and we draw near to it.



## HOMILY XI. *What the ends of the earth are in which the eyes of fools are: and how wisdom reacheth from end to end mightily,* etc.

[0183A]

Yet where is the darkness, and where is the light? And which is the end? And the end where wisdom reaches. And which are the ends of the earth, where the eyes of fools are, who walk in darkness and do not see. And what is the head of the wise man, where his eyes are, who does not walk in darkness like the fool, but has enlightened eyes? Lest, therefore, we too perchance remain in darkness, let us seek out the light of wisdom, that we may understand where wisdom herself abides, and the division of light and darkness. Where light and darkness are bounded, and the limit of each. How far wisdom herself reaches, and what she is, and what is [0183B] outside her, and what is without her. First, because we have heard of two ends — the one from which and the other to which Wisdom extends herself, *reaching from end to end mightily, and ordering all things sweetly [n: (Sap. VIII)]*. And we have not yet come to know which these two ends are, and where they are, and whether these ends have an end, and whether beyond them there is anything else where wisdom does not reach. And if there is, how are they ends which do not arrive at an end? But if there is not, how are the ends of the earth, where the darkness is, contained within these ends, and how can there be darkness in the light, since Wisdom reaches from end to end mightily and orders all things sweetly. First, therefore, there are two ends of all things. The one is an end above which is nothing, and the other one below which is nothing. The end above which is nothing is the highest [0183C] of all. The end below which is nothing, the lowest. And so there are two ends of all things, the highest and the lowest. And whatever is in the universe besides the highest and the lowest is a middle between the highest and the lowest. And each single thing, the more it departs from the one, so much the more does it approach the other, whether from the highest to the lowest or from the lowest to the highest. Do you wish to know whether Scripture perchance somewhere mentions these ends more manifestly? *If I shall ascend,* says the Psalmist, *into heaven, thou art there; if I shall descend into hell, thou art present [n: (Psal. CXXXVIII)]*. Therefore he has established heaven and hell as the two ends of his own course. But nothing is higher than heaven, and nothing deeper than hell. And so heaven is the highest of all, hell the lowest — above [0183D] which is nothing and below which is nothing — and Wisdom reaches from end to end, and fills the whole and penetrates it. If I ascend into heaven, he says, thou art there; if I shall descend into hell, thou art present. He did not say, In heaven thou art present. Nor, In hell thou art there. But, if I shall ascend into heaven, thou art there; if I shall descend into hell, thou art present. Therefore in heaven he is, in hell he is present; abiding in heaven, coming into hell; in heaven he awaits those who come, in hell he follows those who flee. Those who ascend into heaven find him there; those who descend into hell do not escape him there. If I shall ascend into heaven, he says, thou art there; if I shall descend into hell, thou art present. And yet in both places thou art — both where thou art and where thou art present — reaching from end to end. From the end where thou art, to the end where thou art present, mightily, and ordering all things sweetly. Behold, [0184A] we have now distinguished the two ends to which wisdom reaches, the highest and the lowest, of which the one is as it were the place of wisdom where she abides, but the other is like a certain running-out and, so to speak, the goal of the stretching by which she extends herself. That end from which wisdom goes forth is the head, before which is nothing. But the end into which she runs out is the end beyond which is nothing. That end is both head and end. This end is not head, but only end. That end is head, because from it, and not above it; it is end, because up to it, and not beyond. But the other end is not head, because from it is nothing; yet it is an end, because whatever there is, is up to it, not beyond it. That end, therefore, which is above, is as it were a beginning and origin, above which is nothing. But the end which is below is as it were a goal and limit beyond which is nothing. For the origin and beginning [0184B] of all things is from the highest; but the limit and end is in the lowest. Wherefore the end below is an end, and only an end. But the end above is not only an end, but both head and end. If, therefore, heaven is above and hell below, and nothing higher than heaven and nothing deeper than hell can be found: heaven is the beginning, and hell the end. O how good a beginning, and how evil an end.

Therefore perhaps the eyes of the wise man are in his head, because, set in the middle, he looks back to his own beginning, that he may make his end there where he received his origin. For it is not good to be ended in the other end, where the end is one of consuming, not of consummation. Therefore man, who was created for eternity, alone among all animals has an upright countenance, that he may contemplate heaven [0184C] and may tend toward the end of consummation; and that he may reckon that not only is this visible heaven to be looked up to with bodily eyes, but that also with the mind and with the vision of the heart, lifting the inner eyes with a clearer and more piercing gaze above heaven even to the invisible things of heaven and the spiritual things of the virtues, he may climb up by contemplation, in the light of eternity, even to the end which has no end. That is the true heaven above every heaven, which is not only the heaven of heaven, but the heaven of heavens. Not second to the first, but third to the first and to the second. On this account Paul glories that he was caught up even to the third heaven [n: (II Cor. XII)], who had come in mind to the highest of all; and he had passed over not only the visible heaven, which human thought too can surpass, [0184D] but, having passed over also all those invisible things which were created above all this visible whole, he had been lifted up to behold the first beginning and the head of eternity. This is the third heaven. For the first heaven is the visible heaven. The second heaven is the invisible heaven of the visible heaven. The third heaven is the invisible heaven of the visible heaven and of the invisible heaven. The first heaven is only heaven, and the heaven of the earth. The second heaven, the heaven of heaven. The third heaven, the heaven of heavens. And this heaven has no heaven above itself. The first heaven is the supreme state of creation. The second heaven is the advancement of virtue above creation. The third heaven is the sublimity of contemplation. The first hell is the lowest state of creation. The second hell is [0185A] the failing of iniquity below creation. The third hell is the depth of damnation. The first heaven and the first hell, end and end. The second heaven and the second hell, end and end. The third heaven and the third hell, end and end. And everywhere Wisdom: She reaches from end to end mightily, and orders all things sweetly. From the first heaven to the first hell is the first course of wisdom. For beginning from the highest downward, and descending from the topmost even to the bottom of things, she accompanies and cherishes all things which she has wrought, so that the things which have been made and created by her may not subsist without her. But from the topmost even to the lowest, among all the things that subsist, each single thing, the more it degenerates from the perfect beauty and consummate comeliness of the highest things, [0185B] so much the more, as if fleeing, does it in a certain manner go far off from wisdom. But Wisdom herself follows the flight of things from end to end; because, deserting nothing wholly among all the things which she made, and tempering each single thing from the highest to the lowest with a comeliness proper and fitting to its own kind, she testifies that she is present to these also. For she herself, the very same who disposed the most excellent of creatures in a highest and supereminent beauty, fitting the lower things also in due sequence and following them up with her disposition, leaves nothing disordered even to the bottom of things. And so Wisdom reaches to the end, even to those things which in all things are lowest; because in these too something of her is found, which through the infirmity of their creation and the defect of their nature have withdrawn far from the highest. But yet [0185C] Wisdom herself abides in the highest things as it were by remaining, but reaches the lowest as it were by running out; because those things, subsisting unchangeably in their own perfect comeliness, nowhere flow away from her likeness. But these, fleeing through defect, yet by that very thing which they retain of beauty in their own kind, show that wisdom is present to them also. Wisdom therefore reaches everywhere, because in everything that is there is something beautiful, and everything that is beautiful is the work of wisdom. But what, in that which is, is not beautiful, is not the work of Wisdom; because what is beautiful in everything that is, she herself made; but what is not beautiful, she did not make, but permitted in that which she made beautiful. Why then did she not make the whole beautiful in that which she made; and why did she not bring it about that what is not beautiful [0185D] should nowhere be in that which she made? Perhaps she had been wearied in making those former things, in which she had wrought a certain highest and perfect beauty. And therefore she pursued the rest afterward as she could, bringing it about indeed that the whole should be beautiful which she made in them, but not bringing it about that what is not beautiful should not be in them — which she did not make, but permitted; because she could not bring it about that it should not be at all. And so thereafter, descending to one thing after another and making one thing after another, she conferred always so much the less of beauty and comeliness upon the later of her works, as she the more, in making the former, consumed the strength of her might, by which she could do more, and the force and ingenuity of her prudence, by which she might know more. Therefore the order [0186A] of things degenerated downward, descending to the lowest. And unless wisdom had looked out for herself, so as at last to set in these an end of making, there was perhaps nothing further of beauty which she could have conferred on the things to be made.



## HOMILY XI. *What the ends of the earth are in which the eyes of fools are: and how wisdom reaches from end to end mightily,* etc. (cont.)

What do we say? Was the wisdom of God, then, diminished in the making? and did it pursue its work feebly to the end? And where is that which reaches from end to end mightily? If Wisdom mightily reaches from end to end, then the difference of beauty in the creation is not from a failing in the maker. Why then are not all things equally beautiful, unless it be that all things together may be more beautiful? For if individual things were not beautiful in differing degrees, all things together would not be incomparably beautiful; because the highest beauty would be in individual things — whatever one there were would be surpassing that one, and more eminent than all the rest — [0186B] not every beauty would be in all things together. Therefore Wisdom mightily reaches from end to end; because she so pursues her works down to the very bottom of things that she even orders the defect of lower things toward the beauty of the whole, so that the universe is the more beautiful for this, that in the universe certain things suffer defect by comparison with others and in respect of them. For this very thing, which in certain of her beautiful works seems not beautiful, is nevertheless not ugly where it is; but it has less of beauty in the part, because in the whole it is beautiful for that to which it is ordered. Therefore in no part is there ugliness, but nevertheless in certain parts there is greater and in certain parts lesser beauty; because the beauty in the whole of the parts is greatest [0186C] for this reason, that in the parts it is differing, and manifoldly varied and distinguished in every way. Then follows, and he says: Ordering all things sweetly. For she orders all things sweetly, because in kinds of things so many and so diverse, advancing each single one [sic: *promonens*] to its own term, she permits nothing to disturb the concord and peace of the universe; but she so assigns to each what is its own that she leads things running unlike to one end, and so that the working of one does not hinder the force and effect of another. O Wisdom, how widely thou art poured out, and yet thou art not scattered! How far into the distance thou stretchest thyself, and sufferest no failing! How great is thy course, from the highest even to the lowest! By as much as heaven is distant from earth, and hell [0186D] beneath the earth where the end of the earth is, and beyond there is no earth; by as much as the highest is distant from the lowest, so much does Wisdom penetrate through. And yet this way of Wisdom is short, and her stretching-forth is compendious, and she counts all that is a small thing for her to pass over and to lay hold of and to penetrate; nor is anything far off that is stretched out in space, if it be near in goodness. But iniquity is far more distant from justice, and farther than the two, heaven from earth, and hell beneath the earth. For justice is higher than heaven, and not only than heaven, but even than those who are in heaven. The angels are in heaven, and from the beginning they are in heaven; and from thence they ascended, through justice, above that which they had been made. And justice is higher than heaven, iniquity deeper than hell. For it is deeper than everything that is lowest in creation, because that which has fallen through iniquity [0187A] is beneath every creature; nor can anything be deeper than that than which nothing is worse. By as much, therefore, as he ascends who ascends into heaven, by so much does he ascend who advances toward justice. And by as much as he descends who descends to hell, by so much does he descend who falls to iniquity. And by as much as he ascends who ascends from hell to heaven, by so much does he ascend who returns from iniquity to justice. But by as much as he descends who descends from heaven to hell, by so much does he descend who rushes down from justice to iniquity.

And in all these things *they ascend even to the heavens, and they descend even to the depths, and their soul amid evils* wastes away [n: (Psal. CVI)]. Therefore heaven belongs to justice, and hell to iniquity; and the motions of souls are snatched along [0187B] by an alternating surge without ceasing, as though driven by contrary blasts, ascending and descending; and there come to be liftings up, and pressings down, and great commotions in that invisible sea of billowing desires. The good spirit comes, and drives upward. The evil spirit comes, and drives downward: and they ascend even to the heavens, and they descend even from the depths, their soul wasting away in the uncertainty of its own evils. And what does he say? If I shall ascend into heaven, thou art there; if I shall descend to hell, thou art at hand. For if you ascend into the heaven of justice, you have there with you wisdom holding you fast lest you fall; but if you descend to the hell of guilt, wisdom is there laying hold of you lest you escape. Above she has her right hand, and below [0187C] her left, reaching from end to end mightily, and ordering all things sweetly. Above she is strong, below she is strong, everywhere unconquerable. Above she is strong, so as to keep from evil those who trust in her, and to cast headlong into evil, by forsaking them, those who presume upon themselves. Below she is strong, so as to condemn for evil those who despise, and to justify from evil those who repent. Behold how many she has kept safe at the summit, how many she has cast down from the summit, how many she has raised from the depth, how many she has forsaken in the depth. Manifest are her judgments: and he who knows himself knows how Wisdom works all these things daily in the secret of the human heart, always presiding at judgment: and by hidden requital and invisible dispensation, weighing merits, now she takes up through grace, now she forsakes through justice, now through gentleness [0187D] she spares, now through severity she punishes. And when she has forsaken she takes up again, and when she has taken up she again abandons, that a man may not know his own end, and may walk anxiously all the time of his life, so that those who lie prostrate may not despair, and those who stand may not presume. If they are in the heaven of justice, let them fear a fall. If in the hell of guilt, let them seek mercy. Therefore justice herself first descended to hell; afterward she ascended to heaven, that in descending she might give hope to those to be set free, and in ascending might show the way to those to be glorified. Wisdom, then, reaches from end to end mightily, and orders all things sweetly. For she comprehends the end of both — of the good, namely, which is in her; and equally of the evil, which is not in her. But in that which is from her it flows away from her, yet not altogether without [0188A] her, because what nature does not beget out of her own, she restrains by power within her own. For evil has no proper seat of its own, but by nature a stranger to what is its own, it lodges in what belongs to another, so that it is plain that it is not in its own; because if there were not good things in which evils were, evils would not be at all. For since evil is nothing other than the corruption of a good, and everything that is corrupted is good, that from which it falls away is one thing, that in which it subsists another. And that in which it subsists is good; but that which falls away, from which it falls away from the good, is evil: a falling-away from good can be nowhere else at all except in that which subsists in some good. Therefore if there were no good, there would be no evil at all; because there would be nowhere that malice could be, if Wisdom, which did not make it, did not suffer it within her own. For Wisdom did not make [0188B] malice; but in that which she made she permitted malice to be, so that Wisdom, set against malice, might conquer, and being compared might appear the more beautiful. For Wisdom sets herself against malice and measures herself with it in her works, and conquers it on both sides, so that she may reach from end to end; because malice, running toward defect, does not escape Wisdom, but Wisdom, rising toward advancement, transcends malice. For malice running toward defect does not escape Wisdom for this reason, that malice cannot find what it may corrupt except in those things which Wisdom created. But Wisdom, rising toward advancement, transcends malice for this reason: that malice cannot corrupt the whole of what Wisdom was able to create. So that malice may be compared, then, nothing can be brought through malice [0188C] to non-being which has not received being from Wisdom; but that malice may be overcome, malice does not corrupt the whole of what has been made by wisdom.



## HOMILY XI. *What the ends of the earth are in which the eyes of fools are: and how wisdom reaches from end to end mightily,* etc. (cont.)

Wisdom runs, therefore, that she may reach from end to end, transcending malice in the incorrupt goods, whither malice does not come; and following after malice in those things which have been corrupted, whither malice does not go before her. For malice cannot go before Wisdom, so as to be found either prior, before her rising, or posterior, after her failing; because that also is found prior to corruption which at some time admitted corruption, and posterior to corruption that which could not be wholly corrupted, and above corruption that to which corruption did not come. And for this reason Wisdom conquers malice, and goes before folly; because she reaches from end even to [0188D] end, that is, from the rising of every good, that she may transcend malice, beyond the failing of a certain good, that she may shut malice in. For in that in which every good at some time stood without corruption, Wisdom is acknowledged as prior. But in that good also which is corrupted, in which after the corruption something of good remains over which cannot be corrupted at all, Wisdom is found posterior. Wisdom therefore conquers malice; because she reaches from the end in which every good received its beginning, even to the end in which even a corrupted good, after the failing of the good, subsists in some good. And she reaches from end to end mightily, so as to hold malice itself within measure by her power, and to restrain it to order by her reason, lest either corruption, destroying the whole, stretch itself unto the end, or confusion, deforming the whole, [0189A] pour itself out to the uttermost parts. For this reason, therefore, she reaches mightily everywhere, subjecting malice to her dominion: and whatever of corruption malice brings in toward confusion, this Wisdom, by a wondrous law and a secret disposition, turns to the beauty of her own works, bringing it about out of what is not beautiful, that what is beautiful becomes most beautiful, and that what stands as good in itself rises up, out of that which is not good, into the consummation of goodness. She reaches, then, from end to end mightily, and orders all things sweetly. Nothing is excepted from her sweetness. She orders all things sweetly. What things all? Crooked and straight, good and evil, the subject and the adverse — she orders all things sweetly: not only good things, but evil ones too; not only straight things, but crooked ones too; not only subject things, but adverse ones too. She orders all things [0189B] sweetly. How sweetly? Do you wish to know how sweetly she orders all things? Without violence she subjects adverse things; without tumult she sets crooked things straight, and in her most peaceable kingdom, which justice holds within, she makes even evils and the motions of the vices that oppose her to serve, without compulsion, by a secret order and a most wise disposition. For she orders all things, even things not sweet, so sweetly that all the things which raise themselves against her will she neither powerfully extinguishes, so that they are nothing, nor violently compels, so that they can do nothing; but, allowing them to be and granting them to be able, she manifestly permits things running against her will, and secretly leads them to the fulfilling of her will. And when the wicked will those things against her, they will them without her; but when they can do what they will, they can do it from her. And when they do [0189C] what they can, they think indeed and wish to do what she does not will; and yet they do not do otherwise, nor are they able to do otherwise, than what she wills. For they are manifestly permitted and secretly led, that they may be sweetly ordered; and Wisdom reaches even to them mightily, so as to restrain them to her own disposition; and she orders sweetly, so as not to compel them against their own will. But even if at times she outwardly applies violence to crooked wills to be applied to, or to be chastised; nevertheless she never forsakes the sweetness of her disposition; because, remaining tranquil in herself, from that very source whence she disturbs the ill-quiet peace of those who oppose her, she thence composes the kingdom of her justice the more solidly unto peace. For this reason, therefore, she reaches from end even to [0189D] end mightily, and orders all things sweetly.

There remains now that greatest and highest course of Wisdom, from end to end: which ends have no end. Above, the sublimity of contemplation, and below, the depth of damnation. For these ends have no other ends beyond them by which they themselves might be bounded; but all things reach as far as these, which were made for these, and beyond them nothing. To these two ends wisdom, beginning from the middle, ordering on this side and on that, whether by advancing [sic: *promovende*] or by forsaking, leads the course of all things; strewing justice as a way for those ascending above; leaving iniquity as a way for those descending below, so that through justice one may go to blessedness [sic: *ab beatitudinem*], but through iniquity and injustice one may come to damnation. And when it has come thither, she will set all things in their own ends; [0190A] and the ends themselves will never have an end. And then too Wisdom herself will reach from end to end mightily, so that never more will she either fail in the requital of the just, or desist from the punishment of the iniquitous: ordering all things sweetly, on this side to joy, on that to eternal torment. But, I ask, what sweetness will there be in the regions below? what sweetness will there be able to be in eternal torments, that it should be said, ordering all things sweetly? Is it sweetly for this reason, that she who orders remains sweet herself, although those whom she orders are not sweet, nor is that sweet in which she orders? Yet how does she order sweetly? Is it sweetly for this reason that she orders, because the ordering itself is sweet, even if those things are not sweet which she orders: and, just as she orders evils not evilly but well, so she orders unsweet and bitter things sweetly? [0190B] For therefore evils exist, and good things have been permitted by the Omnipotent to be evils, not that evils may become gifts; but that out of evils good may come to be, and that the good things themselves may shine out more beautifully by comparison with evils; not that good things may be increased by evils joined to them, but that they may be commended by things compared with them. But who is wise, that he may understand these things — what evils do in the kingdom of the good: and yet, if they did nothing in the kingdom of wisdom, they would not be evils at all? Therefore some do evils on account of which they have been permitted to be: and both they themselves and evils do good, and evils themselves do not make good, but he makes good out of the evils themselves: he who knows how to use both good things and evil for good. And this is Wisdom, which reaches from end to end mightily, and orders all things sweetly.



## HOMILY XII. *On that passage, according to another translation: The eyes of fools are in the ends of the earth; where our copy has: The fool walketh in darkness, and on the remainder of the second chapter.*

[0190C]

We have perhaps lingered longer over the discussion of these matters than the brevity of a treatise would require; but not fully so long as the difficulty of the subject would demand. Now therefore, since we have found an end, and an end, concerning which Scripture spoke when it said that Wisdom reaches from end to end mightily, and orders all things sweetly; and since it was our purpose to seek these ends on account of the ends of the earth, where the eyes of fools are, who walk in darkness, lest perchance Wisdom, who reaches from end to end, should reach to the ends [0190D] of the earth, and the light shine in the darkness in the ends of the earth, if Wisdom reaches to the ends of the earth, she who reaches from end to end mightily, and orders all things sweetly. Since therefore we have found these ends. If nevertheless the things we have said about these ends are able worthily to fit their own truth to the interpretation of Scripture, and there be no other more excellent interpretation to which this one should by right give place, it now remains to inquire what these ends of the earth are that have been mentioned, and where they are; and also what that earth itself is, and where it is, in whose ends are the eyes of fools, who do not look back to their own head that they may see, and not walk in darkness. First, then, let us ask what this earth is, and where this earth is. Now we know that in this world there is earth and there is heaven, [0191A] and between earth and heaven is whatever is in this world. For in the body of this world heaven is highest and earth lowest; and just as above heaven there is nothing of this kind which is not in heaven itself, so beneath the earth there is nothing, save perhaps what is in the earth. And if we say of this earth that the eyes of fools are in its ends, how this will not be able to stand. For we proclaim eyes keen-sighted rather than blind, which we assert are stretched out by their own vision even to the ends of the earth. Which of the just men has been able to do this, to survey the compass of the earth with his eyes, and to run through in a single seeing such far-off spaces as could scarcely be traversed by the labor of many years? It is ridiculous even to think this, that Scripture said it of bodily vision. Of this visible earth: The eyes of fools [0191B] are in the ends of the earth, unless perhaps someone believe that in these words this is signified: that all fools and lovers of the carnal life, who with the whole intention of the mind always go about after earthly goods [sic: *a terrena bona*], run about in pursuit and search of them even to foreign nations, and often even to the uttermost bounds of the lands: and that they are therefore said to have their eyes there, because by thought and desire they always strain thither. But the wise man, who has raised his mind to the things that are above and directs his intention thither, he himself may have his eyes in his own head, in that he strains toward the attaining of that which is highest of all and chief. This could, so far as pertains to the truth of the interpretation, be brought in suitably enough, if Wisdom did not call us to a higher understanding, and to another earth, and to other ends, and to other eyes [0191C] to be thought of. For if the head of the wise man is rightly understood spiritually, it is necessary that we interpret the earth also and its ends mystically and not bodily, on account of the eyes of fools. As, therefore, this visible world has its own earth and its own heaven, so man has his own earth and his own heaven. For man himself also is a world, and man has been called the lesser world. His earth is his flesh, and his heaven is his soul. But in this greater world the earth is within the heaven; in the lesser world, however, the heaven is within the earth. And yet the heaven of the lesser world is greater than the heaven of the greater world. But what is its earth compared to the earth of that other? How small it is? and yet its heaven is within its earth. What is heaven within [0191D] earth? The soul within the body. The soul is heaven, the body is earth. The soul within the body; heaven within earth. And yet heaven is always above, earth below. For as this heaven is loftier than earth by its place, so that heaven is more sublime than its earth by its nature. Therefore in the greater world, the things that are in the earth are beneath the earth; the things that are outside the earth are above the earth. In the lesser world, the things that are within are above, the things that are without are beneath. What are the things within, and what the things without? Invisible things are within, visible things are without. Therefore invisible things are above, visible things beneath; and invisible things are above our heaven, invisible things beneath our earth. All earthly things are beneath our earth, because they were made for its service and ordained for its obedience. Therefore our earth is above the earth. And what shall I say? [0192A] Is not our earth also above heaven? Did he not carry it thither, and place it there, taken from us, he who took it for us? Therefore our earth is above heaven. Our earth had first been made by nature above the earth; afterward our earth went, through guilt, into the earth, and beneath the earth; but now, through grace, our earth has been raised above heaven. Therefore all earthly things are beneath our earth. And yet this same earth is joined to earthly things and coupled to them by a kind of friendship, as heaven is joined and allied to heavenly things; and earth reaches as far as earthly things, and heaven as far as heavenly things. Therefore earthly things are the ends of the earth, and heavenly things the ends of heaven; because earth reaches as far as earthly things and no farther, and heaven as far as heavenly things and no farther. Earth stretches itself through concupiscence as far as earthly things, and [0192B] heaven stretches itself through wisdom as far as heavenly things. For *the flesh,* as the Apostle says, *lusteth against the spirit; and the spirit against the flesh [n: (Galat. V)]*. For the spirit lusts upward and the flesh lusts downward: and therefore each of the two stretches itself in a direction contrary to the other. And when each has come whither it stretches, there it makes an end and stretches itself no farther. Therefore the ends of the earth are earthly things, whither the earth of the flesh is stretched by carnal desire. And our earth has indeed another end, that of judgment, but of desire it has no other end. Thus far it is stretched by desire, but by judgment it is led farther. Through desire, indeed, concupiscence is stretched toward earthly things; but through judgment, after earthly things it is led into the earth, after the earth to gehenna. Through concupiscence to pleasure, after [0192C] pleasure to iniquity, after iniquity to death, after death to damnation. *Every man,* says Scripture, *is tempted, being drawn away and enticed by his own concupiscence. For concupiscence, when it hath conceived, bringeth forth sin; but sin, when it hath been completed, begetteth death [n: (Jac. I)]*. *He hath conceived sorrow, and brought forth iniquity. His sorrow shall be turned upon his own head, and his iniquity shall come down upon his crown [n: (Psal. VII)]*.

And so the delights of carnal pleasures are the ends of the earth, and the death of the flesh follows upon the delights of the flesh. But the vengeance of damnation follows upon the death of the flesh. Therefore the ends of the earth have one end, death, by which they are received and consumed; but death [0192D] has damnation for its end, by which it is received and consummated. For carnal delights are consumed by death, so that they are no more; but the death of the flesh is consummated by damnation, so that it may be for ever. These ends fools are unwilling to consider, who have their eyes in the ends of the earth. For they gaze only at the first ends of the earth, round about the earth, where the earth itself is ended; not beyond, at the other end, where these ends are ended. They strain toward the ends of their own desire, not toward the ends of their own judgment. To the delight of their flesh, and to the enticements of their pleasures, they open their eyes, and they do not attend to what is to be thereafter beyond. Therefore they are fools who make an end in the middle of the way, where there is no end: and they do not look forward to the end where the end is. *The heart of fools is where mirth is, and the heart of the wise where sadness is (Eccle. [0193A] VII)* And another Scripture says: *In all thy works all the days [sic: omnis diebus] of thy life, remember thy last end, and for ever thou shalt not sin [n: (Eccli. VII)]*. For this reason fools do not fear to sin, because they look to the delight of sin alone, but do not look to what follows thereafter, the damnation of the delight. The eyes of fools are in the ends of the earth; the eyes of the wise man are in his head. What is the head of the wise man? Whatever is highest for anyone, this is his head. For the head is the highest part of a man. The head of the wise man, then, is that which is highest for the wise man. And what is highest for the wise man, save that which is highest of all. For he would not be wise who held for highest what was not the highest. And so that which is highest of all is the head of the wise man: and perhaps Wisdom herself is this head, whom [0193B] he who is wise sets before all things that seem to have anything to be desired, and in comparison with her he despises all things whatsoever. And rightly, because Wisdom is the highest of all; she herself is the head of the wise man, since this very thing, that he is wise, is from Wisdom herself, and what the wise man desires to be is wholly in her. Wherefore the head of the wise man is understood as nothing else more rightly than Wisdom herself; since from her is the origin of goodness, and in her consists the consummation of every good.



## HOMILY XII. *On that passage, according to another translation: The eyes of fools are in the ends of the earth; where our copy has: The fool walketh in darkness, and on the remainder of the second chapter.* (cont.)

And so that which is highest of all is the head of the wise man, and this head is nothing other than Wisdom herself; since Wisdom is the highest of all. The highest good, then, is the head, and the highest evil the end. For there are two, the highest good and the highest evil, [0193C] set on either side over against each other, and from the middle all goods and evils run back to these two ends. Every good to the highest good, and every evil to the highest evil. For there is no true good unless it leads to the highest good, nor a true evil unless it runs back to the highest evil. Therefore in like manner every evil is toward the highest evil, just as every good is toward the highest. But not in like manner is every evil from the highest evil, as every good is from the highest good. For *every* *best giving, and every perfect gift, is from above, coming down from the Father of lights [n: (Jac. I)]*. And what of evils? *The beginnings,* he says, *of sorrows are these [n: (Matth. XXIV)]*. Therefore every good is from the highest good, and every good is toward the highest good. In like manner [0193D] every evil is toward the highest evil, but not every evil from the highest evil. Therefore the highest good is both the head and the end of all goods. The highest of all evils is not a head, but only an end. Therefore every good, apart from the highest good, is good just so far as it leads to the highest good; and every evil, apart from the highest evil, is evil just so far as it leads to the highest evil. For there have been many who had these middle goods, and they were not true goods to them, because through them they did not come to the highest good. And there have been many who bore grievous middle evils, and they were not evils to them, because through them they did not descend to the highest evil. And so the former, lifted up by false goods, fell headlong to the highest evil; the latter, exercised and humbled by false [0194A] evils, advanced to the highest good. For this reason the wise man lifts his eyes as far as his own head, and nothing that is below suffices him, until he comes to that which is highest. For certain men have had their eyes upward and yet have not raised their eyes as far as the head, but have remained in the middle of the body. But what would the members do without the head? Could the members live, cut off from the head? So every good, without the highest good, is not a true good. You have seen a man abstaining from those things which are forbidden by the law, not committing murder, not committing adultery, not plundering, not bearing false witness, not coveting the goods of his neighbor, and you have called him a good man, of good life, of good conversation, worthy of praise, worthy of reward. [0194B] And how many have done these things and have not pleased God; because they did not do for God's sake what they did: and therefore they did not even do well, because they did not do it for the sake of the highest good. You have seen another climbing yet higher and doing greater goods, not only abstaining from evil, but even exercising himself in good, fasting, praying, bestowing many alms, showing compassion to the wretched, coming to the aid of those set in straits and tribulation, and you have proclaimed him great and worthy of imitation, as one near to God and chosen. And yet many have done all these things and have profited nothing; since they did not set God in the case. They did not have their eyes in their own head like the wise man, but bending downward into their own temptation, desiring to please either themselves or others, [0194C] and glorying in their own good without God, they lost their own good, and did not come to the highest good. For deservedly could they not keep the members alive, who cut off the head from the body. Therefore the eyes of the wise man are in his head, that he may first go forward to the highest good by intention and desire of the mind, afterward follow after it by zeal and action, and then come to it by reward. Let him go forward by intention, so that all he does he may do for the attaining of it; let him follow after by zeal, so that he may persevere in it; let him come to it by reward, so that he may receive it as his prize. In this manner, therefore, the eyes of the wise man are in his head.

For this reason, indeed, when our head was below with us, lest perchance our intention also should remain below, [0194D] the head ascended above to the head, that it might draw us after it: and behold, above there are not two heads, but one head. For the head below was Christ the man in the world, and the head above was God the Father in heaven. Do you wish to know that Christ is the head? *The head of the woman is the man, the head of the man is Christ [n: (I Cor. XI)]*. And Christ has been appointed by God head of the whole Church. And the Church herself, the bride of Christ and the body of Christ, what does she say of her bridegroom Christ? and of her head Christ? For she it is, the bride, who in Canticles, commending her beloved, after many such things and many others, adds this to his praise, saying: *His head is the finest gold [n: (Cant. V)]*. Therefore Christ is the head, and Christ has a head. And what is the head of Christ? Hear the Apostle: The head of the woman is the man; the head [0195A] of the man is Christ: *The head of Christ is God [n: (I Cor. XI)]*. And so the head of Christ is God: and Christ himself, about to ascend into heaven, said: *I ascend to my Father and your Father: my God and your God [n: (Joan. XX)]*. If therefore he ascends to God, he ascends to his own head; because the head of Christ is God. And this head is that finest gold which no rust consumes, no age wears away; because immortal divinity does not admit corruption. Behold, therefore, our head above is Christ, and our head above is God, and our one head is Christ and God; because Christ also is God, and the one God is Christ: and the head of Christ is God, and the one head is Christ, and the head of Christ is God; as Christ is the one beginning, and the head of Christ is God: *I,* he says, [0195B] *am the beginning, who also speak to you [n: (Joan. VIII)]*. For this beginning is itself our head: to which the wise man has raised his eyes, now straining toward him through faith, that afterward he may be able to have his eyes upon him, beholding him through sight. That we may lift our eyes to this beginning and to this head, Scripture admonishes us, saying: *If you have risen again with Christ, savor the things that are above, where Christ is sitting at the right hand of God; seek the things that are above, not the things that are upon the earth. For you are dead, and your life is hidden with Christ in God [n: (Coloss. III)]*. For where we are dead, there we ought not to savor; but there we ought to savor, where we have life. If we are dead upon the earth, how do we savor upon the earth? For what dead man perceives? There, [0195C] therefore, we ought to perceive, where we have life; there to savor, where our life is hidden with Christ in God, where Christ is sitting at the right hand of God. What is it to savor? To love him, that is to savor. For love itself is savor, and love itself is wisdom. And so to love the things that are above, this is to savor the things that are above; because love itself draws thither where that is which is loved: and meanwhile it has its own savor by which it is refreshed, until one comes to that which is loved. Since, therefore, what is loved through wisdom is above, for that reason Wisdom herself draws upward. And because what is loved through concupiscence, it is necessary that concupiscence itself also draw downward. But from that whereby Wisdom draws upward, and concupiscence downward, there is as it were a division of darkness from light: and the hearts which [0195D] descend downward fall away from the light; but those which ascend are in the light. For they who descend to vanity fall away from truth, and they who fall away from truth fall away from the light; because truth is light. And the light itself does not fail, but they themselves fall away from the light, when they become darkness; because darkness is not light, nor is it illumined by the light so as to shine. Nevertheless the light itself shines even in darkness, but the darkness does not comprehend it [n: (Joan. I)], so as to receive it into itself and become partakers of it, since darkness cannot have communion with light. These, then, are the darkness in which fools walk: they who savor earthly things and, not understanding their own honor, *are compared to senseless beasts, and are become like to them [n: (Psal. XLVIII)]*. For [0196A] because after the manner of beasts they seek only earthly things, and seek after heavenly things with no affection of the mind, at length they are so blinded by the delight of the flesh, and so lulled asleep by the enticements of pleasures, that they are neither able [sic: *valcant*] to think from what goods they have fallen, nor to look forward to what evils they are to come. What, then, do these do but walk in darkness, who are so blinded by a crooked delight that they can neither know true goods nor foresee true evils? What darkness do they suffer within, who do not see the light that is present, and cannot recognize that good whose presence they are able by no turning away to avoid? Why is this? unless because folly has blinded their eyes: which they have fixed in the ends of the earth, determining with themselves to turn their eyes down into the earth.



## HOMILY XII. *On that passage, according to another translation: The eyes of fools are in the ends of the earth; where our copy has: The fool walketh in darkness, and on the remainder of the second chapter.* (cont.)

[0196B]But lest perchance anyone think it contrary, that here the fool is said to walk in darkness, and that there, as has been recalled, the eyes of fools are declared to be in the ends of the earth. For both seem to be affirmed, that they see and that they do not see, although both may nevertheless be true at once, that they see and that they do not see. For he who said: The fool walketh in darkness, seems to have said outright that the fool does not see. And again he who said: The eyes of fools are in the ends of the earth, seems to have said that they see something, namely those things which are in the ends of the earth. How then do they walk in darkness, if they see? And how do they not see, if they see those things which are in the ends of the earth? But it must be known that fools are said to walk in darkness not because they see nothing: for they see [0196C] the things which are in the ends of the earth, but because, whether in the things they see or in the things they do not see, they do not see the truth. For they do see visible things by intention and desire, and they do not see invisible things. And in the things they see, they think that to be good which is not; and in the things they do not see, they do not know that to be good which is. And so nowhere do they see the truth, and therefore they walk in darkness even in the things which they see. For the delight of the flesh and the [sic: *illececebra*] of pleasures has blinded their hearts, so that they do not see the truth. Because the mind, made drunk with a false sweetness, while it thinks of this alone which seems sweet and pleasant in the present, can neither remember how great is the sweetness of those goods from which it fell by its own turning away, nor foresee how great is the bitterness of the evils that are coming upon it, toward which [0196D] the ruin of its own headlong fall always hastens. And note how the sentiment of truth follows upon itself. For above, this man, having considered the vanity of worldly things, despising all, betook himself to the contemplation of wisdom, saying: I passed over to the contemplation of wisdom, and errors and folly. Then considering that the true wisdom of man is nothing other than to seek true goods, and to love the things that are above, where our King is: who therefore went before us, ascending thither, that he might show whither we should follow. Noting also how difficult it is to raise up a mind accustomed to earthly delights toward the desires of eternal things, he added and said: What is man, that he can follow the King his maker? Yet you see [0197A] that hearts which plunge themselves into carnal delights grow dark away from the light of truth — he gave, as it were, a definitive sentiment concerning good and evil, saying: I saw that wisdom went before folly as much as light differs from darkness. Then, commending the pursuit of both, he added at the last and said: The eyes of a wise man are in his head, but the fool walketh in darkness. Wherefore let the sentiment run back to its sum, and say: It is indeed difficult for mortal man to raise up to the contemplation of the wisdom above a heart pressed down with earthly desires; but nevertheless, because wisdom is light, and all who fail from the contemplation of it are in darkness, it is necessary that even mortal man, so far as his capacity supplies, apply his zeal to the seeking and searching out of Wisdom. [0197B] For this is the wisdom of man in this life: to seek and to search out Wisdom. For to find wisdom and perfectly to lay hold of the truth is not granted to this life, but laid up for the life to come. Yet this very inquiry after Wisdom has now its own light, by which it distinguishes those who love and seek Wisdom from those who love folly and walk in darkness. But the carnal mind, loving the pleasantness of visible light, counts vain the labor for the inner light to be sought; because it either doubts or is wholly ignorant that its brightness, after this light has been taken away, remains inextinguishable in holy minds. For because it discerns that the wise and fools, by an equal lot and like condition, suffer to be withdrawn from this visible light after the death of the flesh, a doubt arises for it also concerning the light [0197C] of Wisdom, whether it can be preserved inextinguishable in holy minds after the death of the flesh. Therefore, although it considers the pursuit of Wisdom itself to be good and praiseworthy, yet it doubts whether it is to be procured with so great a labor, since as to whether its fruit can be perpetual, no undoubted reason as yet makes anything manifest to it. This wavering of the human heart, then, this man, both seeing it in himself through the contemplation of truth and feeling it through the affection of the common infirmity, takes up also in a sentiment, showing how, wisdom having been sought and seen, that very consideration of mortal life was calling him back from the pursuit of wisdom, and that he therefore judged it wholly vain that others should advance in wisdom, because in paying the debt of death [0197D] he recognized himself to be equal with the rest. For this is what, after the praise of Wisdom and the commendation of the pursuit of it, he now suddenly brings in, turned to the consideration of the common infirmity, saying:

*I learned that the destruction of both was one.* For because he saw that both, that is, both the wise man and the fool, are withdrawn from this life by one destruction of death, he began to doubt this also: whether after this life diverse rewards of merits ought to be found according to this same diverse manner of life. And if it stands firm that this is true, why do they go out from this life by one way, who after this life do not attain to one reward? But if, just as the passage is one for all, so also there comes to be one arrival for all: why should they run before death by laboring unlike one another, [0198A] who, going out by a like death, would not come after death to an unlike reward? For this is what follows: *And I said in my heart: If the fall of the fool and my own shall be one, what does it profit me that I have given greater labor to wisdom?* For by those who believe this life alone to be, the pursuit of Wisdom is judged wholly vain: for this reason, that its labor belongs to the present life, but the reward and fruit of the labor to the life to come. Therefore, because they see the labor, they quickly grow weary of the labor itself, and easily turn to those things which, even though wisdom teaches that they are by no means to be sought after, yet, because their fruit is present, they judge more convenient. But because Wisdom does not allow her labor to be wholly fruitless even in this life, and cherishes those who follow after her with a great inward consolation, [0198B] and through the illumination of truth persuades them to have confidence, in this misery of mortality, concerning the reward of eternity, this man, immediately after he had begun, because of the failing of the carnal life, to doubt concerning the fruit of the perpetual life, accuses himself out of his inmost consideration for this same doubt of his, saying:

*And speaking with my own mind, I perceived that this also was vanity.* And at once, adding the reason why he testifies that the aforesaid doubt is a vain thing, he brings in also the vanity itself in respect of which the truth could have come into doubt for him, saying:

*For there shall not be remembrance of the wise man in like manner as of the fool for ever.* For Wisdom, which does not take away mortality from the present life, grants [0198C] eternity to the life to come: and although the condition of the wise man and of the fool is alike at the going out of this life, yet in the life to come the remembrance will not be alike. For as here an unlike manner of living divides them, so there too, when they shall have come into remembrance so that he may be brought to judgment, an unlike requital according to their own merits shall separate them. For future times shall not cover all things with oblivion, but all things that have been done shall come into recollection in their own time, so that every work, whether it be good or evil, may be called into judgment. Therefore the memory of the wise man shall not for ever not be in like manner as that of the fool, and future times shall not cover all things with oblivion, because, when the time shall have come, the one shall receive glory for the merit of his virtue, and the other shall receive punishment for the excess of his folly. [0198D] This, then, is that for which it is not vain to shun folly, and to give greater labor to wisdom; because, when the reward of requital is received, it is made manifest how great is the fruit of just labor. Yet this very consideration of our mortality has a certain just weariness in it; because it is fitting that a man should despise what he sees to be transitory even before it comes to an end? and that he should not afflict himself with empty labor for the attaining of that which cannot long stand. On this account he again redoubles the sentiment concerning the same equal condition of mortality, saying:

*The learned dieth in like manner as the unlearned: and therefore my life became wearisome to me.* And deservedly indeed does such a life come into weariness, which, being wholly liable to confusion, neither in its goods while it seems to stand, nor in its evils while [0199A] it comes to an end, separates the good from the evil or the worthy from the unworthy. For its goods come without discernment to the unworthy equally as to the worthy, and its evils afflict the good and the evil together by an equal lot and with the same outcome. Whence the goods also of this life rightly come into contempt with the just: goods which both afflict with various sorrows those who labor for them, and often, sought with much labor, pass over without profit to those who possess them, from the good to the wicked, and from their own to strangers. Considering which, this man, setting all things in one part — not only those which seem the evils of this life, but those also which put forward the appearance of good, while in real truth they are evils — and adjudging them by a like estimation, gives one sentiment concerning them all, saying:

*Seeing that all things under the sun are evil, and all vanity, [0199B] and affliction of spirit, again I detested all my industry.* And lest perchance you should think that he here detests that industry by which he gave greater labor to wisdom, and not rather that by which he was distended by the occupation of those things which are vain under the sun: presently he sets forth what industry he judges is to be detested, saying: And what [qua] under the sun I most studiously labored at. "I detested" is understood. And immediately he opens the reason why he detests both these things and equally the industry spent on them, when he subjoins:

*Since I am to have an heir after me, whom I know not whether he will be wise or foolish: and he shall have dominion over my labors, in which I have toiled and been anxious.* And lastly, commending his judgment concerning this, he brings in:

[0199C] *And is there anything so vain?* As if he were to say: I judge nothing so vain as when one man, greedily gathering, is worn away with fruitless labor, and another, living both idly and luxuriously, has dominion over the labors of another. For manifold is the vanity where one man is afflicted without cause, and another is unlawfully delighted. For the first vanity is to seek after things that will not profit; the second, to gather things that pass away; the third, to leave the things gotten by our own labor to be possessed by others, and above all by those of whom we do not know what sort they will be. For if all these things were vain even when sought and kept for our own uses, what will they be when handed over to the pleasure of fools? Whence he says:

*I ceased, and my heart renounced to labor any more under the sun.* Redoubling also the reason why he ceased from labor, [0199D] he subjoins, saying:



## HOMILY XII. *On that passage, according to another translation: The eyes of fools are in the ends of the earth; where our copy has: The fool walketh in darkness, and on the remainder of the second chapter.* (cont.)

*For when one man labors in wisdom, and doctrine, and carefulness, he leaves what he has gotten to a man that is idle.* This befell Solomon himself, who, laboring in wisdom and doctrine and carefulness, gathered riches and heaped up wealth, and at last left all to be possessed by a foolish heir. For he left the folly of his own stock, his son Rehoboam, who, succeeding to his father's wealth, scattered all, and left the kingdom of his father — which he had received ample enough and magnificent — torn and troubled by a diminishing of sense and a surfeit of heart. But many also labor in wisdom and doctrine and carefulness, searching out with great labor and zeal the secrets of nature and the hidden things of truth, and handing on what they have found to the memory [0200A] of posterity, by word alike and by writing, for the commending of their own studies; and often they leave what they have gotten to idle men, because sometimes the negligence of those who come after refuses to take up for use this thing prepared, which the keen diligence of the earlier men acquired not without great labor and care.

*This then is vanity, a great evil.* It is therefore vain to labor in the knowledge of truth in order that we may come to the notice of those who neither wish to receive truth for its own sake, nor know how to approve it in others. Fruit, then, does not follow upon labor of this kind, because it neither gathers things that will profit itself, nor are those to whom it leaves what has been gathered stirred by any emulation of virtue to the pursuit of the good. For if there is labor for the one who seeks, and there is no profit for the one who receives, what does such labor profit? [0200B] But it is also a great evil, that from what one man is worn away with labor, another should abuse idly and unlawfully. For it is a great evil, because both the one gathers to his own harm and the other receives to his own harm. Therefore this is vanity and a great evil.

*For what does it profit a man of all his labor, and affliction of spirit, wherewith he has been tormented under the sun?* If it does not profit toward anything else, it profits nothing; nay rather, if it does not profit toward anything else, it does much harm, because the searching after it has great punishment, but the finding has no fruit. For what a punishment it is never to be without punishment?

*All his days are full of sorrows and hardships: nor does he rest in mind by night.* Very wretched is he who cannot have quiet even in that time which is naturally given [0200C] to quiet. By day he is worn away with labor, by night he is tormented with the care and anxiety of labor. For him whom, waking, the distension of labor and its restlessness harasses, it wearies also when he sleeps, being heaped upon his soul through the images and phantasies of dreams. No time, then, is allowed to pass for rest; the whole is taken up with sorrow and labor, and nothing is gained for profit and salvation.

*And is not this vanity?* Truly it is vanity, and utterly a great vanity. But what then? If this is vanity, what then is to be done?

*Is it not better,* he says, *to eat and drink, and to shew his soul good things of his labors?* If those labors are vain which confer no profit on those who labor, [0200D] then it is better to labor and to take the fruit of one's own labors. The reasoning seems plain, but it has many an exception. For it would have been much better if it had been said thus, and it would have stood true everywhere: If those labors are vain from which no fruit comes to those who labor, then labors of this sort are altogether to be set aside, and one must rather labor in those things wherein a fruit both certain and true may come to those who labor. But now by this reasoning vanity has been changed, not dismissed, and one vanity has succeeded another, not every vanity has departed. Vain [sic: *Vena*] carefulness is rebuked, so that a mind harmfully free of care may be loosed through torpor and idleness and incontinence into baseness. For if that man who distends himself with superfluous labor is truly seen to be blameworthy, why on that account is he who dissolves himself into pleasure and incontinence [0201A] thought to please? But the carnal soul finds the middle of truth with difficulty; and therefore it sometimes willingly consents to forsake one vice, since through it it sees a way opened for itself to another which pleases it more. And it pours itself out into the contrary with its whole intention the more boldly, the more certain it holds it that what it forsakes is an evil. For now, in its own evil, it not only judges itself worthy of no rebuke, but even supposes itself praiseworthy; and although it manifestly commits evil, it does not wish to be seen to be doing evil, but rather to be shunning evil. For it does not consider that by turning aside from a vice it has fallen into a vice, and therefore it thinks that by no means is the evil which it does to be marked with ill repute; because in it, as it were, it labors not so much to do evil as to turn aside from it. Whence here too, [0201B] when he had seen that those men are wretched and worthy of rebuke who greedily gather with labor and carefulness riches that will not profit them, and neither use the things they possess, but leave them to others to be possessed and used after them, in order to shun their folly he freely casts himself into the contrary sentiment, saying: Is it not better to eat and drink, and to shew his soul good things of his labors? There is one sentiment of all who are prone to vice. They always rebuke the opposite, that they may commend their own. Let the greedy perish, says the glutton and the spendthrift — and he devours, and gets drunk, and distends himself and fills himself to the point of choking. And his proverb is always: Let the greedy perish. And he thinks himself commended because he is not such a man, lest he be blameworthily such a man as he is. Nay, even for this reason he wishes to be seen to be such [0201C] as he is, lest perchance he be such as he accuses others of being, which he does not wish to be — as though indeed there were no middle ground where both might come to pass; both that he be not such as he accuses others of being, and yet that he be not such as with shameless boasting he exults that he is. It is better to eat and drink, and to shew his soul good things of his labors. How fair are the words, to eat and to drink! Who is there that shudders when he hears "to eat and to drink," or thinks that anything has been said here against modesty or chastity? But a shameless baseness lies hidden, covered with the veil of chastity. Let him therefore say openly: It is better to devour, and to get drunk, and to serve the desires of one's concupiscence with unbridled liberty. For if he had spoken thus, what sort of sentiment would it be? And indeed [0201D] he did speak altogether thus; because he wished to signify this in that which he said: It is better to eat and drink, and to shew his soul good things of his labors.

*And this is from the hand of God.* Men say this, and there is a kind of men who are wont to say this: Let us devour, and let us get drunk, and let us do well by ourselves out of those things which God has given us, because this is from the hand of God; because these things too were given us for this, that we might do well by ourselves. For why were all these things made, save for the uses of men, that men might use them, and that it might be well with them in those things which God gave them? And by saying these things they are blinded, and are carried away without measure and without chastity into their own pleasures, so as to applaud themselves, since God made all these things for use; but they neither know nor consider that although God made all good things for use, [0202A] yet he made nothing for crooked and disordered uses.

*Who shall so devour, and abound with delights, as I?* An evil boast is this. And yet there are those who glory in such things: and these too wisdom ought not to have passed over in the number of the vanities. Such men the prophet rebukes in another place, saying: *Woe to you that are mighty to drink wine, and stout men at mingling drunkenness [n: (Isa. V)]*. Who shall so devour, and abound with delights, as I? What manner of lover of wisdom is this? How far turned backward, nay, how far turned away and perverted from him who a little before saw the light of wisdom, and divided it from the darkness of folly with so subtle a consideration, saying: I saw that wisdom went before folly as much as light differs from darkness. Whence, do you think, was this sense so quickly made worst [0202B] [pessundatus]? Did he not say for his own sake what he said of himself, that he was a lover of wisdom: and this was not what he said; but he wished to signify certain men of that sort, for whose sake he said this which he said of himself? Or was he himself also, in some respect, all this truly, and yet did not on that account cease to be a lover of wisdom; because at once in one man both the sense of the flesh, according to the inborn corruption and the sin that dwells in it, urged wickedness, and yet the spirit, according to the affection of virtue and the judgment of uprightness, loved wisdom? See, nevertheless, what man is, whether in him who said this and was such a man, or in those for whose sake he said what he said of himself: whom he wished to signify to be such. See indeed what man is. Who shall so devour, and abound with delights, [0202C] as I? *Why dost thou glory in malice, thou that art mighty in iniquity? [n: (Psal. LI.)]* If no one can do what thou canst do, it is a great thing if he is able for good. But if thou art able for more in evil, why dost thou glory? For to be able for evil is not power, but weakness. Who shall so devour, and abound with delights, as I? For who is there, he says, to gainsay it, when God commands this? For God created gladness, and willed that those who please him should take joy in it.



## HOMILY XII. *On that passage, according to another translation: The eyes of fools are in the ends of the earth; where our copy has: The fool walketh in darkness, and on the remainder of the second chapter.* (cont.)

*But to the sinner he gave affliction, and superfluous [sic: surperfluam] care, that he may labor, that he may be distended, and gather, and not possess, but hand it over to him who pleases God.* Therefore good things to the good, and evil things to the evil. Consider how great is this perversity. *Iniquity hath lied to itself (Psal. [0202D] XXVI)*. Iniquity has lied both about itself and about God; and yet it has lied to none but itself. Iniquity said: *Good things to the good, and evil things to the evil [n: (Eccli. XXXIX)]*. And where is that word: *Mercy and truth have met each other? [n: (Psal. LXXXIV.)]* Where is that: *All the ways of the Lord are mercy and truth? [n: (Psal. CXVIII.)]* Where is that: *Men and beasts thou wilt save, O Lord: how hast thou multiplied thy mercy, O God? [n: (Psal. XXXV.)]* Where, in short, is that which Truth herself and mercy says: *Be ye merciful, as your Father also is merciful: who maketh his sun to rise upon the good and the bad, and raineth upon the just and the unjust? [n: (Matth. V.)]* If good things have been given to the good alone, what is it that Scripture says of the good: *The kiln trieth the potter's vessels, and the furnace of tribulation just men? [n: (Eccli. XXVII.)]* [0203A] And: *All who wish to live godly in Christ must needs suffer persecution [n: (II Tim. III)]*. *They went about in sheepskins, in goatskins, being in want, naked, straitened, afflicted, of whom the world was not worthy [n: (Hebr. XI)]*. *In hunger and thirst, in cold and nakedness? [n: (II Cor. XI.)]* And so iniquity lied about God, when it said: That God gives good things to the good alone, and evil things to the evil alone. For he both gives good things to the good, and gives evil things to the evil; and gives evil things to the good, and good things to the evil. For to the good he both gives good things that they may be cherished, and gives evil things that they may be exercised; and to the evil he gives evil things that they may be chastised, and gives good things that they may be called forth. And so iniquity lied about God, when it said: God gives good things to the good and evil things to the evil. But it would have spoken truth if it had spoken of true goods and of true evils, namely that true goods and true evils are goods to none but the good, and [0203B] evils to none but the evil. For this reason, then, iniquity lied about God. But iniquity has also lied about itself. When did iniquity lie about itself? When it called itself goodness, then did iniquity lie about itself, because iniquity is not goodness. And where did iniquity call itself goodness? When it said: Who shall so devour, and abound with delights, as I? Then it called itself goodness: for iniquity said this, since this saying against goodness and honor belongs to iniquity and impurity. Therefore iniquity said this, and in this it called itself goodness: because it said that this alone belongs to goodness which it said it would do, since it presumed itself to be goodness. For it said:

*To a good man God gave, in his own sight, wisdom and knowledge and gladness.* First he gave wisdom and knowledge, that he might understand and know how to seek gladness [0203C] in his sight: because God made it that those who please him might exult in it. As though indeed it were not wisdom, and much the greater wisdom, to seek and embrace that sadness which is according to God, rather than a foolish gladness, since on the contrary Scripture says: *The heart of [sic: stulterum] is where mirth is, and the heart of the wise where sadness is [n: (Eccle. VII)]*, This indeed is true, that God has prepared gladness for his elect in his sight, and has set wisdom and knowledge in their heart, that they may know and understand how to seek it where it is.
