But this is not that gladness with which they are glad who do evil: *who rejoice in the worst things [n: (Prov. II)]*. For that gladness is not in his sight, because of those who embrace it Scripture said: [0203D] *Thou shalt set them downward [var: Vulg. dorsum, "the back"], in thy remnants thou shalt prepare their countenance [n: (Psal. XX)]*. Therefore God gave gladness to the good man, but that gladness which is in his sight. Of which it is written: *Let the just feast, and exult in the sight of God: let them be delighted with gladness [n: (Psal. LXVII)]*. And again: *Visit us with thy salvation, that we may see the good of thy elect: that we may be glad in the gladness of thy nation, that thou mayest be praised with thine inheritance [n: (Psal. CV)]*. And again he says: *Thou shalt fill me with gladness with thy countenance: delights at thy right hand even unto the end [n: (Psal. XV)]*. This gladness, then, God gave to the good man in his sight.

*But to the sinner he gave affliction, and superfluous care.* Not by bringing in malice, but by descending to malice into the worst occupation.

[0204A] *That he may add and gather, and hand it over to him who hath pleased God.* Yet for this reason, because the one gathered riches through avarice, and the other, having received them, scattered them to the use of mercy. Otherwise neither is he who gathered rightly rebuked, nor is he who possesses commended. But now all things are said perversely, when it is said that he who devours and abounds with delights is good, and that to him God gave wisdom and knowledge and gladness in his sight — whereas it was said above that they are fools who have their eyes in the ends of the earth, looking to earthly delights and serving the pleasure of the flesh. And so iniquity lied, which said that pleasure is the reward of justice, and that the fruit of wisdom consists in it, and judged that bodily delights contain the sum of happiness. But a false judgment cannot long [0204B] stand in glory. For truth comes forth, and lays falsehood low. He says, in fact.

*But this also is vanity, and an empty carefulness of the mind.* What is this? Not only is this vanity and empty carefulness, that the sinner should add and gather and not possess; but this too is vanity and empty carefulness, that the just man should seek after and covet the riches of the sinner, or, if he has received them, should set his heart upon them. The whole, then, is vanity and empty carefulness, because nothing under the sun can remain, either for him who uses or for him who seeks riches and pleasures and joy and gladness. For all things pass away and flow off, and nothing subsists under the sun, so that the sentiment may be fulfilled: *Vanity of vanities, vanity of vanities, and all things vanity [n: (Eccle. I)]*. For vanity is filled full in this also, [0204C] that the very heart of man does not remain in the same state, but, always other than itself and adverse to itself and a stranger outside itself, always removes far from that which is into that which is not; and it passes over and is snatched away with the greatest vanity, whether from vain things to true or from true things to vain, everywhere vain and unstable, now seeking the highest things, now plunging itself into the lowest. There approving truth; here setting vanity before it. And we now see this greatest wavering of the human mind, contemplating in one man the whole race of men, and we were wondering how quickly the sense of mortals is brought to ruin, and, after so sublime a contemplation of truth, suffers itself to go into the alien things of baseness. From which indeed nothing else is given us to understand, [0204D] except that we should know our own wretched vanity, and that in this, that we reach to high things and approve the better, we should consider what sort of being man was made by nature: but in this, that we seek after things so wretched and abject, we should understand what sort of being man has been made by his own guilt, so that at least in this we may begin to be partakers of truth, that we truly acknowledge our own vanity.



## HOMILY XIII. *How all things have their own time.*

Many are the words of man, because the heart of man is not one. For unless the mind first scattered itself inwardly, away from the fount of truth, through many desires, in no wise would speech derive itself outwardly through so many assertions. But now, because the ruling soul is torn away from [0205A] the love of virtue, through various desires, into the concupiscence of vanity, therefore in the approving of that same vanity the tongue serves it outwardly by word with a various and inconstant judgment. For this reason, then, Solomon, disputing about vanity, so often changes his sentiment in his discourse, namely that he may show his understanding to have been changed in his thinking through the love of vanity. For the human heart, which is not fixed in the desire of eternity, is never able to be stable; since it is shaken from its own stability by the seething of carnal desires as often as it is moved, from those things to which it had clung by love, to the coveting of others. For because it seeks out the fruit of eternity in the use of things that pass away, and thinks that it can find the joy of happiness [0205B] in those things which were made only for the consolation of a time: therefore always, when in the things it has, with experience teaching, it has found either a false pleasantness or a true misery, it straightway turns itself to the seeking of other things, as though better, by which it may either lighten its own sorrow or fill up its pleasantness. Hence, therefore, it always seeks things to come and loathes things present: and it comes about in a certain wondrous way that he who is always deceived in the desire of what is to come is scarcely ever wholly deceived in the experience of things present. For man is his own witness that these very things which he loves are not the true goods; because while he always gapes in mind after things to come, and never acknowledges himself truly happy in the things he enjoys, in this he plainly proves even himself that all these things, although they can be sought after by fools when they are not had, as though for the highest happiness, yet can never, when they are had, be [0205C] possessed unto the highest happiness. And yet the carnal mind, blinded by the darkness of its own concupiscence, cannot plainly see what it also cannot be wholly ignorant of: that although it always sees vanity in the experience of things present, yet it never restrains itself from the appetite of things to come. It disapproves what it has experienced, it praises what is yet to be experienced; and the more immoderately it pours itself out through concupiscence in the desire of the things it does not yet have, the more grievously is it vexed with sorrow in the failing of the things it already has. This wavering of the human mind, then, Ecclesiastes has thus far set forth to us in himself, when in the approving of things, whether from loathing of things present or from appetite for things to come, with a changed mind he so often varied his judgment. For he thought [0205D] to find in those things which are rolled along under time something stable in which his soul might rest: and therefore, approving one thing after another as though by experiment, and disapproving all things once experienced, he acknowledged at last that there is nothing in them all which can either abide perpetually for those who enjoy it, or exhibit, while it is present, the full fruit of pleasantness. Therefore now, reproving his own ignorance, in that he believed himself to possess an abiding gladness in changeable things, he shows that just as all things, not only prosperous but adverse also, are good in their own time to him who uses them well, so to those who with a perverse love love temporal things instead of eternal, and hope that they can become happy in them, not even prosperous things can confer true happiness. He did ill, then, when he chose certain of them, as though better than all, [0206A] for his own pleasantness; because a strong soul, which through temporal things hastens to the state of eternity, not only seeks prosperous things of this world meanwhile for consolation, but also willingly takes adverse things into use, when the time demands, for the exercising of its own virtue. And therefore, all things being proved by experiment, he at last acknowledges the condition of changeable things, and confesses that nothing out of all the things that pass away can stand perpetually, saying:

*[n: (ECCLE. V.)] All things have their time, and all things pass in their own spaces under heaven.* Why then should you either fear the evils of this world or covet its goods, since all things pass under heaven? Since rather this accords with truth: that in all these things you hold not anything, whether in good or in evil, for the highest; because all things [0206B] pass away. Nor should you think anything is to be cast away in its own time, because all things have been justly ordered. For all things have their time, and all things pass in their own spaces under heaven. All things have their time, so that nothing is found perpetual and always abiding: but everything that is either follows something else, so that it does not come from the beginning, or runs before something else, so that it does not extend itself even to the end. All things have also a certain and determined time, when they begin and when they end; and likewise how long each subsists, and when each subsists, so that nothing is beside reason; and let the prudent soul study so to fit itself both to times and to temporal things that it neither set its confidence in the things that pass away as though they abided, nor presume to murmur, in the things that have been well ordered, against the disposition [0206C] of the Creator. And in every event, conforming itself to true and abiding goods, let it so despise the variety of changeable things that, although it use each thing for a time while it is present, it never allow its soul, at the passing of that thing, to turn aside from its own state. For that man is most prudent who so knows how to turn passing things to use that nevertheless he does not know how to bend his mind from its own stability at their failing. For all things in their own time are good to him who uses them well, and yet all things whatsoever that lie subject to changeableness, although in misery they afford some sort of consolation, cannot nevertheless confer ease. Nothing therefore is to be cast away in its own time, and nothing is to be chosen not in its own time; but let the soul be so [0206D] prepared for the use of a time that it nevertheless be not changed to the changeableness of the time. All things have their time, and all things pass in their own spaces under heaven. Why then should any one thing be chosen for every time, since all things have their time? For if some one thing out of them all had every time, all things would not have their time; since, of two things contrary and at odds with each other, if there were a time for the one always to be, there would be a time for the other never to be. And thus this one would have no time, since that one, with which it could not have the same time, would have every time. Now therefore, since all things have their time, no one thing out of them all has every time; but each thing has its own time, when that it should be is good [sic: *es*], and a time when it is good that it should not be. [0207A] When, therefore, it has its time, it is good that it should be, even if that is not good which it is good should be; and when it has not a time to be, it is good that it should not be, even if that is good which it is good should not be. Job knew this, and received good things, praising God in his benefits, and the enemy thought that a holy conscience lay subject to time. He took away riches and goods; he slew the pledges of his house; he smote the flesh, he assailed him with reproaches and injuries, that he might break by adverse things the man whom he could not soften by prosperous ones. But Job, knowing that all things have their time, just as in good things he restrained himself by continence, so in evil things also bravely bore adversities by patience. But his wife, because she was foolish, and did not understand that all things have their time, thought that the rewards of merits were to be weighed by the moments [0207B] of times: and therefore, seeing him smitten and believing him forsaken, she rebuked him for having worshipped God in vain, and mocked him, as though he had deserved the scourges in return for his blessing, saying: *Now bless God, and die [n: (Job II)]*. To whom he answered: *Thou hast spoken like one of the foolish women. If we have received good things from the hand of the Lord, why should we not receive evil? [n: (Ibid.)]* For all things have their time, and all things pass in their own spaces under heaven. If there is a time for the weak to be cherished with prosperous things, there is a time also for the perfect to be exercised with adverse things; if there is a time for good things to the good and evil things to the evil for the sake of truth, there is a time also for evil things to the good and good things to the evil for the sake of testing. All things have their time, and all things pass in their own spaces under heaven.



## HOMILY XIII. *How all things have their own time.* (cont.)

[0207C]Why do you plead, all of you under time, about the laws of times? It belongs to time to lie subject to changeableness and to suffer vicissitude. For if all these things do not come to pass in time, when are they to come to pass? For beyond times there will be eternity, and there will be no changeableness: and there will not then be there, "all things have their time"; but everything that will be will so be that it cannot be now one thing and now another according to a time, but what will be will always be. Therefore, so long as you live under time, suffer the laws of time. Suffer them willingly, lest perchance, even if you were unwilling, you should nevertheless suffer them. Suffer the laws of time, that you may pass through time to the state of eternity. For one cannot find eternity in time, because eternity comes only after times: and those find eternity after times who, in the flowing of alternating times, fixed in mind [0207D] upon the desire of eternity, stand unchangeable. All things have their time, and all things pass in their own spaces under heaven. For heaven itself also measures the spaces of all times, and all things that are under heaven pass away in time, and heaven itself does not pass away, from which time is. For time is, as it were, from heaven itself, and in heaven itself there is no time: and although all things that are under heaven receive their time from heaven, yet in heaven itself eternity stands firm. A great spectacle of wisdom is the visible image, that from the contemplation of visible things we may be roused to the emulation of invisible ones. Look upon heaven. It seems, as it were, to be moved, and it does not seem to be changed. But the things which are under heaven are changed, and thence is the origin of all motion, where [0208A] there is no changeableness. Therefore all things pass away under heaven, because in heaven all things stand firm. And the very things which pass away measure their own being from those things which subsist; because there would be no certain subsistence of things passing away if the state of the things that subsist were not perpetual. For those things which truly are, always are; and always are what they are; and there are other things which truly are not, and those which truly are: in a certain way they emulate them, that they may be something in that which they are; and when they have begun to be what they are, they pass over into that which they are not; and they are not truly this which they are, because they cease to be this, and now are not; nor are they truly this which they begin to be, because they only begin to be this, and are not yet. So these things are wanderers in true being, and in the very passage in which they run they seem to partake of a being [0208B] which they neither take in nor comprehend, although they seem to touch it, lest they be nothing at all. For they appear there, and when they are sought they are not, because they have gone away into the nothing whence they had come, and they were not out of that in which they were. For that alone in which they were truly was; and the whole of what it was, it itself was, nor had it received from elsewhere what it was; and in it all these things were admitted, so that out of that which itself truly was, these too might be something. As much, therefore, as they partake of true being, so much are all these things which do not have true being in themselves: to which this is the whole of being, that they are that very thing which has true being. And although in itself it is one, yet in them it seems to be another according to the capacity of those partaking, since they themselves are others in that which is not another. Each thing, then, has, according to its partaking of that [0208C] which always is, its own spaces of subsisting, some greater and some lesser; according as they are in that which is neither greater nor lesser, but is always the same itself. Therefore from it there is divided and measured out to each its own space and its own time, to one short, to another long; and there are days and months, and years, and lustra, and ages, and short hours, and moments, and instants; and all these are time in the things themselves which pass away and truly are not; and in that from which they are what they are, they are one, and there is found there not time, but eternity. All things have their time, and all things pass in their own spaces under heaven. For out of all the things that are under heaven nothing is eternal, but all lie subject to time and to changeableness. For all things at some time had a beginning, and at some time will have an end: and between the beginning [0208D] and the end is the space itself of time, which is time to that which has a beginning and an end; and that same space is eternity to that which has neither beginning nor end. For that very thing which began, before it began, was not; and when it ceased to be, it then was no longer; and then to it there was nothing, because that was nothing; and yet even then there was that which always was; and that same thing afterward was when this was; and this which began, began in that; and what was, was in that, so long as it was; and when this was, which was not always, that also was which always was, because this was in that; and what to the one was time, because it was not always, was to the other eternity, because it always was. So out of eternity all things have their time, and all things pass in their own spaces under [0209A] heaven. They have a time when they begin, and they have a time when they end. They have spaces for as long as they are to subsist: for this space is the delay of subsisting between the beginning and the end; and these spaces are like for many, unlike for many; yet all are weighed out by an equal judgment. And when the delay comes to those who live in gladness and joy, and lead their days among good things, it seems a great happiness; and when for those who are in hardships and are wearied by adversities the long spaces run on, it is judged a great misery and a great unhappiness; and yet man does not know his own lot, nor can he search out the judgment of God upon himself: for the long times themselves also have an end; and when they have received their end, those things are now no more which seemed good; and in like manner the things also which were thought evil are now [0209B] no more. And often upon passing goods there follow evils that will abide, and upon passing evils, after their end, goods which have no end: and then past goods can profit nothing in the multitude of evils, and past evils can hurt nothing, in the abundance of goods, to those happily brought to consummation.

Let not a man, therefore, glory on account of his own time, before the time, nor let him who is in tribulation be broken by the adversities of times; because all things pass away, until the time comes when there shall be measured back in eternity whatever was drawn out in the spaces of times, whether greater or lesser. Then the long spaces of times will not please the impious, the happiness of times being spent; and their faults abiding which they contracted through the times, when they shall have seen eternal punishments for temporal [0209C] deeds. But to the just their own long times will then seem even scant, when they shall have seen their temporal merits recompensed with eternal rewards. Before this time, whatever is done in time is hidden, whether it be long or short, so that man may not dare to search out the judgment of God, but may be prepared for every time, whether it be good or evil, so that, being proved through good things and evil, he may pass over to good things, to be glorified. And if meanwhile the spaces of time be long, let him not presume in good things, let him not fall in evil ones; because all things pass in their own spaces under heaven, so that, when the time under heaven passes away, there may succeed the eternity which is in heaven.

*A time to be born, and a time to die.* Now [0209D] he pursues, with examples following one upon another, what he had set forth generally, saying: All things have their time. And here he enumerates both goods and evils: and after goods, evils, and again after evils, goods; nowhere evils alone, or goods alone. For at this time of this life it is not possible to have goods without evils, nor evils without goods; because all things are mingled, so long as it is the time that all things have their time.



## HOMILY XIII. *How all things have their own time.* (cont.)

*A time to be born and a time to die; a time to plant and a time to pluck up; a time to kill and a time to heal; a time to destroy and a time to build.* First good things and evils: a time to be born and a time to die. Afterwards evils and good things: a time to kill and a time to heal. Not good things alone, nor evils alone; but both good things and evils. Nor only after [0210A] good things evils, but also after evils good things: yet first after good things evils, and then after evils good things follow. For there are two lives: one according to the flesh, the other according to the spirit. And we know, on the Apostle's testimony, that *not first that which is spiritual, but that which* is carnal, is first [n: (I Cor. XV)]. There are therefore two lives, the first carnal, the second spiritual. And each life has its own good things and its own evils; because *all the ways of the Lord are mercy and truth [n: (Psal. XXIV)]*; *for he himself saves men and beasts [n: (Psal. XXXV)]*; *he opens his hand, and fills every living thing with blessing [n: (Psal. CXLIV)]*. For he has set before every man both good things and evils. Good things indeed, by which he may cherish him through mercy; evils, by which through justice, examining his faults, he may condemn him. And every life has [0210B] its own good things and its own evils, so that all things may have their time, and all things under heaven pass away in their own spans; both are given, and both are set forth; the one out of justice, the other out of mercy. All the ways of the Lord are mercy, truth. *Mercy and truth have met one another: justice and peace have kissed [n: (Psal. LXXXIV)]*. If evils alone, there would be no mercy. If good things alone, there would be no justice. God therefore sets forth both good things and evils, and leaves to man the power of choosing which of the two he shall receive first. For it behooves him to taste [sic: *gustaro*] both, but which of them first lies in himself. But the carnal man, hastening as it were to his inheritance and turning aside from judgment, so that he may lack the blessing at the last, chooses good things first; and God gives him temporal goods that have an end, so that evils that shall abide without [0210C] end may follow upon them. But the spiritual man is first tried by adversities that he may be brought through to prosperity, and he too has his own evils and his own good things; but the evils in time, the good things in eternity. Thus every life is led to its consummation by an alternating lot, the one from good things to evils, the other from evils to good things. And all things have their time that they may be, and pass away that they may not be, running forward in their own spans to their end so long as they are. Good things pass away amid evils, and evils amid good things; and while they pass they sometimes make delay, and they have certain spans, each according as they are ordained, until they receive their end. Therefore he enumerates certain of the things that alternate in the world, that he may show the fluctuation of all mutable things. And first he sets down good things and evils, as though describing the state of the carnal life; [0210D] then evils and good things, expressing the condition of the spiritual life. Afterwards he alternates, and changes good things and evils, and evils and good things, that he may show a certain further confusion: because neither to the evil are there good things unto the end, nor to the good evils unto the end. But even before the end, now good things and evils, now evils and good things follow one upon another. Yet this whole variation of the distribution has its beginning from good into evil, when it is said: A time to be born, and a time to die; and its end from evil into good, when it is said, a time of war and a time of peace. Because the first life has good first, and evil last. The second life has evil first, and good last; in the middle of each, with evils and goods fluctuating by an uncertain lot. But among all these things which are enumerated for an example of all things, [0211A] certain are taken from human life itself, certain from those things that belong to human life, so that the whole of what is man's may be shown mutable — man who perhaps would not go wholly into corruption, if he could have stood at least in some measure either in himself or in his own. A time to be born and a time to die, this is from human life itself [sic: *devita humana*]; a time to plant and a time to pluck up what has been planted, this is from those things that belong to human life. For the work of man will perish together with man; because just as in man what is born is born that it may die, so what is planted by man is planted that it may be plucked up. For the time of every thing under heaven is not in the will of man, but in the judgment of the Maker. Since for every thing under heaven there is a time when God wills it, even [0211B] if man, when the time is, does not will what God wills. Therefore the things that God does, he always does when the time is; but the things that man does, he does not always do when the time is, since either by ignorance he does not know what is right, or by a depraved will he desires what is disordered. Therefore the time of every thing lies under the will of the Creator, when it is time that what is to be done in time be done, and everything that is done in time; if it does not accord with his will, there was a time when it was being done, but it was not the time when it ought to have been done. Therefore the works of men alone do not keep their own times, even when they are done in time; because in that part man alone does not follow the appointed time, where, abusing the liberty of choice, he does not govern himself under the will of the Maker.

[0211C]There is therefore one time which is allotted to each thing by the disposition of the Maker; another which is usurped by human presumption against the disposition of the Maker. In that time man, with the rest, submits to the law; but here he alone, beyond the rest, transgresses the law. And therefore there it is rightly done with him, even in that which he himself does not will; but here he often acts against what is right, doing nothing but what he himself wills. A time to be born, and a time to die. No one is born when he wills, even if someone sometimes dies when he wills. And yet many, when they die, by will die not when the time is; and many who are born by necessity are born when the time is. Even those who are born before the time are born when the time is; and [0211D] those who are born after the time are born when the time is: because necessity confers the time of being born, while a depraved will sometimes chooses the time of dying. Therefore no one offends in the time when he is born; and many offend in the time when they die. For even if nature sometimes passes by, yet the will does not offend, because that is not laid to the fault of the sufferer which not his own will concerning himself, but a just disposition from above, governs. Then therefore is there a time for every thing, when it is just that what is to be done be done, whatever that shall have been. Either by God without man, or by man, with God. For that anything should be done by man without God — there is no time for it, just as nothing just can be done by man without God. A time to be born and a time to die. A time to plant and a time [0212A] to pluck up. Here we may attend to a certain summary and universal comprehension of the mutability of all things, so that, as we distinguished at the beginning concerning the whole course of the work, so here too he may first express that mutability to which all things are subject, and then, pursuing more at large that one in which human actions fluctuate, may show it more monstrous than all and more to be feared. For these first four things which are set down seem to look especially to that mutability which is in all things that have a rising and a setting, and all the rest to belong properly to that which is engaged in human actions. For that all things that rise set, and that things increased grow old, was said on account of those things that live and feel: a time to be born and a time to die; on account of those things that live and do not [0212B] feel: a time to plant, and a time to pluck up; or else for those things that have a natural rising he set first a time to be born, and a time to die; and then for those that are fitted for quickening by care and industry he subjoined: a time to plant, and a time to pluck up. This therefore is that great vanity, vanity of vanities, for which it is too little that all things be mutable, unless they be further snatched into their contraries while all things pass away. For how great a thing it is to go far off and to sojourn away from one's own being, and to go and pass over into a contrary being, where you cannot at all be what you begin to be, unless you have utterly ceased to be what you are? There, then, the passage is hard, where by no reasoning can what follows subsist, unless first the whole of what precedes be destroyed. And what do you think, when there is a going from good things to [0212C] evils, when good things are changed into their contrary evils — what a change that is, most of all there where there is no running back, nor can any return be hoped for? For evils too are sometimes changed into good things, and again good things into evils; and often it happens that the two succeed one another by turns. Nor is that altogether grievous which is mutually tempered, so that neither are the evils great upon which good things follow, nor the good things best which are ended by evils following after; and all this can be borne, and whatever it may be, when it has come to pass, has its comparison, that it be not reckoned too much: there is one thing which, when it has befallen, who will be able to bear?

*A time to be silent and a time to speak. A time to gather and a time to lose. A time to mourn and a time [0212D] to laugh.* All these things are bearable: and when some one of them has befallen a man, that one passes and another comes; and this too, when it has stood its own time, passes, and that returns which had passed; and this happens everywhere again and again as long as one lives; and never does any one thing remain so as to be alone always.



## HOMILY XIII. *How all things have their own time.* (cont.)

*A time to be born and a time to die.* These are not like those, nor have they any running back once it has come to pass. For there is one death and one birth: and just as after one birth there is one death, so before one death there is not more than one birth. And no one dies who has not first been born; nor, when someone has once died, can he thereafter be born. Birth is the beginning of life, death the end, and there is a going from birth to death; but after death the way back to birth [0213A] does not lie open. For from birth the time of man begins, and by death it is ended. And therefore these have their time unlike all other things: the one, that in it the time is first, and it itself is no more; the other, that in it there is no more time, and it is always. This therefore is heavier than all, that it so has its time that it is itself without time, because after its time there will be no time in which what its time has taken away and consumed could be restored anew. Therefore the time to be born seems better than the time to die, because in the time of being born life is given, but in the time of dying life is taken away. And yet those who are born die, and those who die are born; since those who are born unto death begin to die from the moment they begin to live under the failing of mortality; [0213B] and those who die unto life begin truly to live from the moment when, after death, they have now ceased to be mortal. Wherefore a good death is more to be wished for than an evil life, because by a good death one proceeds to a good life, and by an evil life one arrives at an evil death. Therefore all things have their time, and nothing is to be cast away in its own time: since there is both a time to be born, that the first life may begin, which is exercised through justice in time; and a time to die, that the second life may follow, which enjoys the reward of justice in eternity. Afterwards he adds what follows:

*A time to plant, and a time to pluck up what has been planted.* This second thing therefore is of the work of the craftsman, together with nature. For the first work was of nature alone, [0213C] and that too was transitory. But now the work of the craftsman together with nature is subjoined; and that too is shown to be transitory, so that nothing may remain under the sun, the gladness which springs up from birth or from planting be tempered by the memory of death and of uprooting. Then there follow the works of the craftsman alone without the work of nature, and these too are enumerated in great number, and all are shown to be transitory, so that vanity may increase by its degrees; and yet all these things, just as by their mutability they are vain, so in their time they become fitting, so that in all these things man, even if he cannot hold the state of eternity, may yet study to keep the order of reason.

*A time to kill and a time to heal. A time to destroy and a time to build. A time to weep and a time to laugh. A time to mourn and a time to dance.* [0213D]And many other things which are enumerated, and many others which cannot be enumerated. And in all these human life suffers vicissitude and lies subject to mutability. For all these things pass over it, and run in by turns, one after another; and those that had gone before follow after, and again those that had followed go before; and without intermission this is done in the waves of great fluctuations. After weeping comes laughter, and after laughter weeping succeeds; and when joy has succeeded grief, again joys are changed into grief. And all these things have their time, when the will of man conforms itself through justice, according to the time, to the divine will, either willingly for the doing of them, or submits itself for the suffering of them. For all things have their time when man either does them according to God or suffers them for God, because for the man who walks according to [0214A] God all things in their own time, if prosperous things befall, cherish him; if adverse things happen, exercise him. Therefore all things have their time which man can justly do or justly suffer. But what cannot be done without injustice never has a time that it be done, because man has received no time for doing evil — man, to whom his time has been given for this alone, that he may either correct the evils he has done, or supply the good things he has not yet done. Therefore sins have no time; and all things have their time, because in their own time all things are to be well done; and things are good when all are well done, and out of all things sins are not among those that are to be well done, nor are sins good, although it is good that sins themselves should be. Therefore sins have no time, and sins are permitted to be done in time; [0214B] and they are not justly done when they are done, and they are justly permitted to be done when they are done; and when they have been done, they are justly ordered; and there comes a time that they be permitted to be done, and there comes no time that they be done, because they are not well done, and they are well permitted, that they may be justly ordered. A time to plant, a time to mourn, a time to dance, *a time to acquire.* Where is a time to be wanton and a time to get drunk? But those things can be well done in their own time: therefore they have their time. These have no time, because they are never to be well done. But perhaps it may seem to be the contrary that there has been set down a time to kill, a time of war, a time of hatred. But all these can be well done; and therefore they have their time, since they are to be justly done. For there is also a sword of justice, [0214C] with which kings and princes punish criminals out of zeal for equity, and put to death men condemned for their crimes, serving God in this very thing by a power received according to the precepts of the divine law. And these wage wars for the defense of the fatherland and the peace of the Churches, and fight down the enemies of peace, lest they oppress the innocent. There is also a holy hatred by which crimes are hated, not men; and this sometimes, for a wholesome terror, inflicts a strict severity outwardly for the correcting of vices, and inwardly preserves inviolate the sweetness of love, by which it thirsts for the salvation of men. Therefore there is a time of hatred, and a time of war: and all things have their time, when they are to be justly done. It is permitted me to confess what I confessed also at the beginning: that for these high and lofty matters I am too small in understanding to tell what is fitting, because [0214D] as for what is not fitting and does not agree, it is enough to have turned aside from this, if it be granted. For many are the secrets hidden in the deep of wisdom, and man is not able to search out her mysteries; and the words of wisdom, surpassing all understanding, scatter some light concerning the things that lie hidden, and that light is small in comparison with the whole. Therefore man labors when he sees it, and thinks to enter in that he may contemplate; and he is not able, unless wisdom be with him, and show him within the secrets of her ways in that part in which he has been made acceptable to her. And when he has drunk much, this much is much to him, and it is itself scant in comparison with her overflowing fullness. Therefore we have said many things about the waves of things and about the contrary agitation of those things that are under heaven; and all this is still little in comparison with the whole; and there will perhaps be those who will reckon us too lavish [0215A] in speaking, because they do not attend to the multitude of things to be said, and to the profundity of the things that have been said. We have spoken this briefly, that we may bring back again a fresh utterance to the beginning of the narrative to come, and may have our minds more eager.



## HOMILY XIV. *A declaration of the remaining things that have their own time, and a repetition of what has been said.*

*All things have their time, and all things under heaven pass away in their own spans.* Ecclesiastes had sought above some one thing in the world in which he might have rested in mind; and he did not find it among all things, whether because each one was imperfect, or because all were transitory. Therefore he began to loathe [0215B] and to reject all things; and yet he did not cease always to seek in them a full joy, and he could not find it. At length therefore, coming at last to know the condition of mutable things, he saw that he had erred in both: whether in that he charged those things which in their own kind were good with being evil, or in that he supposed that those things which were transitory could stand forever. Now therefore, correcting himself for his past estimate, he professes that all things under heaven are at once both passing away with time and good in their own time. And that the mutability and fluctuation of all these may be the better understood, how great it is, he shows that all these things not only pass away with time, but also with time go over into their contraries, saying: All things have their time, and all things under heaven pass away in their own spans. And [0215C] he then enumerates them all, distinguishing them in their own kind: *A time to be born and a time to die.* First he describes the course of mutable things according to its beginning and its end, that afterwards he may pursue more carefully the mutability and vicissitude of the things that turn in the middle. Now there are four works, by which all temporal things and all things passing away with time are unfolded. The first is the work of God; the second is the work of nature; the third is the work of the craftsman together with nature; the fourth is the work of the craftsman alone, without nature. The work of God is to create the essence of things out of nothing, to dispose the matter of things into form, and to temper the motion of things under a fixed order. Therefore these three belong to the work of God, [0215D] that is, the essence of things, their form, and their order. And these three, as the author himself afterwards testifies concerning the works of God, are stable, and do not admit the mutability of time; because the essences of things never cease to be what they are, and the forms of things, according to the first institution of their Maker, keep in their kinds a perpetual identity, and the motions of things never overstep the law of the first order. For neither could the essences of things be nothing, nor the forms of things be otherwise, nor the order and disposition of things from the beginning be otherwise than they are; but all things keep the norm of the first law and the covenant of the ancient institution; never running otherwise, they all still guard them. Therefore then let the works of God abide, because that which God made is stable in [0216A] all things, so that it may remain always. The work of God, therefore, is to create, to form, to dispose all things. The work of nature is to bring forth the seeds of things out of the hidden bosom by increase, and again, when the same wither and fall through failure, to call them back to the hidden bosom whence they had come forth. The work of the craftsman together with nature is to help by care and industry the things that rise from the earth. The work of the craftsman alone is to work upon the underlying matter of things, either by composing what is disjoined or by separating what is joined; so that it can neither create with God, that there be what was not, nor with nature bestow increase upon the offspring of things, so that greater be that which was less [sic: *crat*]

There are therefore three workers in this world: God, nature, and the craftsman who imitates nature. But these three [0216B] bring about what they produce to effect with very unequal power. For God in his work needs neither the work of nature nor that of the craftsman who imitates nature. For God sometimes works without nature, sometimes in nature, sometimes above nature. For without nature he first made nature itself. For when he made nature, he had no nature working with him. For that which was not yet could not itself work together with him who alone was. Thus therefore he first made nature without nature, so that afterwards, in nature, he might make some things with nature, others above nature. For with nature he makes those things which he brings forth from nature according to nature; above nature he makes, when in nature, beyond nature's accustomed course and first power, by a greater power added, [0216C] he brings something forth to effect. When therefore God works nature, and when he works above nature, nature does not work together with God; because there nature only receives that it should be what it was not, but here it receives that it should be able to do what it could not do out of what it had received. God therefore can work without nature; nature cannot work without God, because what nature does, it does out of that which God does, and together with that which God does. For God made the first power of nature; and that it may come forth to effect, he again himself does it together with that very nature which he makes. Everything therefore that nature does, God does; but not everything that God does, nature also does: he who without nature makes nature. In the third place there follow the works of the craftsman [0216D] who imitates nature; and he indeed sometimes works with God without nature, sometimes with nature without God, sometimes with God and with nature together, sometimes without God and equally without nature. With God he works when he works the works of justice, because God works together with a right will, one ordered according to justice. Without God he works when he works the works of iniquity, since sins are not from God, nor does that which is done against his will come to be through his cooperation. With nature the craftsman works when he applies outwardly his industry and care to the propagating of the seeds and offspring of things, to which nature supplies the increase. Without nature he works when, besides the things that look to the propagation of seeds, and to the tending of things being born [0217A] and of those things that have the power of growth and of sense, he unfolds his effort upon the underlying matter, so as to advance something, whatever it be, to effect; in which nature only suffers, and does not work, because it furnishes the matter to the worker, and does not exercise the effecting of the work. Such are all the works of men that are done upon the earth, of which many the necessity of mortal life compels, many desire urges, many vanity works. And in all these, because nothing stable is found, and all things pass away that are done under the sun — those which either nature brings forth, or the craftsman imitating [sic: *imitant*] nature fashions — the contemplator of wisdom subjects all things to vanity, proving that the works of God alone abide; since neither is a created essence ever consumed, nor is the reason of the disposition [0217B] or order of things changed. Therefore he shows first the works of nature made subject to vanity, whether in those things which nature alone works, because things that rise set; or in those things which the craftsman does together with nature, because things increased grow old. Afterwards he enumerates many and diverse works of the craftsman who imitates nature: some of necessity, some of desire, some of vanity; and he subjects all to vanity, because he shows them liable to mutability. The work of nature alone is told when it is said: A time to be born and a time to die.

Then the work of the craftsman working together with nature is brought in, when it is said: *A time to plant and a time to pluck up.* Last of all the work of man without the work of nature is added, when it is brought in: *A time to kill and a time to heal; a time to destroy and [0217C] a time to build.* And this is then set forth more manifoldly, with more things numbered up, that it may be plainly shown in how many things the vanity of the human heart is entangled and how widely it is scattered. For this pertained most of all to the matter: that the vanity of human minds, which follows the uncertainty of mortal life — because he had undertaken to reprove it — he should both set forth more diligently in the narrative, and set before the rest in the comparison; so that all the others might seem to have been recalled for this alone, that by comparison with them it might be recognized how great this one is. A time to be born and a time to die. It is a time to be born, when man is to be led into this life. It is a time to die, when he is to be called forth from this life. But meanwhile, after [0217D] birth and before death, while he lives in this mortal life, which needs sustenance and is nourished with food, that it may subsist. It is a time to plant and a time to pluck up, that man may till the earth from which he was taken, and sow, and reap, and plant, and gather. Now these two following things can also be compared with the two preceding by a distinction, in this way: A time to be born and a time to plant; a time to die and a time to pluck up. For when a man is born it is a time to plant, that earthly life may be fed with earthly fruits. But when a man dies, it is a time to pluck up; because when mortal life is destroyed, those things also which had been established for the sake of mortal life are taken away. And because to human infirmity, after the fruits of the earth, the use of flesh [0218A] is also granted, therefore after the time to plant and the time to pluck up there is fittingly brought in also a time to kill. And because in this eating a measure is necessary, lest the flesh take for an incitement to vices what it has received for the sustaining of its infirmity, immediately after the time to kill he adds a time to heal: namely, that in all other times we may study with careful concern to nourish and preserve the life of those very animals which we have received to be killed at the opportune time — that is, when necessity demands — for the sustaining of our infirmity. For this is, as it were, to heal the ailing, by coming to their aid in those things wherein they are not sufficient for themselves, and to repair what is failing. Whence perhaps, fittingly, when he had said, A time to kill, he did not add, a time to heal [0218B] what has been killed. As before, after he had said a time to plant, he immediately added, *and a time to pluck up what has been planted.* Namely because things planted can be plucked up, but things killed cannot be healed. Thus perhaps, if we follow the sense of the letter, we prejudge nothing of the spiritual understanding (which is to be traced out afterwards).

*A time to destroy, and a time to build.* When man ought to destroy and when to build, someone may reckon is by no means to be asked; since he may think that here this alone is shown: that man, if by chance he is subjected to various things by the events of the times, suffers these things unequally, even unwilling. But we, applying to an easier understanding these things which have been [0218C] said, can say that it is a time to kill when God, willing to punish the sins of men, brings the sword of slaughter for chastisement; but a time to heal when, cherishing again the afflicted and those wounded by the strokes of tribulations, he restores them with his wonted compassion. And plainly this may seem more consonant with truth, that here we ought to take the times of things not as those which serve the human will, but as those which obey the divine disposition. For neither is it the time of anything when man wills and God does not will. Because man often wills when it is not the time, and when God wills it is always the time. Therefore all times are referred to the divine ordinance; and it is said to be then the time of every thing, when it is justly disposed by God, [0218D] whether for the chastisement and correction of a crime, or for the consolation of infirmity. A time to kill and a time to heal. A time to destroy and a time to build.

*A time to weep and a time to laugh. A time to mourn and a time to dance.* Everywhere evils are enumerated first, afterwards good things are added; because first we are justly afflicted for our sins, afterwards we are kindly cherished again by God's compassion. And it is manifest in what way human life daily suffers the vicissitude of all these things, as often as by a hidden dispensation God either instructs us with scourges or consoles us with gifts. Then there follows:



## HOMILY XIV. *A declaration of the remaining things that have their own time, and a repetition of what has been said.* (cont.)

*A time to scatter stones and a time to gather.* But what this means according to the letter is rightly [0219A] asked, since such a thing, if it be thought to have a time among human actions, seems altogether idle and unfitting. Unless perhaps it is to be understood thus: that, because he is going to say in what follows, *A time of war and a time of peace,* he fittingly sets before it here a time to scatter stones and a time to gather; namely, that when a time of war is at hand the stones may be gathered for building fortifications and the ramparts of walls against sieges; but in a time of peace, because no perils of wars are threatening, the mounds of the fortifications may be scattered, so that access may lie open to all without hindrance. Nor ought it to seem superfluous, on the ground that he had said above, A time to destroy and a time to build, as though the same thing were repeated a second time; because that is taken as more fittingly said of the buildings [0219B] of houses and the dwellings of men, whereas this refers more properly to the defenses of walls. Yet perhaps another sense, more suitable, may be understood under these words. For because he had set before, A time to kill and a time to heal, lest perhaps it should be thought that only the evil, and those who are to be punished for their sins, are exposed to slaughter, he now shows that the just also are sometimes, by God's permitting, for an example and chastisement of the evil, subjected together with the impious and with sinners to deaths as well, and scattered by exiles and proscriptions, as is said through Peter: *It is time that judgment begin at the house of God [n: (I Petr. IV)]*. And that by the name of stones the just are signified is manifestly shown through Jeremiah, [0219C] when he says: *The stones of the sanctuary are scattered at the head of all the streets [n: (Thren. IV)]*. And Isaiah again: *I will make all thy walls of graven stones, all thy sons taught of the Lord [n: (Isa. LIV)]* [var: Vulg. *omnes terminos tuos in lapides desiderabiles*, "all thy borders of desirable stones"]. Therefore it is a time to scatter stones, that the just may understand from the very labor of their scattering and shaking that there is no true rest in this life. And again it is a time to gather stones, that after the wearing down of bodily exile, lest they fail in mind, when peace has been restored they may again grow strong by a temporal benefit unto things eternal. Thus what is hidden can with difficulty be judged toward any one side: since the divine word for this reason dissembles itself from us in certain places, that even there where it was thought to be manifest it may show itself worthy [0219D] of searching out.



## HOMILY XV. *On time and on the things subject to time, according to another interpretation.*

Let it not therefore irk us to attempt once again the hardness of these parables, until perhaps, the bark being worn away by the use of speech, the inward parts of its word may lie open. For we are in times, and our discourse rolls along with us, until we be made stable in that which is not changed by time; and this is, that we should contemplate above all human actions, because in them is the whole of vanity, over which we groan.

*A time to kill and a time to heal.* What were the times of killing and the times of healing? A pitiable beginning of human actions, from slaying and [0220A] slaughter. So must the work of a life given over to death have begun, so as to follow the sentiment of its author: *Accursed, raging unto its own slaughter.* Let us run over in mind the times past, and see the wars and slaughters made from the beginning until now, of nation upon nation and of innumerable peoples. And God bore with the human race raging against its own bowels; and in all these things he wrought his judgments, twisting the furies of men to the vindication of his justice, so that some justly suffered what others unjustly willed. And others rose up, and many were slain and were damned; and many were slain and were saved; and some were freed from the death of slaughter and were kept for damnation; and others were freed lest they be damned if they were slain. In all these he fulfilled his just judgment [0220B] upon them, so as to chastise and to spare according to the governance of that equity by which God disposes all things. Therefore there is a time to kill and a time to heal; and some do what he willed, others suffer what they do not will, and the just Arbiter sits above, doing what he wills and disposing all things that he may be feared. A time to kill and a time to heal: *A time to destroy and a time to build.* How great things from the beginning have the lovers of this world built, and have called their own name upon their own land, that they might have a glorious fame among those who come after. For they labored with heavy labor in their works, that the monuments of their boasting might stand after them; and they prevailed to a certain point, leaving to posterity great signs of their industry; and there still appear certain traces of ruins among them, of ages [0220C] gone by; and we marvel at those very men whom we do not see, that they could do such things, and we groan the more over them, because, though they themselves were men created for eternity, they loved vain things and vanished away, because they were made vain in themselves. For they themselves perished, and after them their works fell, and others came in their place; and these too did great works, and after these went into nothing; and all that they had made was destroyed together with them. And again others rose up and built, and did not understand where the former generation was, and its works, so as to see the vanity which is under the sun. And God permitted them to labor in vain, and to build things that would perish, that man might understand his own vanity; that what has been [0220D] built be destroyed, and again that what has been destroyed be built; and that the work of man upon the earth be not stable. Therefore *all things have their time,* that man may be instructed concerning his own vanity, and concerning the uncertainty of the mortal life through which he walks.

*A time to weep and a time to laugh: a time to mourn and a time to dance.* Weeping and laughter pertain to the mind. But mourning — or, to speak more properly, wailing — and dancing pertain to the body. By weeping, then, and by laughter, the grief and gladness of the mind are expressed; but wailing and dancing signify these same things, only when they are shown forth by the motion of the body. A time to weep, and a time to laugh: [sic: *templus*] to wail, and a time to dance. All these things have their time among men, while this mortal life runs on; and God permits, by a varying lot and by unequal outcome, [0221A] according to the height of his judgments and the depth of his counsel by which he disposes us, that these things should run and be varied over us, alternating prosperity with adversity, and changing adversity into prosperity in due time. And glad times come, and men laugh and dance and think it is well with them; and they often forget their Creator, and do not consider that they are to die. For they look upon present things alone, and rejoice in them as though they were to abide always; nor do they attend to their own condition and to the doubtfulness of mortal life, nor do they think of the perils that are to come upon them. And therefore they sin securely, and pour themselves out into harmful pleasures, and walk after the desires of their heart in the gaze of their eyes, so as to do what is not fitting, and to multiply wrath and indignation. And God sees this, and sows over them [0221B] griefs and bitternesses upon the wantonness of ill-sweet pleasures; and he calls back and restrains them as they flow forth, that they may not go into utter estrangement. Then comes a time to weep, and a time to wail; and men grieve, and often lay the blame upon God when it is dealt well with them. But he dissembles their depravity, and after a time mingles prosperity again with adversity, lest they fail; and again, when it is time, adversity with prosperity, lest they presume; and this he often does, nourishing and exercising, chastising and cherishing man, that he may learn to be fearful, and may know that with the Lord is the power of life and of death. Therefore there is a time to weep, and a time to laugh: a time to wail, and a time to dance. And upon these there follows a time to scatter stones, and a time to gather. This time is enumerated after those, but [0221C] is ordered among them: because in time there are often together causes that lead to one reckoning of time. *A time to scatter stones, and a time to gather.* The whole world is an exile to those for whom heaven ought to have been the fatherland: yet the minds of mortals grow together with it by use, and men begin to love their own places, and their own lands in which they were born or reared; and a great forgetfulness of things eternal succeeds, for the sake of affection for things temporal; and unless God wiped away these ingrown sweetnesses of the passions, there was no way by which desire for him might enter in to us. Therefore he gives his judgments, and there come commotions and shakings of nations and migrations of peoples; and some rise up to snatch what is not their own, and others are plundered and scattered; and again the stronger, to whom the power has been given, when they too have come, [0221D] cast out those who had violently snatched what belonged to others, and possess what is not their own; and the world fluctuates within itself, so that man may see that there is here no stable dwelling, and may accustom himself little by little to draw back his mind and loose it from the bonds of earthly delights. Therefore there comes a time to scatter stones, that there be no stable dwelling upon the earth, and that man may be forced to sigh in his exile for the fatherland, where joys remain unshaken, and the security of peace is troubled by no infestation coming upon it. This, then, is what the scattering of stones does in its own time. But because human infirmity quickly collapses, and cannot long stand unharmed amid adversities, after the time of scattering there comes a time of gathering: that by the quiet of the season it may grow strong, being admonished in this very thing [0222A] to be wise, and to learn by experience how great are the joys of the rest to come; that it may hasten to that place where there is no fear of scattering, nor is the hardship of exile dreaded, to whom eternity of life in the fatherland has been given. A time to scatter stones, and a time to gather. Then again another time follows after that time; and others are named which themselves also have their time — often one time together with those times that have been spoken of, often, apart from those times, another separately in its own time.

*A time to embrace, and a time to be far from embraces.* A time has been given to chaste embraces, that flesh may be sown for propagation, and that the harvest of the human race, which daily throughout the whole world is reaped by death, may be repaired by the care of generation. For the covenant of the first institution, by which flesh was to be taken from flesh [0222B] without the contagion of the flesh, is after the sin of the first transgression confirmed in the flesh of sin by indulgence, not only for the fulfilling of a duty, but also for the conferring of a remedy: namely, lest the motion of the flesh, now boiling up too immoderately, should flow out more shamefully into every transgression, if it could nowhere be lawfully relaxed. A boundary is therefore prescribed, up to which the affection of the flesh may extend itself without loss of chastity: so that what is done in the chastity of matrimony by concession, even if it belong to infirmity, may yet not be imputed to shamefulness. But lest again the blandishments of pleasure turn the mind aside into forgetfulness of the Maker, by a wholesome dispensation man is sometimes suspended even from what is conceded: lest perhaps, if he gape too immoderately after these things that seem sweet in the present, he should not hasten to those things that are eternal [0222C]. Therefore a time to embrace has been given, and a time to be far from embraces. For a time to embrace has been given, that mortality may be repaired and the infirmity of mortals sustained; a time to be far from embraces has been given, that while the flesh is withdrawn from the delight of the flesh, the mind may be nourished unto spiritual delight. A time to embrace and a time to be far from embraces. For when the stones are gathered, it is a time to embrace; but when they are scattered, a time to be far from embraces: since for this reason the times of [sic: *augustiae*] have been given, that the afflicted flesh may temper itself from its own delights, and that afterwards, in the time of joy and consolation, it may chastise itself with fear and reverence from excess. There are also the embraces of dear ones and the fellowships [0222D] of friendly society, in which, apart from the experience of the flesh, a sweet companionship is bound together by a most welcome consent — and these too are loosed in their own time, lest the heart of man rest in man, until there come that covenant of eternal charity which is not loosed. On account of all these things there is a time to embrace, and a time to be far from embraces: so that in time man may not seek what is eternal, and that he who loves the society of a living man may always think of the separation of one who is to die.

*A time to acquire, and a time to lose.* What are the good things that are first acquired and afterwards lost? For certain good things had been lost and were afterwards acquired, so that thenceforth they could not be lost. And there were again other good things unlike the former, [0223A] which nevertheless seemed good: and there was truly something in them toward good, but not as the first good things which were true. And these good things were not the good things of man toward the true good: and man came, after he had lost the true, and acquired false good things, reckoning himself happy in them. For he willed to console himself and to have in time what eternity confers; and he gathered things that would perish, which he could not hold; and God granted this, that he might recognize the distance of the good, and be instructed by his own vanity, so as to turn himself to those things that are true. Therefore, then, God let go his heart, that he might gather together those things that cannot stand; and these very things, in their own time, he now grants to be acquired, now takes away when acquired: testing man in all his works, and showing that all things under heaven are vanity. For this a time is given [0223B] to acquire, and a time to lose: that while man has obtained what he had coveted, he may learn by experience what these joys can confer upon those who greatly esteem things that perish; but when he loses what he had, he may recognize that all things which could be taken from him against his will had been foreign to him even while they were possessed.

*A time to keep, and a time to cast away.* Temporal goods are to be kept in their own time, as much as the necessity of temporal life demands; but because in the death of the flesh everything that belongs to the pleasantness of carnal life is of necessity lost, it is worthy that even before death it be cast away by the will from the love of the heart.



## HOMILY XV. *On time and on the things subject to time, according to another interpretation.* (cont.)

*A time to rend, and a time to sew.* He who rends divides what is united; but he who sews joins together what is divided and [0223C] separated. All these things this man had foreseen to be done without ceasing in the life of men, so long as it lies subject to mutability: in which life the fellowship of human converse is now to be sought after, because it seems to confer something of usefulness; and now to be fled, because often the same brings loss. Social consent, therefore, has its own sewing in its own time; the zeal of a just contradiction has also its own rending in its own time: so that both with those who wish well and with those who do good we may be united in assent and in work; but from those who love depraved things and work iniquities we may be divided in mind and equally in working. But lest perhaps this sentiment seem the same as that in which it was said above, a time to embrace and a time to be far from embraces — since in that place likewise we asserted that the conjunction and separation [0223D] of social love was treated of — the things that were said there can not unfittingly be taken as signifying only love and affection, while the things that are recalled here are referred to the figuring of any fellowship whatever, or of any compact in which there is the same consent and the same pleasure of concord. Thus do the likenesses of things offer themselves for the understanding of wisdom: and wisdom herself uses her own works that she may manifest herself in us. There are many things that are said in parables and worthy of deep searching; but they do not all wish to come out, that they may always be sought within.

*A time to be silent, and a time to speak.* First a time to be silent, afterwards a time to speak. For wisdom goes out from [0224A] the heart, nor has the tongue from anywhere else what it may rightly bring forth, unless understanding first form the word. Therefore first there is a time to be silent, after that a time to speak: so that first, in silence, shrewd thought may gather wisdom into itself, before the tongue afterwards sends it forth by word to the hearing of others. For this reason too there is a time to be silent and a time to speak, since the hearers of the word sometimes receive it with desire and love, but sometimes without reverence despise it by casting it away. Wherefore it is a time to be silent, when we see hearers in every way impatient of admonition; but a time to speak, when we see that they either desire the word or at least are willing to bear it. For this reason also there is a time to be silent, because in those things which we cannot discuss we ought not [0224B] to presume. And likewise a time to speak: because as for other things that we can comprehend, it is necessary, for the instruction of our neighbors, not to keep silent. Whence not only the works of men, but their words also, follow the uncertainty of fleeting life, so that vanity occupies the whole and nothing stable or constant is left: since we can neither always say what we know, nor always retain what we have learned; and now it may be harmful to have kept silent the things that ought to have been said, but now it may be a fault to have said the things that ought to have been kept silent. Likewise we often cannot when we will, and we do not will to be wise when we can; but more often we ought not to do either, when we do it. In this there is great uncertainty, and the gloom of a deep ignorance: so that man is tossed from time to time, and does not understand his own time in all his work [0224C].

*A time of love, and a time of hatred.* Many are the things that are loved in this world, and likewise very many that are hated. And in all the things that are loved, few love truth; and in those that are hated, few hate iniquity. For this reason men love, and often think that they love well, and love ill: because in the things they love they do not love truth, nor do they love them on account of truth. Likewise very many practice hatreds, and boast of their zeal for justice and equity; and within there lurks strife in the heart, and a savage fury feeding itself on unjust hatred. And therefore love and hatred cannot at the first discern their own time: because they do not follow the judgment of justice and truth, but are borne by their own inclination into depraved appetites, so that they do what they will. [0224D] They love and they hate, they cherish and they are zealous, often for things they ought not, often when they ought not; and there comes a great confusion of love and hatred, and the heart is scattered into universal vanity, fleeing the things that are right and coveting the things that are depraved. Therefore a time of love and of hatred has been set before man: that he may understand and discern when, out of those things which according to the time can be good and evil, he ought either to seek after something or to turn aside from it. For outside the times there are certain things which are not changed by time so as to be able to be otherwise: and among these there are good things and evil. And the things that are good are eternal, so that they are always good; and the things that are evil are likewise eternal, so that they are always evil. It is not of those that it is said to thee: A time of love, and a time of hatred. For to those belongs eternal love, and eternal [0225A] hatred: since the things that are to be loved are there always to be loved, and the things that are to be hated are always to be hated. But meanwhile, now in time, while all things are uncertain, the things that meet us as we pass by change themselves according to the time, so that what had stood as fortunate for one gives way into evil for another; and what this man has grieved over as adverse, that man recognizes as having turned out happily for himself. Therefore in all these things that pass away with time there is a time of love and a time of hatred: so that man may not fix his heart upon them either by love or by hatred; things which, when held in hatred, can again be justly loved, and, when loved, can again be justly held in hatred.

*A time of war, and a time of peace.* On account of the time to kill, a time of war is ordained; on account of the time to heal, [0225B] a time of peace follows. And that was the first, but this was recalled last of all: so that the beginning and the end may be enclosed with all the labors and perils that lie between; so that the life of man, which passes through only to cease, may never be safe. For man went out that he might withdraw and not stand with God; and he made a covenant of love with the pleasure of this world, that he might rest in it. And God would not endure that covenant, that man should abide in estrangement; and he raised up against him the things he had perversely loved, that they might oppose and afflict him; and there came about for man a war with all things that are in the world, that he might find grief and affliction in them until he return to peace with God. Then all things will compose themselves to peace with him, so that the things that had been adverse to the exalted may be subject [0225C] to the humble. For at the first all things had been at peace, just as at the last all things will be pacified: so that in the middle the things that pass may run their course by an uncertain lot; until they come to this, that either, for one falling under judgment, all things be adverse forever, or, happily, consummation, all things be made subject. For where hatred will be eternal, there will also be perpetual war; and where there will be eternal love, there everlasting peace will stand fast. Nor will it be said there, a time of peace and a time of war, just as it will not be said, a time of love and a time of hatred: but there will be war, and peace; war without end, and without end peace. Let not man therefore reckon that war greatly to be feared to which peace can succeed; nor that peace true which a war following after can disturb: because all things that pass away are to be reckoned vain in this very thing, that in [0225D] good, and likewise in evil, they cannot always remain.

*What more has man from his labor?* Therefore the labor of man runs in uncertainty: because he cannot have more from his labor, lest man be content with those things that consist in his own labor; because all things are vanity, and pass away with time, to be ended with time.



## HOMILY XVI. *On the spiritual understanding of those things which have been said concerning time.*

But now it is time that, after the running through of the times according to the things that roll outwardly in time, we should also call to mind the spiritual understanding. For little by little [0226A] the discourse of wisdom advances toward the touching of spiritual things, that it may draw the exercised mind to inward things. We distinguished above, then, two lives: one according to the flesh, the other according to the spirit. Now the carnal life is prior in time, in that every man is born in the flesh before he is reborn in the spirit; and everyone who lives in the flesh either walks according to the flesh and has not yet attained the spiritual life, or walks according to the spirit and has renounced the carnal life. Wherefore every spiritual man has been made spiritual out of a carnal man; but not every carnal man has been made carnal out of a spiritual man. For the carnal life is always either alone or first, so that every man either holds it alone, so as never to begin the spiritual life, or, having had it first, exchanges it by a following pursuit of the spiritual life [0226B]. Each of these two lives has its own followers: the carnal indeed those who love the joys of the present life; the spiritual those who seek out the pleasantness of the life to come. Of these, the former cherish the flesh with the blandishments of earthly delights, while the latter exercise the spirit by the pursuit of virtues in the hope and desire of things to come. With these two peoples the world runs down to its end through the mutability of the times, until it come that by the appointed covenant of justice the judgment of both be made.
