For it pleased God, that he might show the riches of the glory to come upon the vessels of mercy, to lead along also the vessels of wrath in forbearance through a temporal mercy; and likewise to exercise those same vessels of mercy, that justice and truth may be fulfilled, meanwhile now with the troubles of temptations: so that every life may have [0226C] both what it may receive freely of mercy, lest goodness be left unknown, and what it may feel as strict of justice, that truth may be made manifest. Therefore God set before every man both good things and evils, that he might show forth both, namely mercy and truth: the one, by which without merit he freely bestows a benefit; the other, by which for evil merit he repays a worthy punishment. Therefore he disposed that man should receive both; and he left it in man's power which he would first receive. But he set a condition of choosing upon both, lest perhaps depraved concupiscence be excusable: so that if man chose temporal goods in this life for his pleasure, he should not be able to obtain the eternal; and if he willingly submitted himself here to the scourge of chastisement in the judgment of truth, in the [0226D] life to come he should not feel the torments of damnation. Now the reprobate, who love the temporal life and set its pleasantness before the joys of the everlasting life, embrace present goods with all their desire, and set all their happiness in them; but the elect patiently endure temporal griefs, that they may be able to come to the joys of eternity that have been promised; and in regard to those joys, whatever is transitory, if it seem bitter, they do not fear; if sweet, they do not covet. Whence it comes about that the lovers of the carnal life have good things first and afterwards receive evils; but the spiritual are first exercised with adversities and afterwards attain to good things. To the former the first things are good, the last evil; to the latter the first things evil, the last good. But because [0227A] again all things that pass away in time are confused and mingled, so that they keep no order, whether from good into evil or from evil into good, therefore now meanwhile between the beginning and the end a manifold vicissitude runs in: namely, that now after good things evils, now after evils good things, should befall by an uncertain lot both the evil and the good alike. All these things, while Ecclesiastes was setting forth a certain distinction concerning the rolling of the times and the vicissitude of the things that are varied in time, he carefully expressed for an example of all things, that he might show the lot of both the good and the evil together, and their advance running into contraries. For when he had said that all things that are under heaven are varied by the turning of time and are borne into contraries, he subjoined examples, and enumerated many things out of all, that [0227B] the nature of all might lie open. And among those things which he enumerated, there are certain in which good things and evils are recalled - which indeed in which evils and good things, so that the state and condition both of the carnal and of the spiritual may alike be recognized. For there are seven in which good things are first recalled, afterwards evils; and again seven in which evils are first spoken of, afterwards good things, so that the whole may run down on either side unto the end. For by the number seven this whole present life is expressed, which rolls on by the course of seven days unto its own end. The evil, therefore, have their sevenfold number, and the good likewise have their own sevenfold number; because this whole life of the evil is from good into evil, and the whole life of the good from evil into good, of those going [sic: *cuntium*]. Of which [0227C] life the beginning of the evil is in good and the end in evil; but of the good the beginning is in evil and the end in good, with the middle things fluctuating by turns. For the middle things are uncertain: and for this reason also that which has been set in the middle has been pronounced doubtfully, whether from good into evil or from evil into good. For the middle was a time to scatter stones and a time to gather: and this itself has received an ambiguous exposition, signifying either of war or of peace, so that the middle things may be left uncertain. For if in this the thing that is said first is evil, and what comes afterwards good, then there will be eight of those in which evils are named first and good things afterwards, and six in which good things are recalled first and evils afterwards; because the life of the good rightly passes over to the number of beatitude, while the intention of the evil, together with their life, does not exceed the labor of temporality. Thus the truth is [0227D] one, whithersoever you may twist the sentiment by interpretation. There are many other things which perhaps might fittingly have been said in this enumeration or distinction. But we, avoiding weariness as far as is possible, let us come to the setting forth spiritually of the things that have been said.

*All things have their time, and all things under heaven pass away in their own spans.* If man had persisted in obedience to the commandment, he would not be of the number of those things which have their time under heaven and pass away. For if he had not flowed down in mind through iniquity, he would have remained stable in body also through immortality. Nor would he then be under heaven, so as to be rolled about with the things that pass away with time; but cleaving to the highest and true good, he would not be subjected [0228A] to the moments of the times, being fixed by contemplation in the solidity of things eternal. For eternity itself was the heaven, where, fixed in mind, he could not be changed by time; because, lifted above the times, he looked down upon all things gliding away beneath him. But after he turned aside his heart, so as to subject it through concupiscence to mutable things, then, as though fallen from heaven, he began to flow downward together with the things to which he clung, and, led through the rollings of his own mutability, to feel the moments of the times. Hence the human mind was made liable to times, so that in it now evils have their turn along with good things — the mind which good things alone before possessed, quiet and unshaken, without vicissitude. Therefore all things have their time, and all things under heaven pass away in their own spans, so that he too who through love subjected himself [0228B] to mutable things, agitated by the law of others with the waves of his own mutability, cannot stand at rest. Thus therefore the times came into the human heart, when many desires entered [sic: *introicrunt*] into it, and mutability began to hold sway there; and some came and others departed, and there arose a great fluctuation and inconstancy. For now the invisible world was rolling along with the visible world, and the moments of the times began to run their course within it, and there began to be years, and months, days and nights, sowings and harvests, rains and droughts, fruitfulnesses and barrennesses. For all these things are carried out in the nature of spiritual beings, the likeness of corruptible things having been brought in by the harm of sin, so that a nature made for eternity, wrongly associated with that which passes away temporally, likewise fails into alteration. For the shoots [0228C] of the virtues rise from the first root, and go on to grow and to come to the effect of consummation. And against them come the risings of vices and the cockle sown over them; and they lift themselves up to choke the things that are right, that they may not prosper; and there comes a great expectation, and a heavy peril mingled with hope and with fear. For if God pour out the rain of grace and the droppings of his truth upon a thirsting soul, there rises a harvest of virtues with manifold increase; and years of fruitfulness come, and calm seasons shine out, the cloud of ignorance being driven away. But if in anger he take away his benefits, and hold back his blessing that he may exercise his judgments, then sad seasons follow, and the good [sic: *genimima*] wither in the drought, and the thistles and thorns of vices are multiplied for labor and grief to sinful [0228D] man. And in all these things the sinful soul suffers its own times, looking out upon the heaven above, which it has lost, unmoved in its eternity, while it itself is rolled downward, temporal; and in it all things which cannot stand pass through by their own spans and measures. And therefore all things have their time, and all things under heaven pass away in their own spans.

*A time to be born and a time to die.* This is the lot of sinners in time: that if by chance any good things are born in them, afterwards they die, and do not persevere unto the end. For sometimes even in the evil some good things are born, and begin as it were to rise and put forth a shoot; but once born they soon dry up, because they have no moisture. For they believe for a time, and in the time [0229A] of temptation they fall away, so that they have a time and do not come through to eternity. A time to be born and a time to die. For the depraved there is a time to be born in good, when through a sudden fervor of mind they begin some right things; but there follows them straightway a time to die, because, not having perseverance in the good, by the same lightness of mind with which they had begun, in the time of impending pressure they abandon the good things begun.

*A time to plant and a time to pluck up.* Good things are planted in the evil, when through exhortation or right admonition they sometimes take up good things; but they are plucked up afterwards, when, shaken by a depraved suggestion, they lose the good things they had begun. They are born, therefore, by their own will; they are planted by another's admonition; but they die when by their own desires [0229B] they grow lukewarm toward the good; they are plucked up when they fall by another's supplanting. And in all these things the evil have their own times, in which by a just judgment they are disposed, whether to this or to that; and everywhere good things go before, and evils follow after for those foreseen unto evil. Then follows the life of the good, in which evils go before that good things may follow after, and those very evils that run before work together unto good.

*A time to kill, a time to heal.* In the elect the old man is first killed, and the carnal life which lives according to the affection of the flesh; then he is healed who according to the spirit is the new man, that he may live unto God.

*A time to destroy and a time to build.* Every man first makes an evil building, when [0229C] he works in the flesh, and daily adds evils to evils, so that a depraved building rises; and this must be destroyed and overthrown in those who are saved, that justice may be built in them.

*A time to weep and a time to laugh.* First a man ought to weep for the depraved things he himself has committed, that afterwards he may laugh securely in the good things he has received from God. For unless a preceding weeping wash the conscience in contrition, in vain does laughter exult over the things that are glad outwardly, without inward pleasantness.



## HOMILY XVI. *On the spiritual understanding of those things which have been said concerning time.* (cont.)

*A time to mourn, or to wail, and a time to dance.* First the flesh ought in penitence to be afflicted with torments for its depraved delight, afterwards to be exercised in good works in the pursuit of virtues. Thus assuredly the life of the just is instructed by griefs, and is prepared by punishments for joys, that [0229D] it may come clean to its own fruit; and its evils always go before that they may pass away, and its good things are deferred to the end that they may remain without end. And we can consider in this narrative a certain order of the correcting and amending of a soul that is to be justified. For the first of all is that in him who is corrected the depraved will be slain, and then the good, either infirm, be healed [sic: *sanctur*], or dead, be made alive. For what lives ill is well killed; and what is ill infirm is well healed. After the will has been changed, the ill-built mass of depraved works is destroyed, and the pursuits of good works, scattered by long negligence, are rebuilt. Then, for past fault, weeping follows, which, when it has cleansed the conscience with a fountain of tears, [0230A] is at last changed into the laughter of consolation and into joy. There follow also in penitence wailings, or mournings, when the grief of the mind is carried on even to the affliction of the flesh, so that afterwards, with the exultation of the mind, the flesh also, though worn down, may be gladdened by its own consolation

*A time to scatter stones and a time to gather.* If by stones we understand the strong works of the virtues, what else ought we to understand in the scattering of stones but the multiplication of good works? For to scatter stones is to offer far and wide, to the knowledge of many, the examples of strong works. But to gather stones is, after the pursuit of labor, to receive the fruit of good works. It is therefore a time to scatter stones, and again a time to gather stones; because a man ought first to exercise himself in good works in the pursuits of the active life [0230B], that he may be able afterwards to receive the fruit of his own work in the taste of contemplation. And because that taste is most pleasant to those who enjoy it and full of the highest delight, so that it seems to excel all the things that can be loved, there is fittingly subjoined at once:

*A time to embrace.* For there are certain embraces of wisdom and delightful bonds of inward love: when the mind, admitted within, lays hold of the joy of its own love, and, binding it fast with certain arms of its desires, makes a covenant and demands a compact of perpetual abiding. But because mortal life is quickly cut off and driven back from the sight of the inward light by the gloom of its own darkness, and by the mutability of its own condition is separated from the fellowship of eternal stability, [0230C] there is fittingly subjoined:

*A time to be far from embraces.* For after the time to embrace there follows a time to be far from embraces; because the human mind, although sometimes, lifted up by grace, it is admitted to the touch of inward [sic: *dulcedidinis*], yet, soon pressed by the weight of its own infirmity, slips back again to earthly things and to its accustomed thoughts. Although this sentiment may seem to belong particularly to the evil, in that it recalls as it were prosperous and good things at its beginning, but adverse things at its end, yet let it suffice to have observed this distinction in the preceding six which we have expounded, so that we may apply the rest that follow rather to a general signification. For one thing is noted by the disposition, and another is expressed by the signification; because the disposition [0230D] of the order suggests as it were a distinction of persons, whereas the signification shows by the sentiment of things only the truth. Therefore what is told of the evil only, or of the good, with the order of speaking changed, is not unfittingly taken without distinction of the good and the evil alike, so that sometimes this even belongs rather to the good according to the signification, which, so far as pertains to the order of the narrative, shows forth the life of the reprobate. Yet we can also take this very thing as not unfittingly said of any of the reprobate: who, even if they sometimes seem to draw near, as it were, to the embrace of wisdom, by the love of the good according to a certain affection having the appearance of virtue, yet because they do not divide their mind from carnal [0231A] desires and earthly cupidities, quickly slipping back to their accustomed ways, in that very thing wherein they seemed to have drawn near, they are made far off. As therefore the elect are for a time held back from the embrace of the highest truth, that by the very deferring of it they may burn the more with desire of it, and at last may more fully grasp what they have desired, so the reprobate too are for a time in a certain manner permitted — that they may see whereby they are confounded, and that malice may not be excused — to approach to the touching of it by some knowledge. But this is the difference: that the just and the elect, after they have tasted the sweetness of the inward good, are for this reason for a time driven back, that they may burn the more with desire; whereas the reprobate, after they have recognized the truth, are cast away, that from that very knowledge they may take against themselves the testimony of their own damnation. The former, then, are admitted [0231B] and driven back that they may be provoked; the latter are admitted and cast away that they may be confounded. Thus the philosophers of the gentiles were admitted in very great part; but they did not touch wisdom from near at hand, nor did they draw her to themselves that they might fully enjoy her sweetness. For they saw and knew, and thought that they loved; but it was a foreign love from far off, commending her appearance, but not drinking in her sweetness. From afar, as with arms stretched out and hands spread wide, they wished to make an embrace; and the beloved was not between the breasts, that he might be strongly held fast, and make delay, and abide; and therefore quickly and swiftly he fled from them, nor could he be held back by a love that was a stranger. The beloved fled, then. But the beloved does not flee that he may escape; rather he only hides himself, and conceals himself for the provoking of love, [0231C] that he may be sought in desire and not found, until he be most ardently loved. And often he returns again as though about to abide, and offers himself to be experienced; and after a little he slips away again, reserving the fruit for hereafter. And in all these things there is a time to embrace, and a time to be far from embraces.

*A time to acquire, and a time to lose.* When is it a time to acquire, save when it is a time to embrace? And again, when is a time to lose, save when it is a time to be far from embraces? For when the beloved is present, the increase of all the virtues is multiplied, and the inner house is filled with abundance and richness. Then barren things bear [0231D] fruit, and the things that had withered are fattened with fruitfulness, and the soul itself marvels at the riches it possesses. Holy affections blaze, and chaste desires, while adverse motions are lulled to sleep; and whatever had been wont to fight back, or to stand in the way importunately, is made far off, so that all things are at peace and quiet. The power of doing well is supplied according to one's wish, and the abundance of a rich will overflows even to the multiplication of holy works. This therefore is a time to acquire, when holy merits are heaped up, and the wages of good works are multiplied in the treasuries of eternal riches. But lest perhaps continual increase should in some measure cast down the human heart through elation, there follows a time to lose: that while by a wholesome dispensation the increase of the virtues is taken from him for a time, he may be the better [0232A] made solid in virtue through humility. And thus the holy mind, resting within in the embrace of wisdom, is increased with the riches of the virtues; but outwardly, scattered through occupation, it is pinched with want and worn thin by poverty: just as, on the contrary, carnal men gather by their labor riches that will perish, and when they have begun to be at leisure for the embraces of pleasures, they soon consume the same by living idly and luxuriously. We can also fittingly take the times of acquiring and the times of losing in another understanding. For there are two lives, namely the active and the contemplative: of which the one, that is the active, in the pursuit of good works gathers by laborious exercise the gains of holy merits; but the other, that is the contemplative, in the knowledge of truth [sic: *peccata*] rests by love. Which lives indeed we rightly understand to have been signified by [0232B] the two wives of Jacob, namely Leah and Rachel: of whom the one, that is Leah, is said to have been of bleared eyes indeed, but fruitful; whereas the other, that is Rachel, is reported to have been of beautiful eyes, but barren. For the active life is fruitful in the offspring of good work, but the contemplative is clear-sighted in the knowledge of truth. And because in each life the virtue of the saints is exercised by turns, and is turned about by a kind of alternating change — namely, that now its patience is proved in the labor of work, now its desire is refreshed in the sweetness of contemplation — rightly is it now said: A time to acquire, and a time to lose. For it is a time to acquire when the devout mind multiplies in the pursuit of good work the gains of merits, and then as it were a time to lose follows, [0232C] when for love of inmost quiet it willingly bears the losses of working. For as it were willingly it sets aside the riches of good work, that it may be able to rest, even with little, in the sweetness of contemplation. Yet from this nothing of its merits is diminished, which it earns more by the very desire and love of the highest good than by the pursuit of work. A time to acquire, and a time to lose. Concerning any of the perverse it is a settled thing: that if at any time they acquire something of good by acting rightly, they quickly lose it, because they have no perseverance in the good begun. To whom assuredly the time of acquiring could profit nothing, because for them there followed the time of losing the things acquired. And these a greater loss often follows in this very thing, because for the things they have acquired they swell up, but for the things they lose they are not humbled.

[0232D] *A time to keep and a time to cast away.* There is a time that God should keep his elect from temptation, because perhaps they might have been broken by adversities; and again there is a time to cast away, that when they are to be exercised with troubles he may, as though angry, wholesomely expose them for a time to temptation. But God also often keeps the evil safe and quiet in this life from the troubles that press upon others, that afterwards in [sic: *judicie*] he may cast them away the more heavily, the more patiently he bears them here also, while he protects his adversaries. But man himself too ought to keep many things for a time, which afterwards in their own time he should cast away: things which, kept in their own time, can bring aid to him who keeps them; and again, unless in their own [0233A] time they be cast away, can make a hindrance. For earthly substance is rightly to be kept for a time and for the sustaining of the flesh; and again in its own time, if peril to the soul is known to stand in it, it is without doubt to be cast away. There are also many things in spiritual discipline and in the pursuit of virtues which for a time one must now keep, now cast away: lest perhaps, if a work of virtue be done without discretion, that very thing be reckoned to fault which might have been counted for merit. For all things are changed with time, and there is not anything that always remains the same; and therefore he who lives under time ought to weigh the causes and outcomes of all things against their times.



## HOMILY XVI. *On the spiritual understanding of those things which have been said concerning time.* (cont.)

*A time of rending, and a time of sewing.* Everyone who lives in the flesh according to the flesh is, through the affection of the flesh, [0233B] as it were one with those things which the flesh covets. It is necessary therefore that the man who is turned from carnal things to spiritual should first be torn away in mind from those things to which he had clung by affection. For then indeed he freely glues himself to spiritual things by love, when nothing is left over of earthly affections to turn his mind aside into alien desires. And so it is a time of rending, that first a man may tear his mind away from earthly desires; afterward a time of sewing, that in mind, through spiritual love, he may begin to cleave to eternal and abiding goods. Nor does this seem to be passed over, that the things which are rent are substantially one; whereas the things which are sewn are indeed essentially diverse, but by the mediating bond of the thread become as it were accidentally one. For so every [0233C] man is united to corruptible and perishable things by nature, but is made a partaker of eternal things by grace. For thus human things and divine are sewn into one, so that what had been two by nature become one by grace. Compunction indeed is the needle itself, and love the thread; and the needle makes the way, and pierces both, that the thread may hold both together. For the sewing could not have been made otherwise, unless both were bored through and both held together, since the one would depart from the other, and would easily divide itself from unity, if the bond did not hold both. For this reason the needle of compunction pierces both, and the thread of love binds both together, that they may stand together and not depart from one another. For the needle of compunction pierces human things [0233D] when it pursues fault; it pierces divine things when it searches out the hidden things of eternal things. For thus compunction stands twofold, and there is an upper watering and a lower watering *(Josue, XV)*. Through the upper watering divine things are pierced, and through the lower watering human things are pierced. For through the compunction which rises from fault the sinful conscience is transfixed; and through the compunction which rises from the desire of eternal things the hidden things are penetrated. The one subtly penetrates the conscience, that it may drive out harmful things; the other by deep consideration searches out invisible things, that it may lay hold of the things it loves. So both are bored through, and both are transfixed: the one by grief, by which guilt is rooted out, the other by desire, by which the things coveted are sought out. After these follows the thread of love: [0234A] by which on both sides the bond of unity is effected, so that things thenceforth joined may not be separated from one another. For love rises on both sides, and the bond of love runs together into one, because the human mind, looking back, sees there how great things have been entrusted to it through mercy; but considering divine things, it contemplates in them how great things have been promised it unto glory. And it loves both, and the knot of charity is made in both, because mercy is sweet unto glory, and glory pleasant unto mercy. And the love of these cannot be separated, because the sewing has been made, and the bonds of eternal charity stand firm. The time of rending therefore was then, but now is the time of sewing.

*A time of keeping silence, and a time of speaking. He that heareth,* saith the Scripture, *let him say: Come [n: (Apoc. XXII)]*. For he who [0234B] first opens the ear of the heart by obedience to the word of God, he afterward rightly looses his tongue to speak to his neighbor. For he who has not first learned by obedience to keep silence before another in the word of edification is convicted of lying under blame, if he himself presumes to teach others. Therefore first is the time of keeping silence, that a man may learn what he is to teach; and then the time of speaking, that he who has now known the truth may not blush to speak it in its own time. For each is a fault: namely, both when a man rashly presumes what he ought not, and when through negligence he is sluggish about that which he ought. And therefore we must first beware lest we commit unlawful things, and afterward take pains lest we neglect what is due. A time of keeping silence, and a time of speaking. Times succeed one another, and a time comes after times.

[0234C] *A time of love, and a time of hatred.* If anyone shall say that in the time of love there is a time of speaking, and that in the time of hatred there is a time of keeping silence, so that what is holy be not given to dogs, nor pearls cast before swine [n: (Matth. VII)], but that the just man set his finger upon his mouth when the days are evil, and be dumb when the sinner stands against him: has not justice therefore come upon the earth, that sinners may be reproved, and that the wicked may be converted, lest they perish? For this reason times are ambiguous, since it belongs to them to run their course into uncertainty. Yet nothing is without a time, whether it be love or hatred. For love has its time, and hatred its time. For to him who is commanded to love his neighbor as himself, to the same it is said that he should hate himself. [0234D] Whence it stands that he who loves God as himself, when in God he loves his neighbor: so, against God, he does not know how to love even himself. There is therefore a time of love, and a time of hatred, so that we who for God's sake strive to love our enemies, when God's cause is injured, may not presume to love even our friends. There is yet another love, and another hatred: that each man both love his own flesh, and that in those things which are necessary for sustaining its weakness, and hate it in those things which demand depraved desires. And so there is a time of love, and a time of hatred, since it behooves us, so long as we live under the defect of this mortality and carry the contradiction of sin in our flesh, to weigh with great discretion applied, when we ought by compassion to cherish our failing nature, lest it fall away, and when again [0235A] to press down by afflicting it the motion of vices rising up in the flesh, lest it grow strong. For it is written: *No man ever hated his own flesh [n: (Ephes. V)]*. And again it is said: *Make not provision for the flesh in its desires [n: (Rom. XIII)]*. Whence it is plainly shown that the flesh is both to be loved, so far as pertains to compassion for nature, and again, so far as regards the chastisement of fault, to be hated. And because in this there is need of a great governance of discretion, a time of love and of hatred is prescribed, so that walking along the middle boundary we may neither smother nature while we pursue fault, nor nourish fault when we cherish nature. But because this cannot be done without great toil and heavy wrestling, there is subjoined: A time of war, and a time of peace, that a man may at once acknowledge both that no one without a fight [0235B] comes through to victory, and that he who lawfully strives rests in peace after victory is obtained. For the time of peace is set after the time of war for this reason, that he who could be broken by the weight of the struggle may be consoled by the gift of peace. A time of war, and a time of peace. There are three who stir up war against us: namely the devil, and the world, and our flesh. For Paul knew that he had all these as enemies, who asserted that he had undertaken a fight against them all. For he testified that the fight against demons was continual for him, equally with all the faithful, when he said: *For our wrestling is not against flesh and blood: but against the powers and principalities; against the rulers of these darknesses, against the spiritual things of wickedness in the heavenly places [n: (Eph. VI)]*. Again against the world, that [0235C] is, perverse men, lovers of the world, he asserted that there had been a fight for him, who related that he had fought against beasts at Ephesus [n: (I Cor. XV)]. Likewise he testifies that against his own flesh there is for him a ceaseless fight, saying: *So I fight, not as one beating the air; but I chastise my body, and bring it into servitude: lest perhaps, when I have preached to others, I myself become a reprobate [n: (I Cor. IX)]*. There are therefore three tyrants who lead forth their armies into battle against us: and each has his own battle lines drawn up for making war upon the soul. The devil indeed draws up columns of suggestions against the faithful soul; the world brings forth prosperity and adversity to overcome us; but the flesh, stirring up crowds of carnal desires, rises up into battle against us. But against all these [0235D] the faithful soul must contend manfully, and, fortified by God's protection, must ply the javelins of the virtues to war down the forces of the adverse power. For we conquer the devil, if with perseverance we refuse to receive his suggestions. We conquer the world, if we both steadfastly despise its prosperity and patiently endure its adversities. And we conquer the flesh, if we do not consent to its depraved desires. But enemies once conquered do not at once cease, so that there might be peace continually; but they attempt again, and again they contrive, and try how they can: and often, as if daring, though they are timid, they themselves make an assault with terror, that they may test what spirit is in us; often they lay ambushes, and advance secretly to explore, [0236A] and hide themselves, and wish to be hidden, until they come upon us unforeseen. And in all these things many perils arise, and daily circumspection, and fear on every side: and there comes to be a great war, and a long-lasting struggle, until our side grows strong in every way, and they, despairing utterly, cease and depart from their hope; so that they may either be frightened away from us, or be subjected to our dominion. For this also will come to pass without doubt, if we have not failed from our hope and from good patience, that at length a full and entire peace may be restored after the labor of the struggle is completed. For then the devil shall be put to flight from us by divine power, so that he dare not presume against us; but terrified he shall flee, and depart into confusion. The world too itself, which was wont to rage in its commotions, [0236B] shall be pacified, and the desires of the flesh, the spirit being strengthened, shall be lulled asleep. Then in the time of our peace we shall remember the ancient times, when there was war, and we shall not then repent: but we shall be glad, and shall exult for the days in which we saw evils; and there shall be sweet peace in thanksgiving for past labors. For this reason there is a time of war, and a time of peace

*What hath a man more of his labor?* What has he more than war and peace, love and hatred, and all the other things which the wheel of its own turning brings forth as the outcome of times? He has therefore no more of his labor than war and peace: since by this labor of his he cannot bring it about that he have peace [0236C] alone. Therefore neither by his labor does he have peace, just as by his will he does not have war. For all things follow the nod of the supernal disposition: nor are the moments of times subject to human choice, that this or that should come to pass; but man suffers the law in all things, whether willing, unto merit, or unwilling, unto torment. Man ought not therefore to trust in his labor, nor to place hope in his own deeds, as though he could himself either effect what has not been ordained from above, or hinder what has been disposed. For the labor of man can add nothing to the sum of things, that there should be anything more: because the just moderation of the Artificer has so disposed all things in equity, that everything which strives without his co-operation, deserting the order of the universe, is moved rather toward not-being.



## HOMILY XVII. *On the confusion of minds arising from the passing of times.*

[0236D]

Just now we have come out from the times, but would that we had come through to eternity! For many things still remain, and very many are rolled about us: and again the times repeat their circuit on every side, so that there is no end until all things shall have been consummated. For upon the mutability of things there follows a grievous confusion of minds: since hearts go along upon the rollings and passings of times, so that they do not stand in truth, but flow away with the things which go that they may pass. Therefore he ceases from all these things which totter without, and fluctuate, as though all these were small and not working greatly unto peril: and he turns himself inward, and there redoubles his complaint concerning the vanity [0237A] which is hidden: and he examines that one with grievous expostulation over the one which appears without as alien. For the mutability of things would not have harmed, if the mutability of minds had not been: since this very thing was good, that transitory things should change their appearance for a time, but it was not good that the judgments of human minds should desert truth. And so we do not lay the blame that times are various, since it is good: but we lay the blame that human judgments are various: since this is not good. For truth cannot of itself be other: because what is good in truth is not evil: and what is not good in truth, is evil. Why therefore is it judged that this is good, and this is evil; and is it judged that this is evil, and this is good: when in truth either [0237B] it is not good, if it is evil, or it is not evil, if it is good. One says, It is good, and another says, It is evil: and he who says, This is good, says That is evil: and he who says This is evil, says That is good: and all speak ill, because the whole is good. But this alone is not good, that they speak ill of that which is good. Wherefore all things are good: and those alone are evil who beyond all the rest ought to have been good. For all the rest are what they ought to have been, and they alone are not what they ought to have been: because they are not truthful, and are not in truth; and therefore all things are good in that which they are, but they themselves are not good in that which they are, because they are not what they ought to have been. This therefore, among the other vanities, is itself also reckoned for a great vanity, [0237C] and alone is set before all vanities by comparison. And he says:

*I saw the affliction which God gave to the sons of men, that they should be distended in it.* For what is that affliction?



## HOMILY XVII. *On the confusion of minds arising from the passing of times.* (cont.)

*He hath made all things good in their time: and he hath delivered the world to their disputation; that man may not find the work which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end.* Therefore the mutability of things does not afflict man, but his own disputation afflicts him. And rightly: because disputation always signifies restlessness and struggle. And therefore the mutability of things is not man's labor; but that he himself has been made restless, this is his labor. And that restlessness itself is the great disputation [0237D] which man has in his own instability, so that he does not feel the same thing: since he is divided, and made another, so that he is not one whole. Consider now the great disputation which man carries on upon the earth. For it is much, and long, and exceedingly involved: so that it cannot easily have an end, until man himself receives his end. And now, that you may believe, see how many sentiments concerning the works of God those curious of wisdom have formed from the beginning: and there has been no one until now who could impose an end upon this so great business. And they wrangle still daily, and fight with struggles as they dispute: and one says This is so, and another says It is not, but it is otherwise; and many things are said, and lies of opinions are feigned as though wrought by craft concerning the judgment of things. And very many assert [0238A] that there is nothing except that very thing which is seen, and others come, and testify that nothing truly is except that very thing which is not seen. And in all these things they heap up and multiply their arguments, and weave reasons, and as it were reasons: and each man's reason is his own estimation. For God has set forth his work before the eyes of men, that he may question their hearts concerning it: and he himself lies hidden behind, until they may make trial of our exercise in it. And they draw with sense, and judge with heart, and bring forth very many things out of the treasury of their judgments, and out of the deep abyss of their fantasies, and of their false opinions and empty thoughts. And some say that nature alone is, and there is nothing else; and God is nothing, but empty fear has devised all things; and so it always was, [0238B] what is from the beginning, and before the beginning without beginning. And the ages revolve, and nature works itself, and renews itself, and cannot be other than it always was. Others on the contrary cry out and wrangle for the wrong done to the Creator, and think that they defend him whom they themselves assail with their lies. They say that there is a craftsman who from matter co-eternal with himself has fashioned all things, that he might give a better form; and these men do not know the power of the Creator, but deny that anything has been made from nothing: and they deny that any of these things which are can slip back into nothing: but that the Founder's work consists only in the alterations of things. After these others follow, wrangling and disputing, and they promise to take away error and to make truth manifest; and these themselves confess that all things were made from nothing, and that he was not made [0238C] through whom all things were made; and that there was once a time when nothing was created, and that then he was who always was. And these men have in their teachings as it were a good beginning, but an evil end follows. For those who think as it were truly concerning the creation of things, lie exceedingly concerning the subsistence of things, and there is no end of the disputations and inventions of men. They feign essences, and forms, and atoms, and ideas of the principal constitutions, and very many elements, and infinite births, and invisible motions and procreative efficiencies. And in all these things the likenesses of reasons are multiplied, and very many disputations are made, and truth is not in them all. For likewise he who speaks, speaks falsely, and he who contradicts, [0238D] speaks falsely, because by disputing and by lying they have all by one vanity gone far away from truth. For neither shall man be able to find the work which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end. Out of all the things which have been made from the beginning and which are to be made unto the end, man will not be able to find any one thing so as to comprehend it, and know the whole of what it is. For even if certain men seem to have found very many things, all things had been set forth, and the things which were hidden could in no way be found. For God delivered the world to their disputation, *and set before them his works, that they might see them and judge.* And in these he manifested many things which he willed to be known, and the things which he willed to be hidden could not be found. Therefore God manifested in them all the things which are known; and man did not find them [0239A] by his own sense, until they were revealed in him so far as God willed. For certain things were set forth without, and certain things were revealed within, and man found nothing, so as to go by his own sense to those things which were hidden, until they were made manifest in him. And if he should wish to contend that he himself found some things, could he nevertheless find all the things which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end? And in the very things which he found, could he find the whole of what was, and did there not remain over something hidden and secret to which his sense was not admitted, so that he might find the whole? For if man had found the whole, there would be no disputation at all, nor would opinions run in opposite directions concerning all those things which are in the world. For we all alike see this world: and it is placed before our eyes, [0239B] that it might be delivered to our disputation; and we see this whole before us, and we do not know whence it came hither. For he who made it lies hid, and stretches his work out before himself, that he may be hidden, and be made manifest in it. For the image that has been made, receiving human contemplation from near at hand, brings it about that one can neither see his Creator manifestly, nor likewise be wholly ignorant of him. Therefore the understanding of man, exercised by the appearance of things, rises up, and from that which offers itself to manifestation strives, according to its emulation of truth, to search out inward things; and it has in the works of God the beginning of first contemplation, but it does not come through unto the end of the works of God, so as to grasp the whole of what is hidden. For if it had been able to come through to the end, it would assuredly have found God, since from him from whom [0239C] all things are, there do all things have their end and consummation. It does not therefore come to the end, since it could not find God, until he himself should manifest himself in us; and they that search searchings have been confounded, since they have failed [n: (Psal. LXIII)], and have not availed to comprehend and to make an end of their disputations. Therefore *God hath made foolish the wisdom of the world [n: (I Cor. V)]*, because he placed the world in their disputation, himself remaining hidden until the disputers and those seeking empty things should fail. And man shall not find every work which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end. After these things God revealed himself and came forth that he might be found, when man could no longer glory as though he himself by his own sense and wisdom had known God, who had failed in [0239D] searching out the work of God. To this end therefore it profited that the world was for a time delivered to their disputation, that man might not find [sic: *invenirit*] every work which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end. These are the disputations of men seeking with vain curiosity the hidden things of the works of God, which they multiply for themselves by fabricating lies concerning the things they do not know, and they fortify their errors with [sic: *concortationibus*]. And there are yet certain other disputations in the desires of men, exceedingly manifold and various, and out of them arise the pursuits of many vanities. For every man prefers what he himself loves, and the hearts of all are drawn into infinite rendings, so that they never agree in one. Some follow wantonness and luxury and pleasure: to be drunken and to play and to laugh and to dance, and [0240A] they reckon this to be the happiness of man and the sweetest delight. Others court honors, and dignities, and great fame among the peoples, and commend their own pursuits, and dispute with grievous struggle, that this portion is better in the life of man. Others heap up moneys and pile up wealth, and think to procure security, and great confidence against oncoming want in the time of straitness. And these men praise foresight and say that solicitude is better, though they wish nevertheless to be secure themselves. And thus indeed all are carried away into their own appetites, each one apart with contrary approval; and they dispute by quarreling, and by dissenting with importunate desires. And the world is delivered to their disputation, that in its affluence each one may make trial of his own pleasures and prove them, and [0240B] see, and consume them into arguments one against the other about themselves out of its plenty, each man of that thing which he himself shall have chosen in the approval of his own desires. And each one snatches with great obstinacy what he has chosen for himself; and each praises his own part, and there comes to be a great disputation and altercation of wills in the various estimations which many desires bring forth. And in all these things man does not find the work which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end. For the beginning of the works of God is the use of temporal things; but the end and consummation, the fruit of eternal things. Therefore he who establishes his disputation in the things of the world, and seeks out the approval of his own desires, cannot find in the works which God hath wrought anything from the beginning unto the end; since, [0240C] although he is allowed to make trial how great delight there can be in the goods of this world, yet that ineffable pleasantness which confers consummated happiness he does not lay hold of in the contemplation of God. For this reason the world has been delivered to the lovers of the world, to whom it is not given to deserve to find the works of God from the beginning unto the end; because to those from whom the sweetness of eternal goods is taken away, carnal delights have been given, and the joys of the world set forth unto the dissipation of their evil desires. We find yet other disputations in the judgments of men, grievous and manifold, and horrendous stirring up quarrels [sic: *rigas coacitantes*], so that they grow even into blasphemy of the Creator, and men say the things they ought not. For men violently seize the place [0240D] of God, and set themselves up as judges of the world, and dispute concerning the world and concerning the works of God, and his providence, and the judgments by which he disposes the whole world. And some say, God has done this well; and others murmur, and say, That was not well done. For these are the complaints of men, and the presumptions by which they provoke the wrath of God; because they will not be subjected to his laws, but dispute concerning his works, and reprehend his judgments. Someone has perished by a cruel death or by grievous loss, and has been crushed by a miserable chance; and his enemy comes and says: God has done well; he has judged an impious and sinful man according to his malice; and his friend murmurs, and raises a complaint against God, why he permitted an innocent man to perish, and did not guard the just man. And that man is condemned [0241A] for his malice; and this one is judged for his rashness. And yet men do not cease to dispute concerning the works of God, and to weigh his judgments by their own estimation; and God is reprehended by the thoughts of malignant men. For men say that God ought not to have created harmful things, nor to have placed among his works the things which are hostile and do harm; and they curse the good creatures of God, and blaspheme their Creator. Frogs, and flies, and serpents, and all venomous things, and all adverse and pestilential things — why did God make them? For he would have done better, if he had made all things good. For evil things are very many, and all evil things are not good. And he indeed made all things good in their time; but man alone is evil, not understanding goodness nor retaining the judgment [0241B] of truth, and with an evil will disagrees with goodness: and when that comes to pass which he himself evilly does not will, he says that it is ill done; whereas it is well done, and he himself evilly wills. Therefore evil wills and wicked concupiscences blind the hearts of men, so that they do not understand what is right, because they do not love nor will what is good. And they reprehend God because he made harmful things; and yet no one reprehends when his own enemy is hurt, though they themselves all are enemies of God, and adversaries of truth. For this reason man does not understand the work which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end. For *the carnal man perceiveth not the things that are of God [n: (I Cor. II)]*. For he follows his own concupiscence, and praises the delight of his own desires, and when what he loves is given him, he thinks that it goes well with him. when rather this comes to pass [0241C] unto his harm; and when his desires are withstood, he murmurs and raises a complaint; and he lays blame, as though it were ill done when it is justly ordained; and he does not understand the works which God works from the beginning unto the end. From the time when he is brought forth out of the earth, until he is called back into the earth: he does not understand the works which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end. For God works many things about man from his beginning and unto the end of him, and man does not find to what end all these things are done, until he himself receives his end. But then he shall understand, and then he shall find the things which God hath wrought in him from his own beginning unto his own end, whether in mercy or in judgment, that he might lead him to such an end as he approved by his works from the beginning unto the end, that [0241D] he should come into consummation. But meanwhile, until the end comes, from the beginning unto the end all things lie hidden, so that man does not find the work which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end. Therefore he can dispute concerning the works of God, which God works from the beginning unto the end; but he cannot find the work which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end. But if man cannot find the work which God works from his own beginning unto his own end, so as to understand at least this which he himself has seen, how then shall he find the works which God hath wrought from the beginning of the world unto the end of the age, which he himself could not see? Let man therefore cease to dispute concerning the judgments of God, by which he governs and disposes the whole world from the beginning unto the end; because man was placed by God neither as ruler [0242A] nor as judge of the world, but as possessor; nor that by his own strength or power he should rule the elements of the world or dispense the outcomes of times at his own choice, but that he should receive the fruits of the world, and the changes of times, according to the Creator's dispensation, for the use of nourishing his own weakness. Therefore he added and said:



## HOMILY XVII. *On the confusion of minds arising from the passing of times.* (cont.)

*I have known that there was nothing better than to be glad and to do well in one's life. For every man that eateth and drinketh, and seeth good of his labor, this is the gift of God.* For every man who contends and disputes concerning the works of God, and lays blame on the judgments of God, and accuses his dispositions in the world: this is not the gift of God, nor is it from God, but against God; and he does better who lawfully uses in this world the gifts of God acquired by just labor, [0242B] than he who quarrels against God over those things which in this world come to pass contrary to his own will or estimation. For man cannot by quarreling and murmuring against God change his disposition: and therefore it is better to receive his judgments with fear, than to murmur, in wrong done to him, over our own hurt. For to this end also God hides his judgments from us, that, while we can neither penetrate them by our own understanding nor change them at our own choice, we may fear the more. Therefore he goes on and says:

*I have learned that all the works which God hath made continue for ever. We cannot add anything to them nor take away the things which God hath made, that he may be feared.* Not only of the works of God by which he created the essences of things, [0242C] and imposed upon the things created, through their several genera and species, form and measure, is this true, that the works of God continue for ever: in that his disposition in them is not changed, and each thing keeps to being what he appointed it to be: and if they pass away, this is not from him, since what they are they are from him; but that they cease to be, they have from themselves: just as this also, that before they were, they were nothing, they had from themselves. And so not only is this true of the works of God by which he disposes the creation of the world, that they remain for ever without change and confusion of order; but also of the disposition and predestination of his judgments, by which he examines the deeds of men, this is true, that they [0242D] remain for ever, and he himself does not change the law of his own providence, and the decree of his own thought. And just as to his works, to the genera of things, we can neither add by creating, so that there should be what was not, nor take away by destroying and demolishing, so that what was something should be nothing at all: so neither can we change his judgments, either by adding, so that what he himself has not disposed should come to pass, or by taking away, so that what he himself has ordained to be done should not come to pass. For that he alone might be feared, and that at all times the regard of the human conscience might be toward him in mercy, therefore he has reserved to himself alone the power of his works and of his judgments, that no one may change what he himself has fixed by eternal laws, to be fulfilled. For this reason let not man dispute in all the things which come upon him, but let him receive the judgments of God with [0243A] reverence and fear: and when indeed God bestows prosperity and grants pleasant things, let him be glad with chaste exultation in his benefits; but when adverse things come forth, and troublesome things meet him, confessing his own evil deserts, let him commend God's justice and truth. But if at any time, when troubles press upon him, he has seen his own patience giving way, let him set against the vice of murmuring and impatience the gifts of his Creator's munificence: so that the mind which is pressed by the weight of temptation may, lest it break out in any degree into blasphemy, be softened by the remembrance of God's benefits. For then he will begin to do well, and to be glad in his life, and to see good things of his labor: not only when with thanksgiving he enjoys the goods of this world acquired by his own industry; but then also when, set [0243B] in adversities, he is glad, for the labor of his patience, in the hope of consolation to come. For he shall eat and shall be glad in the gifts of God, when in prosperity he shall exult over the gift, and in adversity shall be gladdened over the recompense. Thus at all times he will compose himself unto peace with God, so that he may see and understand that he makes all things good in their time, so that he may not justly be accused by man in all the things that shall have befallen. For his judgments are established: and of his laws, standing for ever, nothing great or small can be changed. For just as in all the genera of things which seem to be diminished or to perish, in those things which pass away, they are restored in the things that come upon them and succeed, so that the disposition of the universe may keep its own order established and immovable; and the ordinance of the first disposition may remain always [0243C] inviolable, so that the sure moderation of the works of God neither passes beyond nor remains short: so also his judgments run according to a sure providence, so that nothing can be added to them or diminished from them. And if at any time he himself seems to do otherwise, and to judge otherwise, there is a hidden dispensation by which the counsel is not changed, but the work is varied and the judgment exercised. For this very thing also is a judgment: that judgment is sometimes deferred, that in its own time it may be restored, so that nothing may perish of all the things which have been made, but that they may remain always. For this is always so, that fault has its punishment, and justice obtains its reward. But there is a hidden punishment, and a manifest punishment. Likewise there is a hidden reward, and a manifest reward. And often God tolerates the evil, and defers their manifest punishment: [0243D] and yet all malice has its hidden punishment. And God judges, and seems to defer judgment, or not to attend at all to the deeds of men, in the eyes of those who see nothing but the things which are without. Likewise God sometimes proves the just, exercises them with adversities and tribulations, and justice seems not to have its reward with him: and yet it never lacks its own hidden recompense: and the manifest one is sometimes deferred, that the consciences of men might be questioned concerning their perseverance. And because the things which are in the open run dissimilarly, the [sic: *eorda*] of men fluctuate, and they wonder where the judgment of truth is; when in this world innocence is oppressed, and malice prospers; for this reason he subjoined and said:



## HOMILY XVIII. *On the perverse morals of men: and what Ecclesiastes judged concerning them.*

[0244A]

*I saw under the sun in the place of judgment impiety, and in the place of justice iniquity. And I said in my heart: The just and the impious shall the Lord judge, and then shall be the time of every thing.* But because in human minds a grievous disputation has arisen concerning the judgments of God and concerning the contrary outcome of all things, and because the reasons must now be set forth why the hearts of men are moved against him, and rise up into murmuring, we shall briefly repeat what went before, so that of the things which are to be said we may speak the sentiment out of those very things as though from the beginning. Above, Ecclesiastes had shown that nothing in this world can stand perpetually in the same state; but that the changes [0244B] of times are hurried along without ceasing by contraries going their way: which mutability of things, although it be rightly ordered by the Creator of all things, nevertheless the human heart is through impatience shaken into murmuring and blasphemy against him. And therefore, after the times of things had been enumerated, he subjoined and said: *I saw the affliction which God gave to the sons of men, that they should be distended in it. He hath made all things good in their time, and hath delivered the world to their disputation.* This therefore is the affliction of men, their disputation against God; because, in that they will not be humbly subjected to the judgments of God, and contumaciously reprehend his disposition, which is good in all things, when anything is done contrary to their depraved will, not only does the punishment of adversity and the trouble of tribulation [0244C] wear them down outwardly, but much more does fury within, and the impatience of murmuring, afflict them. Which affliction God is said to have given them, not by corrupting their mind with depravity, but by withdrawing his judgments justly from their knowledge, that they themselves may be proved to be evil; so that, when they cannot see by what justice that is done which is done concerning them, they may the more, through the vice of their own impatience, be blinded away from the knowledge of truth. Therefore he gave them affliction when he gave disputation by concealing his judgments, that man might not find every work which God works from the beginning unto the end. For sometimes certain manifest causes go before the judgments of God in this world, when God, by a recompense that follows in the present life, either rewards the good deeds of men or punishes the evil. And in these alone [0244D] the judgments of God avail to be discerned in some degree, so that man may have some judgment of the hidden truth, in so far as he may venerate even those things which he cannot penetrate.

But when the preceding causes are hidden, or those which follow lie concealed, very many disputations and contradictions arise, and the depraved minds of men afflict themselves by quarreling and murmuring against God, since they cannot penetrate his judgments, nor find the work which God hath wrought from the beginning unto the end. For they cannot penetrate, in so great a darkness, to what end anything is done, even if certain causes seem to stand forth at the beginning, but all things are hidden because of the end. Therefore man does not find every work of God from the beginning unto the end; since, [0245A] even if he seems to see certain things, he does not penetrate the whole, and the whole becomes doubtful, because the whole is not made manifest; therefore, lest man quarrel against God in doubtful things, and murmur when set in adversities, by the gift of God many prosperous things are granted, and very many pleasant ones, that man may lawfully use goods acquired by just labor, and rejoice, and be glad, and soften his own conscience toward God. And let him think well of him, and not dispute nor contend against him, as though God were adverse who has bestowed good things. For this is what is meant by that which is subjoined, saying: *And I have known that there was nothing better than to be glad, and to do good things in one's life.* For he who does good to himself in his life out of those things which by the gift of God have been granted to men to enjoy, that he may be glad and rejoice in his God, does better than he who [0245B] disputes and contends against God. *For every man that eateth, and drinketh, and seeth good of his labor, this is the gift of God.* Therefore, because this is the gift of God, and lawfully granted by God, it is better than to dispute, and contend, and murmur: which is not the gift of God, nor granted to men by God; because it is evil and a great affliction. For he is much afflicted who can neither love nor approve [sic: *approhare*] what he endures; nor change what he hates. Therefore he is much afflicted who disputes and quarrels against God, blaspheming his judgments and detesting his works, since man can by no means [sic: *mullatenus*] change the things which God works.

Therefore that which follows, and he says: *I have learned that all the works which God hath made continue for ever.* [0245C] For just as the works of creation in their own genera do not pass beyond the order of the divine institution, so also the works of his judgments, by which he disposes the deeds of men and the whole world, do not confound the sentence of his predestination and providence fixed for ever. *For neither can we add anything to them,* that there should be more than has been provided; *nor take away,* that the things which have been made should be less, that God may be feared. For for this reason the disposition of visible things does not confound its order in the course of things passing away and in the vicissitude of things succeeding, so that man may know and understand that providence is eternal, which from the beginning disposed all things, by the established and constant examination of its own precept, in what manner their outcomes should follow [0245D] in their own times. And that he may also learn to fear in his own deeds him whose omnipotence he discerns in all his works, and in his judgments a constancy that remains unchangeable. For the established disposition of the work, and the sure moderation by which God brings all things to effect in their own times, manifestly demonstrates that, just as his wisdom is not deceived in discerning, so also his counsel is not changed in judging. *What is done, the same remaineth.* Just as in this whole body of created things what is done remains, because the order of the disposition is not confounded, even if mutable nature be varied, so also in his judgments what is done remains, because even in that which, according to his ineffable dispensation, he ordains dissimilarly for the variation of times, [0246A] he does not change the sentence of his own providence. Therefore what is done remains, both in the universe of things, so far as pertains to the order of the disposition, and in the exhibition of his judgment, so far as regards the sentence of predestination. The things which are to come have already been. For in both, the things which are to come have already been; because both in the genera of all things, what those things were which now are not, this same thing, according to the likeness of nature and the identity of the course, are the several things in their own kind to be, which are not yet. And in the divine judgments, the truth which we have heard was kept in things past, the same we expect to be exhibited in things that come after. For God everywhere restores what has gone away; because just as he repairs the failing of things that pass away by the multiplication of those that succeed in their own genera, [0246B] so, by judging daily the deeds of men, he demonstrates that he has held entire the ancient truth of his judgments, which he exercised from the beginning, although it seemed to have been interrupted unto the temple. For with him malice always has its punishment, and virtue its reward. But because of punishments one is hidden, another manifest: while God through patience tolerates the impious and sinners, and does not straightway inflict upon them a manifest punishment, human folly thinks that he either does not know or does not care about the deeds of men, or even, what is worse, that he approves the depravity of the malignant. Thence therefore the heart of man is shaken against God, whence God is shown to be the more to be loved and praised by men. Man complains that an evil man is tolerated by God, [0246C] when it is manifest that no man would have been good, if no evil man had ever been tolerated. And yet the hearts of the weak are scandalized, while they see in this world the impious prospering and the innocent oppressed; because they look only to those things which are without in the open, and do not attend to those which either lie hidden within, or are kept to be exhibited manifest in the future. From what cause their complaint arises is opened when it is added:

*I saw under the sun in the place of judgment impiety and in the place of justice iniquity.* He saw indeed that in this world God permits the impious to be judges, and to obtain power so that they lord it over and oppress the innocent, and that the deeds of men are iniquitous which ought to have been just, [0246D] and that in all these things there is great confusion under the sun. For because God grants power in this world to the perverse, therefore there is impiety in the place of judgment, and in the place of justice iniquity: so that there is impiety where judgment ought to be, and there iniquity where justice ought to be. For, because those set over are impious, therefore the subjects are iniquitous, because unless those oppressed innocence through impiety, these would not assail justice through iniquity. For inferiors are bold to perpetrate iniquity for this reason, that superiors are not dutiful to protect innocence; because if those did judgment for them that suffer wrong, these would not have been so ready to inflict wrong. But in this too under the sun there is impiety in the place of judgment, and in the place of justice iniquity: that every [0247A] man pursues without mercy the evils of his neighbor, but each one strives, even against justice, as far as he can, to defend his own evils. For in his neighbor's cause every judge is impious, and in his own cause against his neighbor iniquitous, because when his neighbor's evils are to be judged, he does not remember mercy; but when his own acts against his neighbor are to be examined, he does not keep justice. And in this manner all judgments under the sun are perverse, and the minds of men totter, and they wonder where is the judgment of God who endures these things.



## HOMILY XVIII. *On the perverse morals of men: and what Ecclesiastes judged concerning them.* (cont.)

We can still, with another and perhaps more convenient understanding, consider impiety under the sun in the place of judgment, and iniquity in the place of justice. For there is under the sun impiety in the place of judgment, and iniquity in the place of justice, [0247B] when in this world both the just man endures the punishment of the iniquitous, and the iniquitous receives the reward of the just. For the good are judged in this life only, that here they may receive the scourges of God for their own excesses, and may pass more purified to the reward to come. But the evil, because they are kept for the judgment to come, often in this world not only endure no adverse things, but are also permitted to fulfill their own desires, so that all things fall out for them according to their wish, unto the pleasantness and happiness of the present life. And recompenses seem to run dissimilarly, so that the good receive evils and the evil goods, and the judgment of the just seems to be impious, so that the innocent are oppressed and endure the punishment of the iniquitous, and so that the iniquitous take away the reward of the just: and all things confused and mingled, because God does not straightway exercise judgment so as [0247C] to rescue the innocent, and the just from the oppression of the impious, and to punish those who work iniquitous things. Thence arises a grievous shaking of minds, and the pusillanimous who see nothing but what is without alone are kindled with zeal, and think as though God did not care for human things, and had committed all things to fortune, and that nothing came to pass by judgment. And, what is more cruel, often men go into such perversity that God is blasphemed, and it is said as though he approved iniquity, and as though impiety were with him, and he did not know true judgment. And all this arises from this, that under the sun impiety is in the place of judgment, and in the place of justice iniquity, and that, as it were in the vanity which is under the sun, all things are confused, and all come forth dissimilarly. And yet, when man considers the works [0247D] of God, and sees in what manner he disposes all things with established and constant moderation, he understands that God is the inspector and moderator of all: and that his judgments cannot be made void, and that the deeds of men which he does not judge in this life he keeps to be examined hereafter. For if, as has been proved from the disposition of his works, God is a just judge, it remains that what is not done in the present in judging the deeds of men be without doubt believed to be brought to completion in the future. Therefore, in order to set forth the complaint of the weak in that God, in the fickleness and passing of this life, leaves all things as it were undiscussed, and disposes the deeds of men with a recompense unlike their merits, he said: I saw under the sun impiety in the place of judgment and in the place of justice iniquity. But straightway, [0248A] considering that the just judgments of God could not at all be made void, he understood that judgment was only deferred, not taken away, and he said:

*I said in my heart: The just and the impious shall the Lord judge, and then shall be the time of every thing under heaven.*

Then, when the Lord shall judge the just and the impious, there shall be the time of every thing under heaven: because everything which is done in life, whether it be good or evil, shall then be brought to judgment. And even now indeed in this life the Lord judges the just and the impious, but those recompenses are hidden, and those which shall be manifest are deferred unto the future. And therefore the small-hearted murmur, and complain, and think that the judgment is not just; because they do not see the recompenses. Yet even now just judgment is done: and each one [0248B] is judged according to his own merits. But all this is within, whither the eye of the flesh does not reach. And the things which are manifest are deferred until their judgment comes; and then all hidden things also shall be made manifest, and all things shall be judged according to just judgment. And then the Lord shall judge the just and the impious; and then shall be the time of every thing under heaven. But meanwhile all things are obscure, and run mingled toward their end. And the just are wrapped up in one lot with the impious, so long as they run together on the way, so that, when they shall have gone forth together, they may be distinguished and set in order unlike. For up to that point there is no distinction in all the things which appear without under the sun. But just as all things arise together, so they live together, and all things pass away together. And all this comes to pass, that the hearts of men may be proved: [0248C] whether there live in them anything of the knowledge of truth, and of the affection for the good which is hidden, if perchance out of that they may begin to take an argument of faith that they are something more than that alone which is seen. For outside there is nothing whence this can be known, and all arguments are taken away without, that what is within may begin to work, and be proved how great it is. For, if a man shall have known himself out of that, it is a great thing, and it stands to his merit that he seeks God through faith, whom he does not see through sight. For he first withdrew, and turned himself away when he was contemplating him present: and it was a great guilt and a grievous fault to have hated the light that was present; and it has been appointed for man as a remedy of propitiation, if he shall seek him who is absent and shall desire him who is hidden; [0248D] and that he may know that to be which is not seen, that it may be made manifest in its own time. Therefore now all things are withdrawn, and the things which are invisible are hidden, and man is left outside alone with things not his own, so that he may see nothing of his own, that it may be proved whether perchance any remembrance remains in him for turning to the seeking of him. Therefore now this man, considering more deeply the profundity of the judgments of God, sees that it is no wonder if in the cloud of this life no distinction is made between the just and the impious, since our mortality is wrapped in so great a darkness that in it man may even seem like to the beasts. Therefore he infers and says:

*I said in my heart concerning the sons of men, that God might prove them, and show them to be like the beasts. Therefore there is one destruction of man and of the beasts, and equally [0249A] the condition of both.* For man was made mortal for this reason, and therefore the sons of men die in like manner as beasts, that they may be proved and shown to be like the beasts. For two things had been made in man, one to the likeness of God, the other to the likeness of a beast. And that indeed which was created to the likeness of God was made immortal by nature, as God was immortal, to whose likeness it was made. But that which had been made to the likeness of a beast was corruptible by nature, as were those things to whose likeness it had been made. For both were taken from earth, and earth was the matter of both; and that earth was corruptible by nature, as were the things which were made of earth. So then two things were made in man, one earthly, the other heavenly; one by nature [0249B] corruptible, the other immortal; one the likeness of a beast, the other of God.
