And the body indeed was earthly, by nature corruptible, made to the likeness of a beast; but the soul was heavenly, by nature immortal, founded to the image of God. And body and soul were joined in man, two into one: and to the body was given the benefit of the fellowship, that it might share of the immortality of the soul unto incorruption: and all this was done for the glory of the soul, because it was pleasing to God, standing in justice and truth, so that its garment might not be worn away if its obedience persevered. And that which was the likeness of a beast in man was covered with the glory of incorruption, so that it might as it were be dissembled; nor might it see it unto the confusion of love, but might behold wholly the form of that which it loved, [0249C] and might not go far off anywhere. Afterward the soul turned itself away into the estrangement of love, and forgot that which was better, its own, and gazed without upon an alien beauty. And it bent itself upon the dye of its own cloak, that it might delight itself there; and it began to commit fornication with bodily enticements, and spiritual love was withdrawn from its eyes. And God was angry, and this turning away did not please him, and he willed to call man back within to that which was true, so that the gaze of the deceiver might not always cleave upon painted images. And he drew away the glory of its garment, that it might go into corruption, and commanded the soul to turn back, lest it should quicken it and bear it to eternity; and he cut down its garment, that he might show man that his glory was not in that wherein he should see himself like to the beasts. And man fell, and that slipped away [0249D] which was earthly and corruptible, and it began to flow so as to return whence it had come. And it came to pass that man was proved, whether he remembered his own good, and whether he knew how to seek it while he is compelled to leave the vain appearance, and to go out from that to which he had perversely clung.

*Therefore, that God might prove the sons of men, and show them to be like the beasts. Therefore there is one destruction of man and of the beasts, and the condition of both equal; as man dieth, so also they die.* In death therefore the condition of man and of the beast is one; because as man dies, so also they die. But nevertheless the condition of mortality is for man from judgment, for the beast from nature. And what they have alike they do not have from a like cause; because man had it from [0250A] fault that he should die, but the beast received it from nature that it should die. Nevertheless man is proved, and is shown to be like the beasts; because there is one destruction of man and of the beasts, and the condition of both alike: and so man dies as they die.

*And all things breathe alike.* That is, both the one and the other have a like life, and a like breath of quickening. And in all these things:

*Man hath nothing more than the beast.* Because common to both is both their rising, since they were alike made of earth; and their course, since together:

*All things are subject to vanity and to mutability.* And all things pass away, and the consummation of the end the same, they die similar, and they return to the earth of which they were first taken. So also formerly man had with the beasts one origin in the body, which was taken from earth, and one [0250B] condition of living with the beasts, so that the earthly body should likewise be nourished from the earth; but man did not have one end with the beasts, because man had been made that he should not die, nor return into the earth of which he was made. So then through the incorruption of the body his lowly origin was covered, and it was dissembled through immortality, lest it should come into reproach that man was like the beasts; nor did God will to show man whence his ignobility was, so as to expose him wholly in his own condition. But he clothed him with the beauty of immortality, and set him apart outside his own kind into another lot. But when man had sinned against God, he deprived him of his glory, and sent him back to his origin, so that by that whither he was going [0250C] he might know whence he had come. Therefore there is one destruction of man and of the beasts, and the condition of both equal; because as man dies, so also they die: and all things breathe alike, and in all these things man has nothing more than the beast, but all alike lie subject to vanity.

*And all things go to one place.* That is, to the earth their mother, and the beginning of their origin, because:



## HOMILY XVIII. *On the perverse morals of men: and what Ecclesiastes judged concerning them.* (cont.)

*They were made of earth, and into earth they alike return.* And if anyone should wish to say: that man has this more than the beast, that the spirit of the sons of Adam goes upward, so that, the body falling into death, it remains surviving in life; and that the spirit of the beasts descends downward, that is, together with the death of the body [0250D] flows away into corruption: who knows this? Yet not because it is not true, but because it is hidden: therefore:

*Who knoweth this?* For he did not say, This is false, but, Who knows this? No one knows this. For this cannot be known by man, and yet it can be believed. And it is true, because it is believed: and what is believed is true, and that belief itself is not doubtful but firm knowledge; and yet no man knows this in the manner in which those things are known which are seen, and heard, and touched, and perceived by the other senses; and in the manner in which those things are known concerning which one naturally cannot doubt, and which are demonstrated by indubitable reason even to unbelievers who doubt. For by faith alone is this perceived, and therefore there is great doubt for those who do not have faith concerning [0251A] this, because they do not know it, nor can it be demonstrated to them by those who lay hold of it by faith, because they themselves do not lay hold of faith. Therefore they themselves think that man has nothing at all more than the beast, because they do not know that the spirit of the sons of Adam goes upward, and the spirit of the beasts downward. And when it is said to them that man has more than the beast, because the spirit of man goes upward to life, and the spirit of the beasts downward to death, they say: Who knows this? For they do not think that anything can be known unless it be seen with the eye of the flesh, and touched by sense, and therefore they do not receive the knowledge of faith, by which alone man is called back to that in which he has more than the beast. And because they cannot see that, nor touch it in the open so as to comprehend what this is which man has more than the beast: [0251B] they despair utterly of perpetual life, and become unbelieving toward the things which are said, and cast themselves with their whole intent into the delights of the present life, as though this alone were their portion, and they were to receive nothing more afterward. For they do not know that for this reason it is hidden, that it may be believed that man has more than the beast, and that man himself may be proved by God in faith through his own ignorance. For if it were seen it would not be believed, but known; nor would there be merit, nor would man be proved, nor would the iniquitous be convicted, nor the good exercised. Therefore it is hidden that it may not be seen, that which man has more than the beast, so that faith may have merit, and unbelief its place. And there are many unbelievers, and they say: Who knows this? And they prove that things certain are not to be forsaken for things uncertain; and they begin to embrace present things, [0251C] and to snatch the things which are seen, that they may hold what is certain; and they are mocked in the uncertain, because these pass away, and slip out while they are thought to be held; and the things which are certain follow after, which were thought uncertain. All these things this man demonstrates, and he shapes his narrative this way and that, so as to follow the minds of men, since in this manner they themselves totter, and fluctuate in the uncertainty of a darkened life. For he told of the doubt of men concerning their own life, since they do not know whether man has more than the beast, and they do not find who has known whether after the death of the body the spirit of man remains surviving in life; now he takes up their own voice in approval of present delights, in accordance with this very doubt of theirs and despair of the life to come, saying:

*I have found out: that there is nothing better than that a man be glad [0251D] in his own work: and that this is his portion.* For those who do not believe that a life to come exists, these think that this alone is man's portion, that he be glad in his own work, and in this life enjoy his own labor; since those who do not reckon that a wage will follow after the work is completed, these judge that the profit of the work is to be taken not after the work, but in the work, and that he alone is happy who prepares the fruit of his own labor for use in the present, not he who reserves the wage of his own work to be taken hereafter.

*For who shall bring him, that he may know the things to come after him?* From the time when man has once gone out from this life, he is no more brought back, nor called back again into this life, that he may make trial and feel again the things which [0252A] are done in this world, so that he may anew enjoy its delights, and perceive the pleasantness of that which is to come after him. Therefore, as long as he lives, let him take as much as he can, and use this world before he departs, and is led forth from it; because he shall not return any more, nor be brought back, that he may know the things to come after him. And it can come to pass that another after him may abuse the things which he himself was unwilling to use, and a stranger rejoice and exult in his goods, and they can no more be brought back to his own use, once he has been taken away. For this reason, and for reasons of this kind, men think that this is their portion, that they should enjoy the pleasure of the world while they live; and for this alone they reckon that they were made, and that they will be nothing after these things, when this life shall have received its end. And they multiply reasonings and arguments, and heap up the things which are reasons to them, one [0252B] after another, that they may deceive themselves, and that their hearts may be confirmed in evil, and that they may believe what is false. And they build with lies a wall between themselves and truth, that they may not see it; and they set forth all things, and diligently search out all things by which they may plausibly demonstrate that there is no other life, and that this alone is good: and that man was made for this life only, and that there will be no other after it. And all this shaking rises up out of the darkness of the judgments of God; because he placed man in doubt that he might prove him, and that he might not see what man has more than the beast. And yet he himself again pursues that error with other judgments, and shows that this life is not good, in which very many evils reign; nor can true delight or tranquil rest be found in it. And therefore he sows over it [0252C] adverse things, and turns himself back, and goes far off, so that he does not free the oppressed, that they may be greatly afflicted, and that by grief and sadness the evil sweetness and the iniquitous delight may be wiped away. And therefore those who wish to be gladdened in this life, and make a covenant of love with the world, and iniquitously purpose not to seek the truth, are compelled by true judgments to see the evils which are under the sun, so that they may not please themselves overmuch in their turning away. Therefore this man, when he had said that it was good for man to be glad in his own work, and that this was his portion, and had given a definition for resting in these things, is moved by another consideration, that this life is not so, that rest can be in it. Therefore he adds and says:

[0252D] *I turned me to other things [n: (Eccles. IV)]*. He is rightly said to have turned to other things, because by those things which he is now about to see he is admonished to believe something other than what he had before reckoned. For all these things, which shall then be seen, may teach that the true good of man is elsewhere than in this life, which is subject to so great miseries and griefs, in which innocence is oppressed, and grief does not deserve consolation: this thing therefore other, and far other than that which before was seen and thought, was this man considering, and he said:



## HOMILY XIX. *On the oppression and abandonment of the innocent: and on the various and foolish judgment of the impious concerning this life.*

*I turned me to other things. and I saw the calumnies (Eccles, IV)*, *which are wrought under the sun, and the tears of the innocent, and [0253A] no comforter; nor that they could withstand their violence, being destitute of the help of all.* Therefore do not think that here is the fatherland. But consider and acknowledge that you are under the sun, where all things are rolled about and all are confused, since this was done for this reason, that you might acknowledge your exile, and seek another fatherland. Therefore calumnies happen here, and unjust oppressions, so that some assail others without cause, and the stronger oppress the weak without mercy; and God does not bring consolation here to the innocent, because he reserves his consolations for them elsewhere, and now meanwhile leaves them destitute of the help of all, lest they be consoled in another's help, which they do not have as their own, and less groan, and sigh, and desire his consolation, which they do not yet have. But the mind of the perverse, impatient of correction on either [0253B] side, is neither roused by prosperity, nor chastened by adversity. For when they look upon the sweet things of this life, they cleave to them in mind, and say: This is enough, and there is no other good of man besides this to be hereafter. But when they attend to adverse things, they are blunted, and fall down in mind, and fall down in distrust, and despair of themselves, because they have no other hope. And they choose rather not to be, than to be in evil; because it is evil, that which they are, and they have not known a good which they might choose, so as to transfer themselves to it; since in the time of their pleasure they were unwilling to learn it, that in the evil time they might rest in it. And therefore they do that which alone they have known: when they are in evil, they wish to be nothing, because they have not known another way by which to escape being in evil, unless they pass over [0253C] to not-being. Therefore, the evils which are under the sun having been seen, straightway in their voice it is subjoined, when it is said:

*And I praised the dead rather than the living; and I judged him happier than both, who is not yet born; nor hath seen the evils which are done under the sun.* This indeed is the voice of those who have not known the true good, nor think that there is anything for man unto good or unto evil, except what is present in its entirety. Therefore in good things they exult beyond measure, in evil things they despair; and where their hope is, there their despair stands. For this reason, the evils of this world having been seen, they say that the dead are happier than the living, and that than both, those not yet born are happier. For because they reckon that this alone is, which is seen, [0253D] the misery which is in it having been seen, they will not now seek happiness in another being, because they have not known a being other than this; but they set it in not-being alone; since in this whole which alone they think to be, they have found unhappiness. Therefore they praise rather those who have been, and are not, than those who still are, and they proclaim more blessed than both those who are not yet. For if, as it seems to them, it is evil to be at all, it is good not to be, and better not to have been. For if it is evil to be in evil, it is good not to be in evil, and better not to have been. For that which is further from evil in good, is itself the greater good. Just as that which is further from good in evil, is itself the greater evil. If therefore it is evil to be, it is assuredly good also not to be, and much better than both, never to have been. And so such [0254A] perversities do the minds of men bring forth of themselves under the sun, that this also is added to the confusion of all, that man himself grows dark as to his own living. For if man saw what man is and why man was made, he would assuredly recognize his own good, and would no longer say that those who are nothing are happier than those who are something. For the mind bound by the desire of so great a good, although, afflicted by temporal evils, it might loathe the present life, yet in the hope of the consolation to come it would altogether love to be rather than not to be. But now a great perversity grows up in the minds of men who are ignorant of their own good, and a vanity at its consummation, than which none can be greater. For man, flowing away from true essence by the vanity of his own mutability, without ceasing at all times ceases to be that [0254B] which he is; and passes always into that which is not; and so, in a manner accustomed to his own evil, at last casts himself headlong into such perversity that he now covets to be nothing at all. But many, desiring to avoid this madness as though set forth in the open, and such as cannot be hidden, turn themselves about, that they may guard their own being, and they apply great carefulness and industry of their labors, that they may make their life secure from the evils which are under the sun; and gathering wealth, and multiplying riches, and all the other things which can be for the solace of life, they seek out with much foresight and carefulness. And they do many things, and work very many things worthy of memory; and not seldom by their own industry and labor [0254C] they escape another's violence, but another's envy they cannot evade. Wherefore concerning these also, after the impatience of those who despair, he subjoins a sentiment, saying:

*Again I contemplated all the labors of men, and I observed that their industries lie open to the envy of a neighbor.* For when he considered the labors of men, he saw that the industries of the good lay open to the envy of neighbors; since the perverse also, just as they are bound by sloth so that they do not exercise in themselves the works of the virtues, so are they goaded by envy that they may carp at them in their neighbors

*And in this therefore is vanity, and superfluous care.* Either this is vanity, that a man, by envying another's good works, brings care upon his own mind, and the anguish of malice, [0254D] since by envying he harms not him whom he envies, but himself; or else this too is vanity, that a man labors too much for these temporal goods, and is anxious, and afflicts himself with superfluous care, since the acquiring of them sooner stirs a neighbor to envy than draws him to love. For while the industry by which a man is exercised is good, the superfluous care and solicitude by which he is afflicted is evil. Likewise also vain confidence, and the hope by which true security is expected in the multitude of riches; whereas the acquiring of them rather takes away security, when it inflames to envy of us those neighbors who in poverty might perhaps have been able to love us, by reason of their own rivalry. Rightly therefore is industry approved, and vain solicitude reproved. But there comes again another kind of men from [0255A] the flock of vanity, seeking an occasion for their own torpor. And these reprehend those who work, and seem as it were to reprove them as anxious, that they themselves may be dissolute. For thus vanity runs, that it never finds the middle boundary of virtue.

*And therefore the fool foldeth his hands together, and eateth his own flesh, saying: Better is a handful with rest, than both hands full with labor, and affliction of mind.* The fool folds his hands together. For he is a fool who does not know how to provide for himself hereafter. He is a fool who thinks that hands folded in idleness are filled with riches. He folds his hands together: he folds the one to the other, lest they be stretched out to work. He folds his hands together, as though making a covenant with idleness. And he eats his own flesh. He thinks he spares his own body, [0255B] because he does not afflict it with labors, but thence his flesh is consumed by want, whence it is nourished by idleness. He eats his own flesh. For he feeds with his own folly, and his [sic: *otiocitas*] fattens him. But nevertheless his flesh, as much as it is fed in profitable leisure, so much is it wasted by the hunger that follows. Therefore he eats his own flesh, saying: Better is a handful with rest, than both hands full with labor, and affliction of mind. It is true that a handful is better, if it knew not how to fail, and if the blessing of the widow of Sarepta had been given to all [n: (III Reg. XVII)]. But Elias was not sent to all. Why therefore do you foolishly say that a handful with rest is better than both hands full with labor? Perhaps you consider when you have a handful, and have rest; but you do not attend to when you shall not have even that [0255C] handful, what you will then do, and what rest you will then be going to have. Therefore now you eat your handful in rest, and your idleness pleases you; nor do you care meanwhile now to seek with labor another handful, which you may eat when this one has failed. Therefore you eat your own flesh now, because it will be beaten after this importunate idleness, and afterward by its own wasting will pay back what ill-advised rest has spent. And perhaps you may think that now all the kinds of vanities [0256A] have been told, and that there is no other kind of vanity which the watcher may find under the sun. Therefore there follows:

*Considering, I found another vanity under the sun. There is one, and he hath not a second, not a son, not a brother, and yet he ceaseth not to labor; neither are his eyes satisfied with riches, neither doth he reflect, saying: For whom do I labor, and defraud my soul of good things?* For he who is one, and alone has no other successor, neither a son whom he has begotten, nor a brother with whom he himself was begotten: for of his father he himself is the successor. He therefore who has not a second, whom he may leave after him as successor in his own goods: and yet does not cease to gather, nor enjoys the things which he possesses; but keeps avariciously what he has greedily gathered: what can be more vain than this? For he who labors and enjoys his own labor, [0256B] takes some fruit; and he who labors, and does not use the things which he acquires by his labor, and yet leaves them to be possessed by those whom he loves, takes some profit of his labor, the desire and the wish of his own love. But he who labors, and labors neither for himself, nor for another whom he loves: for what does he labor, unless for his own vice alone, which he serves? For this man serves his vice alone, and there is no other affection which might excuse his vain solicitude, to which pardon could be given, save that one alone which vice has begotten. Many are the labors of men, which are left to strangers, and those who do them take no fruit from them. And many also labor in wisdom, and give effort, and study to know many things, and to say very many, and commit their thoughts to writing, that they may be handed on to posterity; nor do they take fruit from [0256C] all these things, so that it should be better for them, but they remain empty of truth and void of the sweetness of wisdom. And all their labor is vanity, even if it seem to draw near to truth. For men of this kind do not know for whom they labor, and defraud their own soul of good things; because, while they intend only upon the greatness of the work, taking no fruit from it, they see also that no usefulness comes to them from it, and they do not know whether these very things will profit others after them.
